# CHRYSOGONUS
## POLYDORUS’S PREFACE
### to several small books regarding <term>alchemy</term> <note>original Greek: περὶ χυμείας (peri chymeias)</note> addressed to the Reader.

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial 'C' featuring a figure, possibly a scholar or saint, within a circular frame surrounded by foliage.</image-desc>

WHEN I wished to release from our library several selected treatises of the ancients regarding <term>alchemy</term> to the printers to be published, so that a greater benefit might flow from them to those eager for Philosophy, I judged that I ought to provide some small preface. For I do not doubt that many men—good men indeed,
<margin>5</margin>
but nevertheless unskilled in the nature of things—may believe that this entire branch of art should be utterly despised and covered in silence: either because they suppose it to be entirely false, or because they think it too difficult to be investigated with such great labors for an uncertain outcome. I myself, truly, although I was once of that same opinion, was enticed by a desire for learning and diligently examined this class of authors as the opportunity arose; and I found so much knowledge of things...

<summary>This page serves as the opening preface by Chrysogonus Polydorus (likely a pseudonym for Andreas Osiander) for a collection of ancient alchemical texts, defending the study of alchemy against skeptics who view it as fraudulent or overly difficult.</summary>
<keywords>alchemy, Conrad Gessner, philosophy, ancient texts, scientific defense, 16th-century printing</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page begins the preface of the work, following the title page. The catchword "re" suggests the next page begins with a word like "reliqua" or "rerum."</meta>

# P R E F A C E

*...debating, namely, what might be the primary and immediate matter of metals <note>materia metallorum: the fundamental substance from which metals are formed</note>, what is their efficient cause, how they differ among themselves—whether by some substantial form or truly by accidents alone—what affinities they have, whether one can be transformed into another, and how they ought to be brought to the highest perfection. All these things, and many others of this kind,*
<margin>5</margin> *while they either investigate them or assert with great constancy and authority that they have already discovered them long ago, they bring so much light and knowledge that I, for my part, would not unwillingly doubt whether either Aristotle or Theophrastus <note>Theophrastus (c. 371–287 BCE) was Aristotle's successor and wrote extensively on mineralogy</note> could have provided as much. Therefore, this so remarkable addition to Philosophy ought not to be neglected or despised by students, even if it must be begged for here and there from a "Barbarous" <note>The author refers to Medieval or non-Classical Latin writers as "Barbarous"</note> class of writers, and gathered, as it were, like gold from dung <note>original: "aurum è stercoribus" — a common alchemical metaphor for finding value in base or rejected materials</note>. There is also another cause not*

<summary>The author introduces the preface by arguing that alchemical investigations into the nature and transformation of metals provide insights that rival the classical philosophy of Aristotle and Theophrastus.</summary>
<keywords>matter of metals, efficient cause, transformation, Aristotle, Theophrastus, gold from dung, alchemical philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>The previous page likely introduced the antiquity of the chemical arts; this page continues the historical and mythological justification for alchemy, focusing on the Roman Emperor Diocletian and the myth of the Golden Fleece. Catchword: "confligentis" (clashing).</meta>

# TO THE READER.

<margin>Diocletian</margin>
...to compile the remains of <term>chemistry</term><note>original Greek: χυμείας (chymeias). At this time, "chemistry" and "alchemy" were used interchangeably to mean the art of transmuting metals.</note>. *That is, Chemistry is the making of gold and silver; Diocletian sought out and burned the books of this art because the Egyptians had attempted a revolution against him. He treated them harshly and as enemies. At that time, he also tracked down and burned the books written by the ancients concerning the chemistry of gold and silver, so that the Egyptians might no longer gain wealth from that art, nor, relying on an abundance of money, rebel against the Romans in the future.* These are his words. Nor do they differ from other historians describing Diocletian. Moreover, the ancient Poets suggested that this art not only existed, but also how it is to be practiced, most perfectly through delightful fables, yet <term>enigmatically</term><note>original Greek: αἰνιγματικῶς (ainigmatikōs); meaning in riddles or allegories.</note>. For what else is the *Golden Fleece* sought by the *Argonauts* from *Colchis*, if not the investigation and shadow-sketch of this art? Concerning which <term>Suidas</term><note>A 10th-century Byzantine Greek historical encyclopedia.</note> says elsewhere: *This is not as it is poetically reported, but it was a book written on skins, containing how gold ought to be made through chemistry. Justly, therefore, the men of that age named it the Golden Fleece, because of the power derived from it.* <note>original Greek: τοῦτο δὲ οὐχ’ ὡς ποιητικῶς φέρεται ἀλλὰ βιβλίον ἦν ἐν δέρμασι γεγραμμένον. περιέχον ὅπως δεῖ γίνεσθαι διὰ χυμείας χρυσός. εἰκότως οὖν. οἱ τότε χρυσοῦν ὠνόμαζον αὐτὸ δέρας, διὰ τὴν ἐνέργειαν, τὴν ἐξ αὐτοῦ.</note> The Golden Fleece is not given to *Jason* unless he first undergoes certain and dangerous labors; that is, unless he first learns the <term>practice</term><note>original Greek: πρᾶξιν (praxin); the hands-on laboratory work as opposed to mere theory.</note> by working with his own hands. For he possesses a book written about this art in vain who is ignorant of the practice. Furthermore, the dangerous voyage indicates the difficulty of searching out the matter of the *Philosophers' Stone* (as they call it). That matter, because it holds the place of the earth and is metallic, is called the *Field of Mars* (the god of metals); and because it must be purged, prepared, and perfected in fire so that it is fit to receive the seed, it is feigned to be plowed and cultivated by *Bulls* breathing fire. These *Bulls* are the furnaces, which, unless they are rightly constructed and provide a continuous fire in the due amount, either the matter is ruined or the vessels are destroyed, and the craftsman is struck by a noxious odor or even perishes in the fire. Next, the ever-watchful *Dragon* is <term>Quicksilver</term><note>Mercury.</note>. The *Dragon's Teeth* are <term>Sublimated Mercury</term><note>Mercury that has been heated into a vapor and solidified again; here described as corrosive and toxic.</note>, because it possesses the whiteness of ivory, the hardness of bone, and an incredible "biting" quality, not without poison. While it is being sublimated, it is received in a glass vessel very similar to a helmet (the *Arabs* call it an <term>alembic</term><note>A classic alchemical still used for distillation.</note>); and so the *Dragon's teeth* are brought to *Jason* in a *Helmet*, so that he may scatter them in the place of seed into the Field of Mars. For *Mercury* must be sublimated from that earth so many times until it is fixed in it. While this happens, it rises out of the earth in the manner of little spears, of which some are erect, some inclined, some entirely transverse, and as if aimed at the throats of enemies; they grow continuously in length so that they seem to move, appearing like a most dense army even now most fiercely clashing...

<summary>The author interprets the myth of Jason and the Argonauts as an alchemical allegory, equating the Golden Fleece with a book of transmutation and the Dragon's teeth with sublimated mercury. He also cites the Roman Emperor Diocletian's destruction of Egyptian alchemical texts to prove the art's historical reality and power.</summary>
<keywords>alchemy, Diocletian, Golden Fleece, Jason, Argonauts, Philosophers' Stone, Mercury, sublimation, alembic, Field of Mars</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>The catchword "enigmatibus" from the previous page suggests a discussion on the allegorical riddles of alchemy, which continues here with the interpretation of the myth of Jason and the Golden Fleece.</meta>

# P R E F A C E

you will present them in conflict. For this reason, armed men are imagined to be born from the earth, who fall by mutual wounds—that is, they are finally fixed and buried in that earth. And if the small vessel were made of glass, or of flint without pores, the craftsman would be endangered by being struck by the odor. Therefore, Jason is imagined in the works of Ovid to have turned the flint of danger away from himself and toward his enemies. Thus far the <term>Philosophers' Stone</term> <note>The legendary alchemical substance capable of turning base metals into gold and serving as an elixir of life.</note> is perfected; it remains to lull the ever-watchful dragon to sleep—that is, to convert <term>Quicksilver</term> <note>Mercury; in this context, the volatile metallic principle that must be "fixed" or stabilized.</note> into gold or silver through its efficacy. When this succeeds, the victorious Jason carries away the Golden Fleece—that is, the Craftsman has attained this most precious art. But before this is done, they sacrifice to Hecate <note>The goddess of magic and the underworld; here representing a specific stage of chemical transformation or purification.</note> in the manner described by Orpheus, in which sacrifice a great mystery lies hidden. Furthermore, because this same <term>Philosophers' Stone</term> is also able to heal human bodies, and keep them healthy and unharmed even...
<margin>5</margin>

<summary>This page provides an alchemical interpretation of the myth of Jason and the Argonauts, equating the sowing of dragon's teeth and the Golden Fleece with the chemical stabilization of mercury and the perfection of the Philosophers' Stone.</summary>
<keywords>Alchemy, Jason and the Argonauts, Philosophers' Stone, Quicksilver, Ovid, Hecate, transmutation, Golden Fleece</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the philological investigation into the etymology and spelling of the word "Alchemy" and its Greek roots.</meta>

The first letter is pi. The second is epsilon. The third is iota. The fourth is rho. The fifth is alpha. The sixth is nu. <note>The author is spelling out "Peiran," likely referring to an experiment or trial.</note>

*Wait, the Greek in line 12:* nothing healthy <note>original: "ουδεν υγιες" - a common Greek idiom for something unsound or corrupt.</note>.

*Wait, the Greek in line 21:* CHEMEIA <note>original: "ΧΗΜεια" - the Greek root for Chemistry/Alchemy.</note>.

*Wait, the Greek in line 23:* from the word "cheo" <note>original: "απο του χεω" - meaning to pour or melt.</note>.

*Wait, the Greek in line 24:* I pour, things poured <note>original: "χω χυματα" - referring to the casting of metals.</note>.

*Wait, the Greek in line 24:* from the word "scheo" <note>original: "απο του σχεω" - meaning to hold or check.</note>.

*Wait, the Greek in line 24:* I hold, forms <note>original: "σχω, σχηματα" - suggesting the word relates to the outward appearance or shape of things.</note>.

*Wait, the Greek in line 26:* Greatest <note>original: "Μεγιστην" - likely referring to the "Great Work" or a superlative quality of the art.</note>.

*Wait, the Greek in line 29:* by catachresis <note>original: "καταχρησις" - a grammatical term for the misapplication of a word or an strained metaphor.</note>

<summary>This page analyzes the linguistic origins of the word "Alchemy," debating whether it derives from Greek terms for "pouring" metals or from terms related to "form" and "shape."</summary>

# I N D E X.

XII. Diverse opinions of those who suppose that the Art <note>The "Art" refers to Alchemy or the Great Work of transmutation.</note> exists. 34

XIII. Reasons of those denying the Art is based in sulfur. 35

XIIII. Refutation of the preceding reasons. 36

XV. Reasons of those denying the Art is based in Arsenic, and their refutation. 37

XVI. Reasons of those denying the Art is based in Sulfur, Quicksilver <note>original: "Argento uiuo"</note>, Tutia <note>A medicinal zinc oxide crust found in furnaces.</note>, Magnesia, Marcasite, and Sal Ammoniac, and their refutation. ibid.

XVII. Reasons of those denying the Art is based in spirits <note>Volatile substances that vaporize when heated.</note>, together with the fixing of bodies, and their refutation. 38

XVIII. Reasons of those denying the Art is based in bodies, and first in white lead—that is, tin, or Jupiter—and their refutation. 39

XIX. Reasons of those denying the Art is based in black lead <note>Lead (Plumbum nigrum).</note>. 40

XX. Reasons of those denying the Art is based in the mixing of hard bodies with hard, and soft with soft bodies. 41

XXI. Reasons of those denying the Art is based in the mixing of hard bodies with soft, and perfect with imperfect. 42

XXII. Reasons of those denying the Art is based in the extraction of the soul, or the governance of fire. ibid.

XXIII. Reasons of those denying the Art is based in glass and gems, and their refutation. 43

XXIIII. Reasons of those denying the Art is based in middle minerals, or vegetables, or the mixture of any things whatsoever. ibid.

XXV. On the natural principles of metallic bodies, according to the opinion of the ancients. 44

XXVI. On the natural principles of metals, according to the opinion of the moderns and the author. 45

XXVII. Division of the discussion concerning the three principles: namely Sulfur, Arsenic, and Quicksilver. 47

XXVIII. On Sulfur. ibid.

XXIX. On Arsenic. 49

XXX. On Quicksilver, or Mercury. ibid.

XXXI. On the effects of the principles of nature, which are the metallic bodies. 50

XXXIIII. On Saturn, or Lead. ibid.

XXXV. On Jupiter, or Tin. 53

# I N D E X.

*In the Fourth part, concerning artificial principles, the following are contained.*

| | | |
|:---|:---|:---|
| **XXXVIII.** | Division of the topics to be discussed in this part, with an introduction to the perfection to be taught in the second book. | 56 |

# THE BOOK OF GEBER THE ARAB,
## THE MOST SKILLFUL PHILOSOPHER
## AND MOST EXPERT IN NATURAL MATTERS (ESPECIALLY
## METALLIC ONES) ON CHEMISTRY <note>original: "peri chemeias"</note>, WHICH HE ENTITLED
## *On the Investigation of Perfection*. Translator unknown.

### THE PREFACE, IN WHICH HE EXPLAINS
### why he wrote this book. Chapter I.

<image-desc>A large, square woodcut initial 'I' featuring intricate floral and foliate patterns in a classical style.</image-desc>

**I** shall hand down to you the investigation of this most noble science <note>The "noble science" refers to alchemy, the art of transmuting base metals into gold and seeking the perfection of matter.</note>, derived from continuous and frequent diligence in the work, and from excessive study comparable to this, as well as from our deepest thoughts and various related matters. I do this so that the following volumes may be better and more clearly understood by you, and so that once understood, and having been devised and examined regarding the same, they may be more easily and promptly brought to effect.

->commentary<-

<summary>This page serves as the title and opening preface for a treatise on alchemy attributed to Geber, emphasizing that the knowledge provided is the result of intense study and practical labor.</summary>
<keywords>Geber, alchemy, investigation of perfection, metallic philosophy, chemical science</keywords>

<page-num>2</page-num>
<header>ON INVESTIGATION</header>

to reveal by the commentary, which we have composed <gloss>regarding it</gloss> for the purpose of explaining it. Therefore, by right, this book precedes that one, since through this book I seek to investigate the knowledge of the perfecting thing <note>The "perfecting thing" refers to the Elixir or the process that brings base metals to the state of gold.</note>.

### On the things which perfect and corrupt metallic bodies. Chapter II.

<margin>compare</margin>
Since this science treats the imperfect bodies of minerals, insofar as it is fitting to perfect them, we have considered two things regarding them: namely, Imperfection and Perfection. Upon these two we found our intention. We compose this book concerning the things that perfect and corrupt, according to what we have investigated through our own experience, because opposites placed next to each other shine forth more clearly. Now, the thing which perfects in minerals is the substance of quicksilver <note>original: "argenti uiui"; mercury</note> and sulfur mixed together in proportion, thickened and fixed by a long and temperate decoction <term>decoction: a process of heating or "cooking" a substance in a fluid to extract its essence</term> in the bowels of the clean earth, with the preservation of its radical moisture—which is not corrupting, but rather leads to the substance

<summary>The author explains that this book serves as a necessary precursor to his other works by defining the nature of perfection and imperfection in metals, specifically identifying the balanced combination of mercury and sulfur as the source of metallic perfection.</summary>
<keywords>mercury, sulfur, perfection, imperfection, decoction, radical moisture, alchemy, mineralogy</keywords>

--- Page 25 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "Summa Perfectionis" (The Height of Perfection) attributed to Geber, focusing on the chemical preparation of metals and the nature of the Philosopher's Stone.</meta>

<page-num>33</page-num>
->ON THE PERFECTION OF GEBER<-

<margin>That is: a greater perfection in some, such as in copper and iron; a lesser imperfection in others, such as in tin and lead.</margin>
...of each one's perfection. Truly, the second [consideration] concerns these four, namely tin, lead, copper, and iron, according to the imperfection of each. And because these imperfect bodies <note>In alchemy, "bodies" refers to the base metals</note> cannot be restored to health and perfection unless the contrary is worked within them—that is, that which is manifest <note>visible/physical</note> is hidden, and that which is hidden is made manifest—and since this operation or "contrariety" is achieved through preparation, preparation must therefore be applied to them. <margin>Preparation [see pages] 4, 7, 16</margin> To prepare, therefore, is to take away the superfluous, to supply what is absent, and thus to impart to them the mark of perfection. Perfect bodies <note>Gold and silver</note> do not require this [type of] preparation. However, they do require a preparation by which their parts are further refined <note>original: "subtilientur"</note> and reduced from their corporality to a fixed spirituality. The intention of this is to make from them a fixed spiritual body—that is, to thin and refine them much more than they were before. Regarding the preparations of all these things, according to our investigation, we shall treat them sufficiently in their proper place in this book. After they have been sufficiently prepared, they will be fit for the Great White or Red Elixir <note>The "White Elixir" transmutes metals into silver; the "Red Elixir" into gold</note> to be made from them.

### *Concerning the Stone of the Philosophers: that it is only one, for both the White and the Red; from what things it is extracted; and concerning the possibility and method of perfection.*

### Chapter III.

<margin>From whatever [thing]—Note Well: For there are various things from which quicksilver is extracted, as also follows here.</margin>
We have found, however, that <gloss>modern</gloss> <gloss>philosophers</gloss> <gloss>unless it be</gloss> <insert>moderns to us</insert> write of only one single Stone, complete for the White or the Red, which we also concede to be true. For from whatever thing the White or Red Elixir is composed, there is nothing else in it but quicksilver <note>mercury</note> and sulfur, one of which does nothing without the other, nor can it exist. And therefore it is called "one stone" by the philosophers, although it may be extracted from many bodies or things. For to extract it from a thing in which it does not exist would be a foolish and vain thought, as certain fools think—

<meta>catchword: think-</meta>

<summary>This page describes the alchemical process of "preparation," which involves refining base metals (tin, lead, copper, iron) by balancing their internal qualities. It also introduces the theory that the Philosopher's Stone is a single substance derived from the union of mercury and sulfur.</summary>
<keywords>Geber, alchemy, transmutation, preparation, mercury, sulfur, Philosopher's Stone, Elixir, base metals, refinement</keywords>

--- Page 26 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the investigation of metallic transmutation, likely following a section defining the nature of metals. The catchword "in pa-" suggests the next page begins with a word like "in patella" (in a pan).</meta>

<page-num>44</page-num>
->ON INVESTIGATION.<-

they thought, which was never the intention of the philosophers.
<margin>the philosophers</margin> » However, they say many things through metaphor <note>original: "per similitudinem"</note>. And because all metallic bodies are composed of quicksilver <note>mercury</note> and sulfur—whether pure or impure, by accident, and not innate in their primary nature—it is possible to remove these impurities through suitable preparation. For the stripping away of accidental qualities is not impossible.
» Preparation, therefore, is the removal of the superfluous and the supplying of what is lacking in imperfect bodies, which cannot be done without the assistance of labor and purifying substances. Furthermore, preparation varies according to the diversity of the things in need. For experience has given us the methods of acting, namely: calcination <note>reduction to powder by heat</note>, sublimation <note>vaporizing a solid</note>, descension <note>liquefying a substance downward</note>, solution, distillation, coagulation, fixation <note>making a volatile substance resist fire</note>, and ceration <note>softening a substance to a wax-like state</note>. We have given a sufficient account of each of these in the *Sum of the Perfection of the Mastery* <note>referring to the "Summa perfectionis magisterii," a foundational medieval alchemical text attributed to Geber</note>. For these are the operations that assist in preparation.
<margin>pages</margin>

### Concerning the things that assist preparation, and their cleansing. Chapter 4.

» **T**he things that assist in preparations are these: namely, all kinds of salts, alums, vitriols <note>original: "atramentorum," referring to sulfate minerals</note>, also glass, borax, and those things of this nature, as well as very sharp vinegar and fire. And with these we propose to prepare the imperfect bodies; but it is proper to cleanse them first, if any impurity exists in them, according to our experience, in which we have become certain through the aforementioned things.

### The cleansing of common salt.

» For common salt is cleansed in this manner: First let it be burned <note>calcined</note>, <gloss>then</gloss> once burned let it be dissolved in common warm water; once dissolved let it be filtered <note>original: "per filtrum distilletur," referring to capillary filtration</note>, and once filtered, [let it be evaporated] over a slow fire

It introduces the primary operations of the "Mastery" and begins a practical guide on purifying reagents, starting with common salt.</summary>

# THE PERFECTION OF GEBER.
<gloss>The glaze</gloss>
Let it be congealed in a glazed dish; once congealed, let it be calcined <note>burned to a powder</note> for a day and a night in a moderate fire, and keep it thus sufficiently cleansed.

## Cleansing of alkali salt.
<margin>Fat</margin>
Alkali salt <note>likely potassium carbonate or sodium carbonate</note> is cleansed in the same way as common salt, and it is the <term>fat</term> <note>original: "sagimen"; here referring to the "scum" or essential extract</note> of glass. First let it be ground, and the whole dissolved in warm common water; afterwards let it be distilled through a filter, congealed, and calcined with a slow fire.

## Cleansing of salt gem.
<note>original: "salis gemmae"; rock salt or pure halite</note>
First let it be ground, then let it be treated as was done with common salt.

## Cleansing of salt ammoniac.
<note>original: "salis ammoniaci"; ammonium chloride</note>
Let it first be ground with the preparation of cleansed common salt. Afterwards let it be sublimed <note>heated until it turns to vapor and solidifies again</note> in a tall <term>aludel</term> <note>a pear-shaped subliming pot used in alchemy</note>, until it has been totally extracted in a pure state. Afterwards let it be dissolved upon a <term>porphyry</term> <note>original: "porfidum"; a hard stone slab used for grinding</note>, in the open air, <margin>the stone p. 25</margin> if you wish to make water <note>a liquid solution</note> from it, or let it be kept in its sublimed and sufficiently pure state.

## Cleansing of other salts.
Various other types of salts are still to be found, which are prepared and cleansed in the manner already described.

## Cleansing of alums.
First, concerning <term>glacial alum</term> <note>original: "alumine glaciali"; likely clear potassium alum</note>. Many things can be prepared by means of it without any cleansing of its own. However, it is cleansed in this manner: Let it be placed in an <term>alembic</term> <note>a distilling apparatus</note>, and let all the moisture be extracted from it, which is of great value in this art. The dregs remaining in the bottom of the vessel may either be dissolved upon a stone in some damp place, or in the water extracted from it, or they may be set aside.

## Cleansing of Jamen alum.
<margin><insert>of the prepared stone</insert></margin>
Jamen alum <note>original: "alumen Iameni"; a variety of alum traditionally sourced from Yemen</note> is prepared just like glacial alum, except that in this art it is of greater power.

### The cleaning of other alums.

Many more alums are yet to be found, all of which are prepared and cleaned in the manner already described. <note>Alums were a class of astringent mineral salts essential for dyeing and chemical reactions.</note>

<margin>The first flower of copper-green <note>original: "χαλκάνθου floris α". This refers to the highest grade of vitriol or copper sulfate.</note></margin>
### The cleaning of inks.

First, concerning black ink <note>term: atramentum (a general term for black pigments, vitriols, or inks)</note>. Black ink is cleaned in this way: First, let it be dissolved in pure vinegar, afterwards distilled and coagulated. Or, let it first be placed in an alembic <note>a teardrop-shaped glass vessel used for distillation</note> and the total moisture extracted. The dregs from this distillation, having been calcined <note>reduced to powder by heat</note> upon a porphyry stone, should be dissolved in their own water and coagulated. Or, that water may be reserved according to the will of the craftsman.

### The cleaning of copperas.

Copperas <note>term: cuperosa (ferrous sulfate or green vitriol)</note>, or vitriol, is cleaned just like black ink. However, black ink possesses a greater earthiness than green ink.

### The cleaning of other inks.

There are diverse inks, and they are found in various colors; these are cleaned as described above.

### Concerning glass and boraxes.

<margin>antimony >></margin>
Glass and boraxes, if they have been made in the proper manner, do not require preparation.

### The cleaning of very sharp vinegar.

Vinegar, and every kind of sharp or sour liquid, is refined and purified, and their virtue or effectiveness is improved through distillation. We have treated their cleaning and purification sufficiently, by which means imperfect bodies <note>a reference to "base" metals like lead or tin</note> can be prepared, purified, improved, and refined, always by the mediation of the proper fire.

### On the preparation and improvement of <insert>mineral</insert> bodies in general. Chapter V. <insert>A noteworthy chapter.</insert>

<margin>Mineral or metallic bodies, pages 8, 20, 21.</margin>
**T**hey are prepared and purified for the purpose of the fire <note>meaning: to withstand the heat of the furnace</note> in this manner. For these imperfect bodies possess superfluous humidities and a combustible sulfurousness which generates blackness within them and corrupts them. They also possess an unclean, drossy, and combustible earthiness that is far too coarse, which hinders their penetration and fusion. <gloss>such as Iron</gloss> <note>original: "ut Martis" (of Mars). In alchemy, Mars is the symbol for Iron.</note> These and similar things are the superfluities in the aforementioned bodies, which have been discovered in them through our experience and certain, ingenious investigation. <margin>Because</margin> And because these superfluities come upon these bodies accidentally <note>meaning: they are external impurities, not part of the metal's true essence</note> and not radically, and since the stripping away of accidental traits is possible, it is necessary for us—once these aforementioned things are cleansed with artificial fire—to remove all accidental superfluities, leaving only the substance of <term>mercury</term> <note>original: "argenti vivi" (living silver)</note> and radical sulfur remaining. <margin>The complete preparation.</margin> And this is the complete preparation of the imperfect [metals] and their perfect purification. The improvement, purification, and refinement of the pure substance that remains is done in many ways, according to what the <term>elixir</term> <note>The "philosopher's stone" or agent of transmutation</note> requires. Therefore, the method of preparation and purification in general is this: First, all the superfluous and corrupt humidity in their essence must be raised up <note>evaporated</note> with a proportional fire, as well as the subtle and burning superfluity; and this is done by <term>calcining</term> <note>heating a substance to high temperatures in air to reduce it to a powder or "calx"</note>. Then, the total remaining corrupt substance in their "calx" <note>powdered residue</note> of burning humidity and blackness must be eroded away with these <gloss>lead</gloss> aforementioned cleansed corrosives, whether sharp or bitter, until the calx is white or red, or colored according to the nature and property of the body <margin>of tin, of lead</margin> <gloss>such as Copper and Iron</gloss> <note>original: "ut Veneris et Martis" (of Venus and Mars)</note>, and is clean and pure from all the previously mentioned superfluities or corruption. And these are cleansed with these corrosives by grinding, soaking, and washing. Afterward, however, the total earthiness must be deleted or set aside...

Ensure its proper fusion <note>melting</note>, stirring the liquefied body at that time with a perforated iron spatula. Draw off the dross <note>scoria</note> that forms upon it, and stir the body again in a steady heat of the fire until a good quantity of powder has gathered upon its surface. <margin>Is it not extracted?</margin> Let that powder then be set aside, and stir the metal again, until by alternating these steps the entire body has been reduced to a very fine powder. Then, let that powder be sifted and placed back into the furnace, applying a strong heat that does not exceed its melting point, and stirring it many times. Let it remain in the fire of its calcination <note>the process of reducing a metal to an oxide through heat</note> for a natural day <note>24 hours</note>, or thereabouts, until all accidental moisture and superfluity have been destroyed, <margin>together with its combustible sulfur</margin> along with the combustible and corrupting sulfur. Afterward, let the cleansed white lime <note>calx; the powdered oxide</note> be extracted. For the fire elevates and consumes every fugitive and inflammable substance from the aforementioned materials. Specifically, by washing it many times with cleansed common salt, alum, and with purified and very sharp vinegar; then place it in the sun or the open air, and grind it again, wash it, and dry it. Let this be repeated in turns until, through the sharpness of the salts, alums, and vinegar, all its moisture, blackness, and impurity have been consumed, corroded, and destroyed. <margin>ground glass</margin> Then, let ground glass be added along with these aforementioned things. And when the whole has been made into a paste, let it be brought down through a <term>Botum barbatum</term> <note>original: "Botum barbatum"; a specialized alchemical vessel used for filtration or "descensory" distillation, often shaped with a "beard" or downward spout</note> with sufficient fire. For the pure and clean body descends, while the entire earthy and dross-filled substance remains with the glass, salts, or alums. In that clean body which has descended and been recovered, there is an equal and perfect proportion of clean <term>quicksilver</term> <note>original: "argenti uiui"; mercury, considered a primary building block of metals</note> and of <term>white sulfur</term> <note>original: "fulphuris albi"; a non-combustible alchemical principle</note> that does not burn. For the fire and the corrosives have separated all the moisture and the fugitive, inflammable, and corrupting substance, as well as the blackness; and through its <term>descensory</term> <note>original: "defcenforium"; a furnace or vessel designed to let molten purified material flow downward</note>, or through the paste of salts, alums, and glass, the entire [earthy] substance has been divided.

**S**aturn <note>The alchemical name for Lead</note> is also prepared thus: Let it be placed similarly in the calcining furnace, stirring it as you did with Jupiter, until it is converted into a most fine powder. Let it be calcined again with pure sal ammoniac, as <gloss>r</gloss> with Jupiter, ground most subtly, and dissolved by the same method.

## On the preparation of Venus,
### Chapter VIII.

**V**enus <note>term: Venus (Copper)</note> is also best prepared by this method: Let a layer of well-cleaned common salt be placed in a crucible, and upon it a plate of the metal, and above that a layer of salt, and above that a plate, and so on in turns until the vessel is full. Let it be covered, sealed with clay <note>original: "lutetur," referring to 'lute,' a paste used to make vessels airtight</note>, and placed in the calcination furnace for a natural day <note>a 24-hour period</note>. Let it be taken down and whatever has been calcined <note>term: calcination (the process of heating a substance to high temperatures to change its physical or chemical state, often to a powder)</note> be scraped off, and let the plates be replaced with new salt. And so let it be calcined by turns until all the plates have been consumed or corroded through the benefit of the salt and the fire. For the salt corrodes the superfluous moisture and the combustible sulfurousness; the fire elevates the fugitive and inflammable substance in due proportion. Let that powder be ground very finely and washed with vinegar until the water flowing from it is free of blackness. From here, soak and grind it by turns with new salt and vinegar, and after the grinding, let it be returned to the calcination furnace in an open vessel, and let it stay there for three natural days. Then let it be extracted and ground well and finely, and washed with vinegar well and for a long time, until it has been cleaned and purged of all impurity. Let it be dried well in the sun, then let half as much sal ammoniac <note>term: sal ammoniac (ammonium chloride, used as a flux and to clean metals)</note> be added to it, grinding it well and for a very long time until the substance is impalpable <note>too fine to be felt as distinct grains</note>. Let it be placed in the open air or in the heat of decomposing dung <note>original: "resolutionis fimo," a common alchemical method for providing gentle, steady heat</note> until whatever is subtle there has been dissolved; renewing the cleaned sal ammoniac if necessary, until the whole becomes water. Honor this water, which water

## On the preparation of Mars.
### Chapter IX.

**M**ars <note>original: "Mars"; refers to iron</note> is better prepared by this method: Let it be calcined <note>term: calcination; the process of heating a substance to high temperatures in air to change its physical or chemical state, often reducing it to a powder</note>, just as Venus was, with cleansed common salt, and let it be washed with pure vinegar. Once washed, let it be dried in the Sun: when dried, let it be ground again with new salt and vinegar and saturated, and let it be placed in the same furnace for three days, as was said concerning Venus. Honor that which is dissolved—namely, the water of fixed sulfur—which marvelously increases the color of the Elixir. And let these things said concerning the preparation of imperfect bodies suffice.

## On the preparation of Sol.
### Chapter X.

<margin>+ 21 Sal ammoniac <note>original: "Sal ammon."; ammonium chloride</note> at half the weight of the perfect body of gold or silver.</margin>
**P**erfect bodies <note>In alchemy, "perfect bodies" refers to gold and silver, which do not corrode</note> do not truly require preparation in terms of their perfection, since they are already perfect; however, so that they may be further refined and thinned, the following preparation should be applied to them: Take Sol <note>original: "Sol"; refers to gold</note> beaten into thin plates, and place it with very well-prepared common salt in layers within a calcining vessel, and set it in the furnace, calcining it well for three days, or until the whole has been finely calcined. Then let it be taken out, ground very well, washed with vinegar, and dried in the Sun. Afterward, let it be ground well with half its weight of cleansed sal ammoniac, and set to dissolve, until the whole has been dissolved into a most clear water by the benefit of the common salt and the sal ammoniac. This is the ferment for the red elixir, a precious and true spiritual body.

## On the preparation of Silver, Chapter 11.

<margin>cc</margin>
<margin>.</margin>
**S**ilver <note>original: "argentum"</note> is refined and thinned, and reduced to a spiritual state <note>meaning a volatile or purified essence</note> by the method already described. Therefore, in every single step of its refinement, act exactly as you did with Gold <note>original: "Sole," literally "the Sun"</note>. And the water of dissolved Silver <note>original: "Lunae," literally "the Moon"</note> is the ferment for the white spiritual elixir.

## On the properties of the Great Elixir.
## Chapter 12.

<margin>cc</margin>
<margin>cc</margin>
<margin>..</margin>
<margin>but the words with them seem to be deleted.</margin>
<margin>..</margin>

**W**e have now sufficiently determined the preparation of the imperfect metals and the refinement of the perfect ones, so that any discerning person may fulfill their intention regarding them. Let one therefore attend to the properties and the modes of action for the composition of the Great Elixir. For we seek to make one single substance, yet one gathered and united from many, and fixed <note>stable under heat</note>, so that when placed over a fire, the fire does not drive it out. Let it be mixed with melted metals and liquefy with them, by means of that "ingressive" substance <note>a substance capable of penetrating the metal</note> within it; and let it be mixed by its "miscible" substance, and consolidated by its "consolidative" substance, and fixed by its "fixative" substance. Let it not be burned by those things which do not burn gold and silver, and let it bring about consolidation and weight with proper and perfect ignition. However, do not understand "short time" to mean that it can be completed in a few days or hours on the first attempt. Rather, in comparison to other modern physicians <note>here meaning alchemists or practitioners</note>, and even in respect to the operations of nature, the truth is reached more quickly in this way. Whence the philosopher said:

> Medicine is that which has anticipated a long space of time.

Therefore I tell you to endure patiently, because perhaps it lingers; and indeed, haste is of the devil.

<margin>of the sun or the moon <note>original: "solis aut lune"; referring to the creation of gold or silver</note></margin>
it is. Therefore, he who does not possess patience should stay his hand from the work, because a hasty belief will only hinder him. For every natural action has its own measure and determined time, in which it is completed in a greater or lesser space. For these things, three are necessary: patience, delay, and the proper preparation of instruments. Regarding these, we have addressed the craftsman in the *Summary of our Perfect Mastery* <note>referring to the "Summa Perfectionis," a foundational alchemical text attributed to Geber</note>, in various chapters, in which he may test himself if he has been sufficiently practiced in our sayings. In these chapters, we conclude with manifest and open proof that our Stone is nothing other than a "fetid spirit" and "living water" (which we have also named "dry water"), purified through natural proportion and united by such a union that they can in no way be separated from one another. To these a third must be added to shorten the work: that is, a "perfect body" reduced to a fine state, <insert>like a leaven</insert> <note>original: "fermenti"; the addition of a "ferment" or starter was believed to catalyze the transformation of base metals</note>.

and through experimenting with the persistence of ingenious labor, he shall arrive at the totality of the work. Therefore, let the craftsman exercise himself, and he will find the method of this investigation (his heart being moved by love) through our consideration, which also gives a full knowledge of the truth regarding the matter and form of that which perfects and that which corrupts. For we have considered, <margin>of metallic bodies</margin> <insert>in</insert> our investigation, that the matter of perfect bodies <note>The "perfect bodies" refer to Gold and Silver.</note> along with their form, from the root of their mixing even unto their completion, is pure, without any supervening corruption. We have also considered, from the opposite sense, that the substance of the imperfect and the perfect is everywhere one and the same: namely, quicksilver <note>original: "argentum uiuum"; Mercury.</note> and sulfur: which, before their mixing, are pure and clean. And through this consideration and through our exercise, we have found that the corruption of the imperfect bodies has arrived accidentally, which gave a new and corrupt form to the matter. For when we saw the imperfect bodies prepared through our experience and our genius, and cleansed from all superfluous corruption and fleeting impurity, or delivered from earthiness: we found them to be of greater clarity and brightness, or purity, than the naturally perfect bodies themselves. Through which consideration we have arrived at the perfect and complete end of this science, which we have written perfectly and completely in our books. Study, therefore, in them, and you will find the whole of our science, which we have abbreviated from the books of the ancients. <margin>He does not boast of himself as the first inventor or writer, but candidly confesses that he has briefly compiled these things from the books of the ancients.</margin>

### of the perfection of Geber.

## THE FIRST BOOK OF THE SUM OF PERFECTION BY GEBER.

### PREFACE, CONCERNING THE METHOD OF TEACHING THIS art, and concerning suitable disciples. Chapter I.

<margin>q 18 July 1698</margin>

<image-desc>A large decorative woodcut initial 'T' depicting two figures, likely scholars or alchemists, engaged in conversation within a landscape setting.</image-desc>

**T**he whole of our science *concerning chemistry* <note>original: "περὶ χημείας"</note>, which we have abbreviated in our volumes from various compilations of the books of the ancients, we have here reduced into one summary. And that which was left incomplete in other books written by us, we have sufficiently compensated for in this tradition of our present book, and we have supplied their defect with brief speech. And that which was hidden by us in one part, we have made manifest in that same part within this our volume, so that the completion of such an excellent and noble part of philosophy may be revealed to wise men. Know therefore, dearest son, that in this work the entire operation of this art is sufficiently contained in general chapters and universal discussion, without any omission. By God, whoever shall have worked according to this book will rejoice to have found for himself the true end of this art.

Even if one has learned the natural principles and all the causes of minerals, they have not yet attained the true end and profit of this most occult <note>hidden or secret</note> art. However, such a person has an easier access to the principles of this art than one whose ignorance falls upon their very intention regarding the method of our work; the former is but a little distance from the entrance of the art. But he who knows the principles of all <gloss>things</gloss>, the causes of minerals, and the mode of generation which consists of the intention of nature, is indeed missing very little of the completion of the work. Nevertheless, without this final knowledge, our science cannot be perfected, because art cannot imitate nature in all its works, but imitates it as far as it rightly can. Therefore, dearest son, we reveal a secret to you: craftsmen err in this, that they desire to imitate nature in every single variation of the properties of its action. Labor, therefore, to study our volumes, and strive to revolve them very often in your mind, so that you may acquire the true intention from our manner of speaking; for in them you will find that upon which you ought to ground your mind, and you will learn from them how to repel errors from yourself, and in what ways you can imitate nature in the craftsmanship of your work.

## FIRST BOOK CONCERNING THE IMPEDI-
### ments by which craftsmen are hindered from attaining the true end of this art.

<margin>chapter 4.</margin>
**T**herefore, the impediments occurring in this work are generally two: namely, natural inability, and the lack of necessary funds, or occupations and labors. However, we say that natural inability is manifold. Specifically, on the part <gloss>chapter 4</gloss> of the craftsman’s organ <note>original: "organi"; here meaning the physical body or the sensory instruments of the person</note>, and on the part <gloss>chapter 5</gloss> of the soul. Furthermore, on the part of the craftsman’s organ, it is manifold: either because the organ is weak, or entirely corrupted. Finally, from the inabilities of the soul it is manifold: either because the soul is perverse in its organs, possessing no rectitude or reason in itself on account of the organs—such as the soul of an insane person or a fool—or because it is "fantastic" <note>original: "fantastica"; referring to an overactive or unstable imagination</note>, unduly and easily susceptible to contrary forms, suddenly stretching from one piece of knowledge to its opposite, and likewise from one desire to its opposite.

### On the obstacles to this work, regarding the body of the craftsman. Chapter 4.

**W**e have already defined for you the obstacles to this work in a general sense. Now, however, we shall address you in this chapter with more specific speech, and we will describe to you more openly all those <gloss>1</gloss> obstacles most fully and in order. We say, therefore: that if anyone does not have his physical organs complete, he will not be able to reach the completion of this work by himself—for example, if he were blind or missing his limbs—since he is not aided by the members through which this art is perfected, acting as servants to nature <gloss>2</gloss>. Furthermore, if the body of the craftsman is weak and sickly, such as the bodies of those suffering from fever, or lepers whose limbs are falling away, or those struggling in the final stages of life, or those of a decrepit <margin>cc</margin> old age, he will not reach the completion of the art. By these natural disabilities of the body, therefore, the craftsman is hindered in his intention.

### On the obstacles regarding the soul of the craftsman. Chapter 5.

<margin>cc</margin> **W**e have set before you one chapter in which we described, in absolute and manifest speech, the obstacles depending on the body of the craftsman. <margin>cc</margin> It remains for us to describe briefly the obstacles on the part of the soul <note>original: "animae"; in this context, it refers to the intellect, spirit, and psychological disposition</note> itself, which greatly hinder the completion of this work. <margin>cc</margin> We say, therefore, that he who does not possess a natural genius, and a soul subtly investigating natural principles, the foundations of nature, and the crafts <margin>by which</margin> they might follow nature in the properties of its action, will not find the true root of this most precious science. Such are many who are stiff-necked <note>meaning stubborn or intellectually inflexible</note>,

in every investigation, they possess a mind that is empty of talent; these are men who can scarcely understand common speech, and who similarly learn common works with great difficulty. Besides these, we also find many who have a soul that easily adopts any sort of imaginary fantasy <note>original: "fantasiam"</note>, but what they believe they have found to be true is entirely fantastic, deviating from reason, full of error, and remote from natural principles. This is because their brain, being filled with many smoky vapors <note>original: "fumositatibus"; in medieval physiology, vapors from the stomach were thought to cloud the mind</note>, cannot receive the true intention of natural things. There are also others besides these who have a restless soul, shifting from opinion to opinion and from one desire to another; just as they now believe this and want the same thing without any foundation of reason, so a little while later they believe and want something else entirely. These are so unstable that they can hardly complete even the smallest part of what they intend, but instead leave it diminished and unfinished. There are likewise others who cannot see any truth in natural things any more than beasts can, such as the mentally impaired, the insane, and children. There are still others who despise knowledge and do not think it exists; this science similarly despises them and repels them from the goal of this most precious work. And there are those who are slaves, serving money, who affirm this wonderful science themselves <margin>to incur expenses</margin> but fear to put forth the necessary costs. Therefore, although they affirm it and investigate it according to reason, they never reach the experience of the work because of their greed for money. To these people, therefore, our science does not come. For how <margin>Exclamation.</margin> could one who is ignorant, or who has neglected to investigate knowledge, easily reach it?

[Those who are] ignorant, they wastefully destroy their entire treasure; and when they have come close to the end of it, they have nothing further with which to work. # THE SECOND PART OF THIS
## FIRST BOOK, IN WHICH ARE
### reviewed the arguments of those who deny this art,
### and then indeed they are refuted.

## A general division of the things to be discussed. Chapter VIII.

**S**ince in the summary of this little book of ours we have already set before you all the obstacles to this work, and have given you sufficient instruction for adhering to this art, it is now expedient, according to the intention of our proposal, to dispute against the sophists <note>In this context, "sophists" refers to those who use clever but fallacious arguments to undermine alchemical truths.</note> and the ignorant. First, we shall set forth their own arguments, according to what we promised from the beginning that we would determine. Finally, we shall indeed destroy them all, so that by manifest demonstration it may be clear to the wise that those arguments contain nothing of the truth.

## Various arguments of those who simply
## deny the art. Chapter IX.

**T**here are, moreover, various people who deny and seek to destroy it. For some do so simply <note>original: "simpliciter"; meaning they deny the art's existence entirely without qualification.</note>, while others do so based on the statements made by those who suppose it to exist. For there are some asserting that the art does not exist at all, corroborating their opinion sophistically in such a manner; for they say: The species and diversities of things are distinct because the proportions of the elements to one another in their mixture are diverse and distinct. For an ass is different from a man in species because it had a much different proportion of elements in its composition; and so it is to be brought forward in the other diversities of things, and therefore also in minerals. Therefore, if the proportion of the things to be mixed—by which the form and the perfection of the thing is obtained—is unknown, how can the mixture and the thing to be mixed be 
<meta>catchword: formed</meta>

<summary>This page introduces the second part of the treatise, outlining a plan to present and then refute the arguments of those who deny the validity of alchemy, specifically focusing on the argument that unknown elemental proportions make the creation of species impossible.</summary>
<keywords>refutation of critics, elemental proportions, species, mineral composition, sophistry, alchemy vs. ignorance</keywords>

# THE SUM OF PERFECTION BY GEBER

we might know how to form them: But we are ignorant of the true proportion of the elements of the Sun <note>original: "Solis"; refers to Gold</note> and the Moon <note>original: "Lunae"; refers to Silver</note>, therefore we must also be ignorant of how to form them. From these points, therefore, they conclude: This art is useless and impossible. Similarly, they argue in another way, attempting to destroy our mastery <term>magisterium: the successful completion of the alchemical work</term>. For they say: Even if you knew the proportion of the elements, you would still be ignorant of the method of mixing them together, since nature procreates these things in caverns, mines, and hidden places; therefore, since you are ignorant of the method of their mixing, you are likewise ignorant of how to make them.

They argue again in a similar fashion: Even if you knew this properly, you would still be ignorant of how to balance the acting heat in the process of mixing, by means of which the thing is perfected. For nature has a certain quantity of heat by which it brings metals into being, the measure of which you do not know. Likewise, you are ignorant of other differences in the active causes of nature, without which nature could not truly perfect its intent: therefore, since these are unknown, the entire method of operating in this art will likewise be unknown.

Furthermore, they bring both reason and experience against you. For so long, they say, has this science been sought after by wise men, that if it were possible to reach it by any path, they would have completed it a thousand times over by now. Similarly, since philosophers appear to have handed it down in their volumes, yet we do not find the truth in them, it is sufficiently manifest and probable through this that this science does not exist. Likewise, many Princes and Kings of this world, having infinite treasure and an abundance of philosophers, have desired to discover this art, yet they could not reach the fruit of this most precious art. This is surely a sufficient argument to prove that the art is frivolous. Similarly, even in the weak mixtures of species, we are not able to follow nature. For we do not know how to fashion a donkey <note>The author argues that if man cannot create a "simple" living creature, he cannot create complex metals</note> and other similar things, whose mixtures are weak and manifest

<column-break/>

<summary>Geber outlines the primary arguments used by critics to dismiss alchemy, including the ignorance of elemental proportions, the secrecy of nature's subterranean processes, and the historical failure of kings and philosophers to achieve the Work.</summary>

as if by the senses. For this reason, we shall be even more ignorant of how to fashion the mixture of metals <note>original: "metallorum mixtionem"</note> (which is the strongest of all), as it is entirely hidden from our senses and experiences. A sign of this is the difficulty of releasing the elements from the metals themselves. Similarly, we do not see an ox transformed into a goat, nor any other species transmuted into another, or reduced by any other artifice. How then, since metals differ in species, do you strive to transform them into one another according to their species, so that from such a species you might make another? This seems absurd enough to us, and far removed from the truth derived from natural principles. Similarly, nature perfects metals over thousands of years; how then can you, in the artifice of transmutation, endure for thousands of years?

<margin>To these things</margin>
since you are scarcely able to reach the limit of a hundred years? If, however, it is answered in this way: "That which nature cannot perfect in a vast space of time, we fulfill in a short time through our artifice; for artifice completes the defect of nature in many things." We say again, that this is impossible, especially in metals, since they are composed of most subtle vapors <note>original: "fumi subtilissimi"</note> which require a tempered decoction <term>decoction: a process of heating or boiling to extract essence or achieve maturation</term>, so that their own moisture may be thickened within themselves according to a balance, and not flee from them, leaving them deprived of all moisture—the very thing by which they sustain hammering and extension <note>The author refers to the malleability and ductility of metals.</note>.

<margin>,,</margin>
If, therefore, through your artifice you wish to shorten the time of nature's decoction in minerals and metallic bodies, it will be necessary to do this through an excess of heat; but this heat will not balance the moisture, but rather, by dissolving it, will dissipate and destroy it from their bodies.

<margin>,,</margin>
For only a tempered heat thickens moisture and perfects a mixture, not an excessive fire. Similarly, being and perfection are granted by the stars, as if by the primary perfectors and movers of the nature of generation and corruption <note>original: "generatio et corruptio" - the Aristotelian cycle of coming into being and passing away</note> toward existence and non-existence.

<summary>The author argues against alchemy by claiming that human artifice cannot replicate the thousands of years nature requires to mature metals, nor can it transform one species into another without destroying the metal's essential moisture.</summary>

the existence of species. This, however, happens suddenly and in an instant, when one or more stars, through their movements, reach a determined position
<margin>cc</margin>

<margin>cc</margin>

We cannot fully note the diversity and distinction of these from one another, since they are unknown and infinite to us. How then will you supply the defect in your work arising from your ignorance of the diversities of the positions of the stars

<margin>cc</margin>

<margin>cc</margin>

<margin>cc</margin>

<margin>cc</margin>

Similarly, in natural things, this is the order: that it is easier to destroy them than to build them. But we can scarcely destroy gold; how then do we presume to construct it? Because of these sophisticated arguments, and others less apparent than these, they believe they can destroy <margin>overthrow</margin> this divine art. All these are the persuasions of sophists <note>those who use clever but false arguments</note> who simply deny our art. Truly, I shall set down the reasons of those who deny the art based on supposition, along with the destruction of those arguments, in what follows. From here, however, we must pass to the refutation of those arguments just stated, first setting forth our true intention for the completion of the work.

# SECOND PART OF THE FIRST BOOK

**W**e say, therefore, that the principles upon which nature founds its action are of the hardest and strongest composition: and these are sulfur and quicksilver <note>original: "argentum uiuum"; mercury</note>, as certain philosophers say. Therefore, because they are of the hardest and strongest composition, they are also very difficult to break down <note>original: "resolutionis"; the chemical process of dissolving or reducing a substance to its primary components</note>. However, so that their thickening and hardening may occur in such a way that they can withstand striking and stretching by the force of a hammer without shattering, it is necessary that the viscous moisture <note>the "glue" of the metal</note> in their mutual mixture be preserved through successive cooking <note>original: "decoctionem"; a slow heating process</note> within the ore. 

But we give you a general rule, dearest son: that no thickening of any moisture occurs unless there is first an exhalation of the most subtle parts of that moisture, and a preservation of the thicker parts, provided that the moisture in the mixture overcomes the dry, and there is a true mixing of the dry and the moist: so that the moisture may be tempered by—

<page-num>29</page-num>
->THE SUM OF PERFECTION BY GEBER<-
<note>Geber is the Latinized name of Jabir ibn Hayyan, the most influential figure in medieval alchemy.</note>
<sig>d iij</sig>
<meta>catchword: turn.</meta>

so that their total mixtures are divided into parts, but their entire substance ascends, or very little of their components is dissolved. Therefore, the successive, long-lasting, and equal resolution of the subtle, smoky moisture is the cause of the thickening <note>original: "inspissationis," referring to the hardening or solidification of metallic vapors into solid metal</note> of metals. We also cannot achieve this thickening through this specific method. Therefore, in this regard, we are not able to follow nature. For we cannot imitate nature in every variation of the properties of its actions. Our intention, therefore, is not to follow nature in its initial principles, nor in the proportion of the miscible elements, nor in the manner of their mixing with one another, nor in the balancing of the thickening heat. Since all these things are impossible and utterly unknown. It remains, therefore, to destroy the arguments of the sophists <note>In this context, "sophists" refers to those who use clever but fallacious arguments to deny the validity of alchemy</note> who deny this most excellent science through their ignorance.

### *Refutation of the arguments of those who simply deny the Art. Chapter XI.*

**I**f they say, therefore, that we are ignorant of the proportion of the elements, the manner of their mixing with one another, the balancing of the heat that thickens the metals, and many other causes and accidents following the natural actions: we certainly grant this to them. But they do not destroy our divine science because of this, for we neither wish to know those things, nor can we, nor can they be applied to our work. Instead, we assume another principle for this task, and another method for the generation of metals, in which we are able to follow nature. If they say that even the philosophers and the Princes of this world have desired this science and have not found it: we respond that they lie. For it is certain that some Princes (though few) and especially the ancients and the wise men found in our own time, have already investigated this science through their own industry; but they did not wish to hand it down to such people, neither by word of mouth nor in writings.

<summary>Geber argues that while alchemists cannot perfectly replicate nature's hidden proportions and timescales, they possess a distinct "divine science" and method that achieves the same ends, refuting critics who claim the art is impossible.</summary>

they have wished for it, though they are unworthy of it. Therefore, when they have not seen anyone possessing this <term>knowledge<note>original: "scientia"; referring here to the Alchemical art</note></term>, error has fallen upon their minds, leading them to judge that no one has discovered it. Furthermore, if they argue based on a delusion, asserting our inability to imitate nature even in weak <term>mixtures<note>original: "mixtionibus"; the combination of elements in a body</note></term>, such as in the mixture of a donkey or an ox, and therefore not in strong ones either: we reveal to them their manifold error. For in their mode of arguing, there is no logical necessity by which we are forced to concede that our art does not exist. They strengthen their delusion and error by arguing from a similarity, or from the greater to the lesser, in which there is no necessity, but only contingency, as is most often the case. We shall also show by another way, demonstrating that they assign no apparent similarity between a weak mixture—the composition of animals—and the firm and strong composition of minerals. And this is because in animals and other living things, in which the composition is weak, it is not only the proportion that completes them, nor the ingredients of that proportion, nor the qualities of those ingredients, nor the mixing that follows from their mutual action and passion (which arises from the gathering of those primary qualities); rather, it is the **soul**, according to the opinion of many, which comes from the secrets of nature, such as from the <term>fifth essence<note>original: "essentia quinta"; the quintessence or ether, the celestial element beyond the four earthly ones</note></term> or from the <term>prime mover<note>original: "primo motore"; the Aristotelian concept of the first cause of all motion in the universe</note></term>. We say this also according to the opinion of many, and we are ignorant of the hidden nature of this soul. Therefore, although there is a weak mixture in these things, we do not know how to complete them, because we are ignorant of how to infuse that perfecting principle, which is the soul. Through this, it is certainly clear that it is not a defect on the part of the mixing that we do not complete an ox or a goat, but from a defect in the infusion of the soul. For we would know how to make a weak or even weaker composition, as well as a strong or stronger one, by following our <term>craftsmanship<note>original: "artificium nostrum"</note></term> and imitating the natural path and course. In metals, therefore, the perfection is less than in those living things, and their

Perfection is concerned more with proportion and composition than with anything else. Thus, God the most high and glorious diversified perfections from one another in many forms. the agent of the philosophers <note>The "agent" refers to the active force or "alchemical ferment" that drives transformation.</note> completes its work in thousands of years; but because we are not able to follow those initial principles, it does not matter whether nature completes this in thousands of years, or more, or fewer, or even in a moment—their persuasion does not hold. <margin>»</margin> which we do not know: We say that it is not necessary for us to know this position, 
<margin>»</margin> because there is no species of things capable of being generated or corrupted 
<margin>»</margin> wherein the generation and corruption of some of its individuals does not occur 
<margin>»</margin> every day. By this it is certainly clear that the position of the stars is every day 
<margin>»</margin> perfecting and corrupting individuals of every species. 
It is therefore not necessary to wait for a specific position of the stars, though it would be useful; rather, it is sufficient only to prepare the materials for nature, so that nature herself, who is wise, may arrange them with the fitting positions of the moving bodies <note>The "moving bodies" refer to the planets and celestial spheres.</note>. 
<margin>»</margin> For nature cannot complete her own movement without the movement and 
position of these moving things. Whence, if you have prepared the artifice of nature and considered whatever belongs to the requirements of this mastery <term>Mastery: The "Magisterium" or the completed alchemical process</term> in a due manner, it will be perfected under the proper position by nature, which is suited to it, without your specific consideration of that position. For when we see a worm brought into being from a dog or another decayable animal, we do not immediately consider the position of the stars, but rather the dispositions of the surrounding air and other causes of perfecting putrefaction <note>The theory of spontaneous generation, where life was thought to arise from decaying matter under specific environmental conditions.</note>.

### *The diverse opinions of those who suppose the art to exist. Chapter XII.*

<margin>Those who work in spirits and bodies</margin>
**H**aving therefore assumed this art to exist, we find that there are many who hold different intentions. Some indeed work in spirits <note>original: "spiritibus." Others work in bodies <note>original: "corporibus," referring to the solid metals</note>. Others work in salts and alums, nitres and boraxes. Still others work in all things.

<margin>Note</margin>It does not require a hidden speech; nor one that is entirely manifest. And for the most part, we find the sect of each side to be void of reason.

### Reasons of those denying the art based on sulfur. Chapter 14.

**S**ome, therefore, supposing the Art to be found in sulfur, spent their labor on sulfur, and being ignorant of the perfection of its preparation, they left the preparation itself diminished. For they believed that cleansing and purification alone would be the perfection of the preparation; and since this is done through sublimation <note>term: Sublimatio — the process of heating a solid until it turns into vapor and then solidifies again, used to purify substances</note>, it was brought to their intention to think that sublimation alone in sulfur is the perfection of its preparation, and they introduced the same thinking regarding its companion, namely arsenic. Coming therefore to the projection <note>The final stage of alchemy where the medicine is cast upon a base metal to transform it</note>, which is

regarding the intention of alteration <note>The goal of transforming base metals into gold or silver</note>, they saw that the substance was burned away and vanished, making no lasting stay within the bodies of the metals. Instead, the bodies were left by them more impure than they had been before that projection <note>The act of casting the alchemical medicine onto a molten metal</note> was made. Therefore, because they saw this delusion at the completion of their work—and for a very long time it had been revolved in their minds that this science was to be found in sulfur alone—and since they did not find it therein, they argue that it cannot be found in anything else. Thus, since it is found neither in this nor in any other thing, they argue it is found nowhere at all.

### ->The Refutation of the Preceding Arguments. Chapter 14.<-

**W**e respond to them briefly, therefore, and say that they understand little in this matter and possess very little wisdom, because they suppose that sulfur alone is our matter. And even if this supposition were true, they are nonetheless deceived in the method of preparation, because they believe that sublimation <note>The process of heating a substance until it vaporizes and then solidifies again</note> alone is sufficient. They are like a child who, from the beginning of his birth until old age, is shut up in a house, not thinking the breadth of the world extends beyond the width of his own house, or beyond what he can see with his eye within that house. For these men have not applied their labor to many stones; therefore, they could not perceive from what our medicine may be drawn and from what it may not, especially since they have excused their hands from an abundance of labors. It is only right, then, that they should be ignorant of what completes the work and what does not. But as to why their work was diminished, we say it is because they left the "burning quality" <note>combustibility</note> and the "flight" <note>volatility</note> within the sulfur, which not only fail to perfect the work, but actually scatter and destroy it.

Others, however, believing that this stone is necessarily found in the same <term>Sulphur</term> and its companion <term>Arsenic</term> <note>Arsenic was often considered a "second sulphur" in medieval alchemy due to its volatility and yellow/red colors</note>, and looking more deeply toward the completion of the work, did not only cleanse the burning sulphurous quality through <term>sublimation</term> <note>The process of heating a substance until it vaporizes and then solidifies again, used for purification</note>, but also attempted to remove the earthiness, while leaving the volatility within it. Certain others looked more deeply into the substance and cleansed it, removing both the volatility and the burning quality; it became for them fixed and earthy, giving no good fusion in the heat of the fire, but only a glass-like fusion. Therefore, they were unable to mix it into the metallic bodies during projection. Consequently, they argue like the first group, and we respond to them as we did to the first: they left the work diminished and did not know how to complete it. For they were ignorant of how to seek <term>Ingression</term> <note>The ability of the alchemical agent to penetrate and permeate a solid metal</note>, which is the final perfection. In all other <term>spirits</term> <note>Volatile substances like mercury, sulfur, and ammonia that "fly" from the fire</note> the method of preparation is similar, except that in the case of <term>Mercury</term> and <term>Tutty</term> <note>A powder of zinc oxide collected from the flues of smelting furnaces</note> we are excused from the greater labor of removing the burning quality, for these do not possess a combustible and flammable sulphurous nature, but only volatility. they contain more <term>quicksilver</term> <note>original: "argentum uiuum"; mercury</note> and <term>sal ammoniac</term> <note>a volatile ammonium salt used as a flux</note>, but truly less <term>sulfur</term>, and even less of its companion; fourthly, even less <term>marcasite</term> <note>iron pyrite or similar metallic sulfides</note>; fifthly, <term>magnesia</term> <note>likely a manganese ore or a similar mineral, not modern magnesium</note> less than that; and sixthly and lastly, least of all <term>Tutia</term> <note>zinc oxide collected from furnace flues</note>.
