### *The arguments of those denying the art based on the spirits, together with the bodies to be fixed, and their refutation. Chapter XVII.*

**T**here are also others striving to fix <term>spirits</term> <note>volatile substances</note> into <term>bodies</term> <note>solid metals</note> with no other preparation preceding this, but a distressing delusion has similarly brought them sorrow and despair, and they were forced by it to believe that this science does not exist, and to argue against it. For this is the cause of their confusion and unbelief: that in the <term>fusion</term> <note>melting</note> of the <term>bodies</term>, those <term>spirits</term> abandon them and do not adhere to them; rather, they flee the harshness of the fire, while only the <term>bodies</term> remain in it, because they cannot endure the pressure of the fire's cruelty on account of their flight, which has not been taken away from them. Similarly, a delusion sometimes happens because the <term>bodies</term> also flee the fire along with them, and this occurs when the unfixed <term>spirits</term> have not adhered inseparably to the <term>bodies</term> in their depths, since the quantity of the volatile overcomes the quantity of the fixed. Whence they argue similarly to the first group, and we respond to them just as we did to the first. Therefore, this is their whole rejection: "If you wish to convert <term>bodies</term>, O sons of doctrine, then if it be possible for this to be done through any material, it must be through..."

<summary>This chapter addresses the failures of alchemists who attempt to bond volatile 'spirits' to solid 'bodies' without proper preparation, explaining that the spirits flee the heat of the furnace unless they are first 'fixed.'</summary>
<keywords>alchemy, spirits, bodies, fixation, sulfur, mercury, volatilization, Geber, chemical theory</keywords>

--- Page 61 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical and practical defense of alchemy from Geber's "Summa Perfectionis," specifically addressing the arguments of those who believe the art is impossible based on failed experiments with metals.</meta>

<page-num>39</page-num>
->THE SUM OF PERFECTION BY GEBER<-

it is certainly necessary for these spirits to be created: but it is not possible for them to adhere usefully to bodies if they are not fixed; rather, they flee and leave those bodies impure. Furthermore, it is not possible for them to enter while fixed, since they have become like earth, which does not melt. And when they appear fixed while enclosed in bodies, they are not truly so; either they depart from the bodies while the bodies remain, or both flee together. Therefore, since it is in no way possible to find this art in matter that is more closely related <note>referring to "spirits" like mercury or sulfur</note>, it will not be found in more remote matter; therefore, it is found nowhere. Our response is certainly this: that they do not know everything that is knowable concerning this matter, and therefore they do not find the whole work resulting from it. Consequently, they place the strength of their argument on a flaw arising from their own insufficiency.

---

### The arguments of those denying the art based on the underlying bodies—firstly in white lead, that is tin, or Jupiter—and the refutation of them. Chapter XVIII.

<margin>And from this they judged the art of transmutation to be impossible.</margin>
However, certain people have placed the art within the bodies themselves. Yet when they came to the work, they were deceived. For instance, they thought that both types of Lead—namely the livid <note>Lead/Saturn</note> and the white <note>Tin/Jupiter</note>—being of an impure whiteness, were very similar and close to the nature of the Sun <note>Gold</note> and the Moon <note>Silver</note>. They thought the livid was very close to the Sun, but little to the Moon; while the white was very close to the Moon, but little to the Sun. Therefore, some of these men, imagining that tin (or Jupiter) was very similar to the Moon—differing only in its "creaking" <note>The "cry of tin," a crackling sound made when bending the metal</note>, its softness, and its speed of melting—believed it was easy to melt and similarly soft because of an excess of its humidity. They believed its parts possessed that creaking sound because of the fugitive substance of quicksilver <term>quicksilver: mercury</term> falling within it. They exposed it to fire, and by calcining <term>calcining: reducing a metal to a powder or oxide through heat</term> it, they kept it in the fire as long as it could endure, until it was made white in its ash. When they later wished to reduce <note>to return the powder back to a metallic state</note> this ash, they were unable to do so, and thus they judged the task to be impossible.

<meta>catchword: And of these</meta>

<summary>Geber refutes critics who claim alchemy is impossible, arguing their failure to transmute tin (Jupiter) into silver (the Moon) stems from their own ignorance of the metal's properties rather than a flaw in the art itself.</summary>
<keywords>Geber, Summa Perfectionis, alchemy, tin, lead, mercury, calcination, transmutation, Jupiter, Sun, Moon, silver, gold</keywords>

# SECOND PART OF THE FIRST BOOK

And some of these practitioners have reduced something from that material, and they found in it a screeching sound <note>original: "stridorem"; likely referring to the "tin-cry" or a similar sound made by certain metals when bent</note>, as before, as well as a softness and a quickness of melting. And therefore, they believed this to be impossible by this method, and they were led into disbelief, such that they thought the art of hardening that substance could not be discovered. Some of these men, however, calcined <note>The process of heating a substance to high temperatures in air to change its physical or chemical state</note> and reduced it, and again—by drawing away its dross <note>original: "scoriam"; the stony waste matter separated from metals during smelting</note> with the application of a greater fire—they calcined and reduced it. And so, by frequently repeating the work upon it, they saw it already hardened and without the screeching sound. Yet, because they did not entirely remove the speed of its melting, their minds erred, and they judged that the goal could not be reached. And so, others among these men, wishing <margin>•</margin> to provide the same substance with hardness and a delay in melting through the addition of hard bodies <note>Refers to alloying the substance with harder metals or minerals</note>, fell into a delusion; for whatever hard bodies were mixed into it were shattered by it.

<page-num>41</page-num>
<header>THE SUM OF PERFECTION BY GEBER. 41</header>

It returns more quickly from its lime <note>original: "calce"; refers to the powdery oxide formed when a metal is heated in air</note> than Jupiter <note>The alchemical name for Tin</note>, but they are unable to remove its lividity <note>The dull, bluish-gray color characteristic of lead</note> because they are ignorant. Therefore, through that method, they cannot whiten it with a true whitening, nor could they, through their own imagination, associate it so stably with the stable bodies <note>The "stable" or "fixed" metals, like Gold and Silver, which resist fire</note> that it would not happen to recede from the mixture through the strong expression of fire. And this indeed deceives them greatly; in the preparation of this substance, they suppose that knowledge can be found in nothing except in it, because after two reductions from its lime it takes on no hardness, but rather a greater softness than it had before; and in other cases, they likewise did not see it improved by different treatments. <margin>cc</margin> And therefore, when they thought it would be found more closely and better in this substance and yet did not find it, they are compelled by this to believe and argue that the science does not exist, but is a delusion; and thus these men err just as the former ones did.

<header>SECOND PART OF THE FIRST BOOK</header>

## *The arguments of those who deny that the art is based on the mixing of hard substances with soft, and perfect substances with imperfect. Chapter XXI.*

**O**thers, however, inquiring more closely and deeply into this matter, have pondered and exerted their ingenuity, wishing to find a way so that hard things united with soft might stably harden them, and perfect things joined to imperfect might lead the latter to perfection, and generally that they might be transmuted by one another and transmute each other through a firm transformation. And for this reason, they wished to find the likeness and affinity between them, sometimes indeed through medicines <note>In alchemy, a "medicine" or "elixir" refers to a substance used to catalyze the transformation of base metals into noble ones.</note>, and at other times through the administration of fire—thinning out the coarse substances, such as Venus <note>original: "Venerem"; the alchemical name for Copper.</note> and Mars <note>original: "Martem"; the alchemical name for Iron.</note>, and thickening the subtle ones, such as Jupiter <note>original: "Iupiter"; the alchemical name for Tin.</note> and those similar to it. And some of them, believing they could complete this administration, were deceived in the mixing of these hard bodies; either because they made the result entirely brittle, or entirely too soft (not altered by the hard), or too hard (not altered by the soft). And thus they did not find the proper harmony, and for that reason, they denied that the art exists.

## *The arguments of those who deny the art based on the extraction of the soul, or the governance of fire. Chapter XXII.*

**O**thers indeed, looking even more closely and deeply, wished to alter bodies by the extraction of their souls <note>original: "animarum"; in this context, the "soul" refers to the volatile essence or "sulfur" of a metal that gives it its properties.</note>, and with the soul extracted, to treat all other things similarly. And their experience could not reach that goal, but they were deceived in their intention, judging by this that the art could not be found. Others truly, attempting to perfect these things by fire alone, were deceived in their opinion because they did not know how to arrive at the result. And these men, for this reason, thought the art did not exist. To all of whom we respond just as we did to the first group.

<summary>This page details how failed experiments in alloying metals (like copper and tin) and unsuccessful attempts to extract metallic "souls" lead many practitioners to wrongly conclude that alchemy is impossible. The author attributes these failures to a lack of skill or proper method rather than a flaw in the art itself.</summary>
<keywords>metallurgy, transmutation, copper, iron, tin, soul extraction, alchemical fire, skepticism, alloying</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical defense of alchemy, specifically addressing critics who dismiss the art based on failed experiments with glass and gemstones. The catchword "prius" indicates the next page will begin with that word.</meta>

# On the Errors of the Sophists

Those, however, who have placed that <term>virtue</term><note>The transformative power or "medicine" of the alchemists.</note> in glass and gemstones have discovered through experience that alterations cannot be brought about in bodies by means of glass and gems. This is because that which does not penetrate cannot alter; and since neither glass nor gemstones penetrate the substance, they therefore do not cause alteration. 

But even if they have attempted to unite glass with these bodies—though this can scarcely be done—they still do not achieve their purpose. This is because they create the glass out of the bodies themselves, and for this reason, they judge that this error falls upon the entire Art, and thus they argue that the Art does not exist. 

To these people we respond: they are working with improper material. Therefore, by drawing improper conclusions, they cannot destroy this Art based on their own errors. <margin>Note: Those who work in glass fail because it lacks the power of penetration.</margin>

they would sooner fail in their labor than it would be possible for the work to be completed. Therefore, if such men do not find the Art through their labors, it is not permitted to argue that the Art cannot be found by any labors at all. However, all those previously mentioned, being in error, established a single matter of their own and assumed no other besides it. And indeed, all of these have now been refuted. But many others—almost infinite in number—who create a composition of all these things, or of certain things under a diverse proportion, proceed ignorantly and unknowingly. Their error extends into infinity, since the diversity of the proportion of mixable things is infinite, and the diversity of the mixable materials is infinite. In both of these infinities, it happens that one errs infinitely—sometimes from overabundance, sometimes from diminution—and in these cases, correction is possible.
<margin>unwilling</margin>
But we, sparing ourselves the length and prolixity of speeches, do not wish to dwell upon infinite matters, since we shall hand down universal knowledge in brief statements, by which they might evidently be able to amend the infinity of their errors and correct them. Now, however, let us first discuss the natural principles according to their causes, as we made you attentive to them from the beginning and by the recollection of them.

which we also allow to be called "dry water." We have divided the stinking spirit <note>original: "spiritum foetentem"; likely referring to sulfur or arsenic compounds due to their odor</note>, for it is white in its hidden state, and both red and black in the mastery of this work. In its manifest state, however, both tend toward redness. We shall therefore speak in a brief, yet complete and sufficient discourse, regarding the generation of each of these, and likewise the manner of their generation. It is expedient, therefore, for us to broaden and expand our discourse, and to provide an individual chapter for each individual natural principle. In general, however, we say that each of them is of a most powerful composition and uniform substance; and this is because the parts of earth in them are so united with the parts of air, water, and fire through their smallest particles <note>original: "per minima"; an early concept of atoms or fundamental particles</note> that none of them can leave the other during resolution. Rather, each is resolved with the other because of the strong union they have with one another through their smallest parts, achieved gradually by a natural and equal heat condensed, multiplied, and balanced within the minerals in the bowels of the earth, according to the proper course of nature and the requirements of their essence, according to the opinion of certain ancient philosophers.

### On the natural principles of metals, according to the opinion of the moderns and the author. Chapter XXVI.

**O**thers, however, have said otherwise: that the principle was not quicksilver <note>original: "argentum vivum"; mercury</note> in its own nature, but altered and converted into its own earth, and likewise sulfur altered and changed into earth. Whence they said that the principle was something other than the "stinking spirit" and the "fugitive spirit" <note>original: "spiritus fugitiuus"; a substance that evaporates or sublimes easily, usually mercury</note> in the intention of nature. And the reason by which they were moved was this: because they do not find in the mines of silver or other metals anything that is quicksilver in its own nature, nor anything that is likewise sulfur; rather, they find each of them separated by themselves in their own...

# THIRD PART OF THE FIRST BOOK

<page-num>46</page-num>

The metals exist in their own ore according to their own nature. And similarly, by another line of reasoning, they also affirm this: they say there is no transition from one contrary to another contrary except through a middle disposition <note>A "middle disposition" refers to an intermediate state or "mean" between two extremes, such as a semi-solid state between liquid and solid.</note>. Therefore, since this is the case, they are forced to confess and believe that there is no transition from the softness of quicksilver <note>original: "argenti uiui"; mercury</note> to the hardness of any of the metals, except through a disposition that lies between their hardness and softness. 

But in the mines, they do not find anything in which this middle disposition is preserved; therefore, by this reasoning, they are compelled to believe that quicksilver and sulfur <note>The two primary principles of alchemy: Mercury (spirit/liquidity) and Sulfur (soul/combustibility).</note> are not, in their own nature, the principle intended by nature. Rather, it is something else that follows from the alteration of their essences at the root of nature into an earthy substance. And the method is this: first, each of them is converted to an earthy nature, and from both of these earthy natures, a very thin smoke <note>original: "fumus"; often used interchangeably with "vapor" in alchemical meteorology.</note> is released by the multiplied heat in the bowels of the earth. 

This dual smoke is the immediate matter of metals. When this smoke <margin>c</margin> has been "cooked" <term>decocted: the process of refining or ripening a substance through steady heat</term> by the temperate heat of the mine, it is converted into the nature of a certain earth, and therefore it undergoes a certain fixation. Then, water flowing through the bowels of the mine and the sponginess of the earth dissolves it, and is uniformly united to it in a natural and firm union. 

And so, those who hold this opinion have said that the water flowing through the passages of the earth finds a dissolvable substance from the substance of the earth in its bowels; it dissolves that substance and unites uniformly with it, until both the dissolved substance of the earth and the flowing water that dissolves it become one by a natural union. To such a mixture all the elements come according to their due natural proportion, and they are mixed "through the smallest parts" <note>original: "per minima"; an early physical concept suggesting mixing at the atomic or molecular level rather than just a bulk blend</note> until they create a uniform mixture. And this mixture, through successive decoction in the mine, is thickened and hardened, and becomes a metal. And these thinkers, although they are close to the truth, do not yet grasp the pure truth.

### *The division of the discourse concerning the three principles, namely, Sulfur, Arsenic, and Quicksilver. Chapter XXVII.*

### Concerning Arsenic. Chapter XXIX.

**I**t remains for us to determine the nature of Arsenic at present. We say, therefore, that it is of a subtle matter, and similar to sulfur; <margin>.</margin> therefore, it need not be defined differently than sulfur. However, it is distinguished from sulfur in this: that it is easily a tincture <note>A "tincture" in alchemy is a substance capable of infusing or changing the color and quality of a metal.</note> of whiteness, but only with great difficulty a tincture of redness; whereas sulfur is easily a tincture of redness, but only with great difficulty of whiteness. Furthermore, there are two kinds of sulfur and Arsenic: namely, the lemon-yellow and the red, which are useful to this art; the many other kinds, however, are not. Arsenic is "fixed" <note>To "fix" a substance means to make it resistant to evaporation or change when heated.</note> just like sulfur. Indeed, the sublimation <note>Sublimation is the process of heating a solid until it turns into vapor and then collecting the purified solid as it cools.</note> of both is better when performed from the calxes <note>A "calx" is the powdery residue left after a metal or mineral has been roasted or oxidized.</note> of metals. Yet sulfur and Arsenic are not the perfecting matter of this work. For they are not complete unto perfection, though they may provide a support for perfection in certain cases. One should choose that which is bright, scaly, and easily split. <margin>cc</margin>

### Concerning Quicksilver, or Mercury. Chapter XXX.

**A**s for Quicksilver, which is also called Mercury by the custom of the ancients, it is a viscous water in the bowels of the earth, consisting of a subtle substance of white earth united with a most temperate heat in a total union down to the smallest parts, until the moist is tempered by the dry, and the dry by the moist equally. Therefore, it flees from a flat surface easily because of the moisture of its water. Yet it does not stick to surfaces, even though it possesses a viscous moisture, because of that dryness which tempers it and prevents it from adhering. It is also (as some say) the matter of metals when combined with sulfur. It also adheres easily to three minerals,

## On the Sun, or Gold. Chapter XXXII.

**W**e have provided you, in the general chapter, with a summary of the intentions regarding metals; however, we shall now provide you with a specific discourse on each one. And first, concerning Gold. We say, therefore, that Gold is a metallic body, lemon-yellow <note>original: "citrinum"</note>, heavy, mute <note>meaning it does not ring or resonate loudly when struck, unlike copper</note>, shining, equally digested in the belly of the earth, washed for a very long time by mineral water, extensible under the hammer, fusible, and able to withstand the trial of the ash-cup <term>cupellation: a refining process where lead is oxidized in a porous cup of bone ash to isolate precious metals</term> and the cement <term>cementation: a process using salt and heat to purify gold from silver</term>. From this definition, therefore, you may conclude that something is not gold unless it possesses all the causes and differences of the definition of gold. Nevertheless, whatever metal it thoroughly colors yellow, brings to equality, and cleanses, it makes gold out of every kind of metal. Therefore, through the work of nature, we have weighed the matter and found that by artifice, copper <note>original: "æs"</note> can be changed into gold. For we have seen in copper mines, from which water flowed, that the water carried with it very thin scales of copper, <margin>and those same</margin> and by a long and continuous flow, it washed and cleansed them. Then, when the water ceased to flow, we saw these scales cooked out by the heat of the sun in dry sand for a period of three years, among which most true gold was found. We estimated, therefore, that they had been cleansed by the benefit of the water, and by the heat of the Sun in the dryness of the sand, they were equally digested and reached a state of equality. Thus, imitating nature as far as we are able, we likewise alter things, though we cannot follow nature in every respect. Gold is also the most precious of metals, and it is a tincture of redness, because it tints and transforms every body. <margin>not without utility</margin> It is calcined <note>reduced to powder by heat</note> and dissolved, though not without utility, and it is a medicine that brings joy and preserves the body in youth. <margin>cooked in the heat of the sun. line 22. by the waters. notable things.</margin> It is broken most easily with Mercury, and

### Concerning Venus, or Copper. Chapter 36.

<margin>concerning the sound, omitted.</margin>
**L**et our discourse at present be concerning Venus <note>The alchemical name for Copper</note>. It is a metallic body, livid, participating in a dark redness, capable of being ignited, fusible, extensible under the hammer, but fleeing from the ash-test <note>cupellation</note> and cementing. Venus, therefore (as has been told), in the depth of its substance manifests the color and essence of gold, yet it is hammered and fired like silver and gold. Therefore, you should take the secret from it, because it is a medium between the Sun and Moon, and it happens that its nature is easily converted to either one; it is of good conversion and requires little labor. It combines vehemently with tutia <note>zinc oxide or zinc ore</note>, which colors it yellow with a good yellowness, and from this you may draw profit. We are thus excused by it from the labors of its hardening and ignition. Therefore, you should choose it above the other imperfect metals in the minor and middle work, but in the greater work, not at all. However, it has this flaw compared to Jupiter: it turns livid easily and receives infection from sharp and acrid substances, and to eradicate that flaw <gloss>vice</gloss> is no easy craftsmanship, but rather a deep matter.

# THE SUM OF PERFECTION BY GEBER.

### Concerning Mars, or Iron. Chapter XXXVII.

<margin>.</margin>**T**he description of Mars <note>The alchemical name for Iron</note>, and its entire secret, is a work of nature; for it is a metallic body that is very livid <note>bluish-gray</note>, with a slight redness, sharing in a non-pure whiteness. It is hard, combustible, and fusible—though not with a direct or easy melting—extensible under the hammer, and very resonant. Mars is, however, difficult to handle because of the weakness of its fusion. If it is melted by a medicine <note>an alchemical reagent or elixir</note> that changes its nature, it joins with the Sun <note>Gold</note> and the Moon <note>Silver</note>, and is not separated by the trial <note>the cupellation or assaying process</note> without great industry. But if it is prepared and joined, it cannot be separated by any artifice, provided its fixed nature is not changed, but only its impurity removed. Therefore, the tincture of redness is easily achieved, but whiteness only with difficulty. And when it is joined without being altered, it does not change the color of the mixture, but increases it in quantity. Among all bodies, therefore, Jupiter <note>Tin</note> is transformed most splendidly, clearly, brightly, and perfectly into a solar or lunar body. But in it, the work is of easy handling but long labor. After that, Venus <note>Copper</note> is chosen, though of less perfect mutation and more difficult handling, yet of shorter labor than Jupiter. Saturn <note>Lead</note>, after Venus, has a perfection in transmutation diminished from hers; it is of easy handling, but of the longest labor. Mars, however, stands among the others as being of the least perfection in transmutation, of the most difficult handling, and of the longest labor. Therefore, whichever <gloss>bodies</gloss> are further removed from the speed of liquefaction <note>melting point</note> are found to be of more difficult handling in the work of transmutation. Such are Venus and Mars. Those that are more [fusible] are more [easily handled], and those most [fusible] are most [easily handled]. Whichever bodies participate more in lividity and infection from the earth are found to be of greater labor and lesser perfection. These various differences of perfections, which we determined a little while ago, belong to the artifice of the minor and middle work.

### On Sublimation, and why it was invented. Chapter 39.

**P**ursuing therefore our purpose, we speak of sublimation <term>Sublimation: the process of heating a solid until it turns into vapor and then collecting the purified solid as it cools</term>. And the reason for its invention was because our ancestors did not find—nor have we, nor those who will come after us—anything that could be united with the bodies <note>In alchemy, "bodies" usually refers to the seven solid metals</note> except for spirits alone, or something that contained within itself the nature of both body and <gloss>spirit</gloss> spirit. And when these were cast upon the bodies without their purification, they saw that they either gave imperfect colors or entirely corrupted, burned, blackened, and defiled them. And this occurred according to the diversity of the spirits themselves. For some are burning, such as sulfur, arsenic, and marcasite <note>A term often used for various metallic sulfides, particularly iron pyrites</note>, and these indeed corrupt entirely. Others, however, do not burn, such as every kind of tutia <note>Zinc oxide, often found as a crust in furnaces</note>, and these indeed give imperfect colors. This is because those things which burn, blacken, and defile do so for a twofold reason. One is because the burning oiliness of sulfur—whose property is to be easily ignited, and through ignition to be blackened, and consequently to blacken other things—has not been removed from them. The other reason is the earthiness, which likewise was not removed from them. However, in those things where a perfect color is not given, the cause is the earthiness, which makes the color leaden. Burning can also create a leaden color. Therefore, we have devised...

to cleanse these from their burning oiliness—that is, those that possess it—and all of them from their earthy superfluity. And we could arrive at this through no other mastery except by sublimation alone. For when the fire elevates, it always elevates the more subtle parts, and therefore leaves behind the coarser ones. From this it is clear that by sublimation, the spirit is cleansed of the earthiness which both hindered its entry <note>The "entry" or "ingression" refers to the substance's ability to penetrate and transform a metal.</note> and gave it a foul color. Indeed, experience makes this well enough known to your own sight, that they have been freed from that earthiness. Because we have seen an adherence to bodies with alteration in no other things than in the spirits, we were excused by no other cause from the necessity of preparing them, along with their cleansing, which is done through <margin>...</margin> sublimation; therefore, this was the necessary cause of its invention. Let us therefore describe the entire order of sublimation without omission, and the cause of it.

--- Page 82 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of chemical purification from the previous section, focusing specifically on the sublimation of minerals.</meta>

# FOURTH PART OF THE FIRST BOOK

<page-num>60</page-num>

If arsenic or sulfur is to be sublimated <note>Sublimation is the process of heating a solid until it turns into vapor and then collecting the purified solid as it cools and reforms.</note>, it is necessary that they be sublimated by a gentle fire. This is because they possess very subtle parts joined uniformly with coarse parts; if the heat were too high, their entire substance would rise without any purification, and would instead be blackened and scorched. Therefore, so that one may separate the unclean, earthy substance, it is necessary to find two types of ingenuity: namely, the correct proportion of the fire, and the method of cleansing by mixing the substance with dregs <note>original: "fecibus"; likely referring to inert materials like salt or crushed pottery used to trap impurities.</note>. This mixing with dregs captures the coarse parts and keeps them pressed down at the bottom of the *aludels* <term>aludel: a pear-shaped chemical vessel used in sublimation</term>, and does not permit them to climb.

<margin>..</margin>Hence it is necessary for the craftsman to apply a triple degree of fire to the sublimation. The first degree is proportioned such that only the altered, cleaner, and brighter parts rise through it, until he clearly sees them cleansed of their earthy foulness.

<margin>∴</margin>The second degree is so that whatever remains of their pure essence within the dregs may be sublimated with a greater expression of fire—namely, with the ignition <note>Heating until glowing</note> of the *aludel* and the dregs themselves, which he will be able to see with his own eyes.

The third degree is that a very weak fire should be administered without dregs to that which has already been sublimated from the dregs and purified. This is done so that hardly any of it rises; <margin>...</margin>that which does rise from it is a most subtle thing which is of no value in this work, because that is the substance through which scorching <note>original: "adustio"</note> occurs in sulfurs.

The entire intention of sublimation is therefore this: that once its earthiness is removed through the proper administration of fire, and once that most subtle and smoky part (which brings about scorching and corruption) is likewise cast away, there remains for us that part which consists in a state of balance. This part achieves a simple melting over the fire without any scorching, and without fleeing from the fire or bursting into flame.

<margin>⁘</margin>That the most subtle part is the cause of scorching is proven by the most manifest arguments. For fire converts into its own nature each of those things which is most akin to it, because <margin>.</margin>...

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the chemical properties of spirits (volatile substances) and the necessity of using "dregs" or fixed bases during the sublimation process to achieve purity.</meta>

<page-num>61</page-num>
->ON GEBER'S SUMMIT OF PERFECTION<-
<note>original: "Summae Perfectionis Geberis." Geber is the Latinized name of Jabir ibn Hayyan, the most influential figure in early alchemy.</note>

from every combustible thing, that which is subtle is more closely related to it; and that which is even more subtle is more related still. Therefore, that which is most subtle is the most closely related. Likewise, from experience, we know that sulfur or arsenic that has not been sublimated <note>Sublimation is the process of heating a solid until it turns into vapor and then collecting the purified solid as it cools.</note> catches fire very quickly—sulfur even more easily. However, once sublimated, it does not catch fire directly, but instead evaporates and is thinned out without flaming, though melting occurs first. By these facts, it is clear that our discourse is truthful.

---

### On the dregs of metallic bodies to be added to spirits in sublimation, and their quantity and quality. Chapter XLI.

<margin>See Book II, Chapters 4 and 3.</margin>
The proof of the administration of dregs <note>term: "feces" (dregs). In alchemy, this refers to the solid, fixed residue or base material used to hold or filter the volatile "spirits" during heating.</note> in their proper proportion is this: that a material should be chosen with which the spirits to be sublimated are most compatible, and with which they may be most deeply intermingled. For that material to which they are more closely united is more powerful in retaining the dregs of the substances being sublimated than one to which they are not. The proof of this is sufficiently reasonable and manifest.

The proof that the administration of dregs is necessary is this: if the sulfur or arsenic to be sublimated were not joined with the dregs of some fixed thing, then it would be necessary for them to rise with their entire substance, unpurified. Those who are practiced in sublimations know by experience that we speak the truth. 

The proof that it is necessary to administer such dregs—those with which the substances to be sublimated are compatible and united in their depths—is because if the dregs were not mixed down to the smallest parts <note>term: "per minima." An early chemical concept suggesting mixing at the level of the smallest possible particles, similar to a molecular level.</note>, then the same thing would happen as if they had no dregs at all. That is, their entire essence would rise without any purification, just as they rise with their whole substance when without dregs; therefore, it must necessarily happen that they sublimate away from dregs to which they are not united. Experience knows this to be true for anyone who has seen and known this: for when one sublimates from a thing foreign to the nature of [metallic] bodies, he sublimates...

<summary>Geber explains that volatile "spirits" like sulfur and arsenic must be mixed with compatible solid "dregs" (fixed bases) to ensure they are purified during sublimation rather than simply evaporating in their raw state. He emphasizes that this mixing must occur at the most minute level to be effective.</summary>

# FOURTH PART OF THE FIRST BOOK

<margin>Dialogue on Alchemy, page 105, verse 4. These things are repeated.</margin>
in vain, so that he would find it in no way purified after their ascension <note>original: "ascensionem"; in alchemy, this refers to the rising of vapors during heating</note>. If, however, he sublimated <note><term>sublimation</term>: the process of heating a solid until it becomes a vapor and then collecting it as a solid again to purify it</note> it with the lime <note><term>lime</term> (calx): a powder produced by heating a metal or mineral to high temperatures</note> of any of the bodies <note>original: "corporum"; here referring to the seven classical metals</note>, then he sublimated well and was able to cleanse it perfectly and with ease. It is therefore the intention with them that they be administered from the limes of metals. For in them, sublimation is of easy operation, but with other things it is most difficult. There is, therefore, nothing that can take their place. We do not say, however, that sublimation is impossible without the limes of the bodies, but that it is most difficult and of the longest labor and delay, even unto despair. But in this, however, it has some benefit, because that which is sublimated without dregs <note>original: "fecibus"; the solid residue or impurities left at the bottom</note>, or without the limes of the bodies, is of great 
<margin>But notice also this</margin>
quantity, while that which is with dregs is of less. Furthermore, that which is with the limes of the bodies is of the least quantity, but of the easiest and shortest labor. That which excuses us most from the dregs of the bodies is every kind of prepared salt and things of a similar nature. And with these, sublimation of a great quantity is also possible for us, because the separation of the dregs to be sublimated is made easy by them through the dissolving of the salts, which happens in no other things. The proportion of the dregs, however, is such that the quantity of the dregs should equal the quantity of the things to be sublimated. In this, even an unrefined craftsman <note>original: "artifex"</note> will not be able to err. For a moderate craftsman, it happens that placing half the weight of the things to be sublimated from the dregs is sufficient, and in this he ought not to err. For the practiced and especially the expert, the smallest part of them suffices. For the smaller the quantities of dregs are, the greater the abundance of the sublimation must necessarily be, provided that according to the diversity of the removal of the dregs, there is a proportional removal of the fire. For in a small quantity, a small fire is drawn out for perfection; in a large quantity, a large fire; in a greater quantity, a greater fire.

## On regulating the fire in Sublimation.
### Chapter 42.

<margin>Book 1, chapter 5.</margin>
<margin>iron scales <note>original: "ferri squama"; likely referring to iron oxide or dross used in the process.</note></margin>
<margin>., .. ... ... " .. ,, ,, ,, ,, ,,</margin>
**B**ut since fire is not a thing that can be easily measured, it happens very frequently that one errs in its use when they are not well-practiced, arising as much from the diversity of furnaces and the wood to be burned as from the vessels and how they are fitted together. However, we provide a general rule: that in the beginning, it is useful to use a small fire to press out only the watery portion <note>original: "aqueitatem"; the moisture or volatile impurities</note> from the substances to be sublimated. Once this is removed, if anything rises because of that heat, the fire should not be increased at this stage, so that the most subtle part may be lifted by the weakest fire and set aside, as this part is often the cause of scorching <note>original: "adustionis"; overheating that ruins the chemical composition</note>. But when little or nothing more rises (which you can test by inserting a cotton wick into the upper hole of the aludel <term>aludel: a pear-shaped chemical vessel used in sublimation</term>), let the fire beneath it be strengthened. As for how strong the fire ought to be, the experience of the wick will show you.

### On avoiding errors regarding the quantity of dregs and the arrangement of the furnace in the sublimation of sulfur and arsenic. Also on constructing the furnace and choosing the wood. Chapter XLIII.

**I**t is therefore necessary for us to set the craftsman right in all matters where one might err through ignorance during the sublimation of these two spirits <note>The "two spirits" refer to sulfur and arsenic, which were considered "volatile" or "spiritual" because they transform into vapor when heated.</note>. Let us first explain that if he should use too many dregs <note>original: "feces"; the solid residue or impurities left after a process</note>, then unless he increases the fire proportionately, nothing of the substance to be sublimated will rise. How he may find that balance, however, has already been sufficiently explained to him. And if he should use a small quantity of dregs, or if they are not made from the lime <note>original: "calce"; a powder produced by heating a substance to high temperatures</note> of metals, then if he does not find the correct proportion, the substance to be sublimated will rise along with its entire mass <note>meaning the impurities will rise with the vapor instead of being separated</note>. I have likewise provided a sufficient method for discovering this. Similarly, errors can occur regarding the furnace; for a large furnace gives off a great heat, while a small one gives little, provided that the wood and the openings of its "ears" <note>original: "auricularum"; likely referring to the air vents or intake holes of the furnace</note> are proportional. If, therefore, he places a large quantity of the material to be sublimated into a small furnace, the fire—

### From what material and in what form the Aludel vessel should be made. Chapter 44.

<margin>ib. 11. chapter 6.</margin>
<margin>Aludel: all these things are clear. <note>original: "σαφῆ πάντα ταῦτα" (Greek). A marginal note likely added by a later scholar or editor.</note></margin>
The **I**ntention for the **Aludel** <term>Aludel: a pear-shaped chemical vessel used for sublimation, often stacked in series</term> vessel is that it should be fashioned as a thick glass vessel. For it is not effective if made of another material, unless perhaps it were of a substance similar to thick glass; for only glass and its likeness, since it lacks pores, is able to hold the spirits <term>spirits: volatile substances like mercury, sulfur, or ammonia that vaporize when heated</term> so they do not flee and be destroyed by the fire. There is no other material, because through their pores the spirits are successively diminished and vanish. Nor are metals effective in this, because the spirits, on account of their friendship and affinity with them, penetrate the metals and unite with them, whereby passing through them they vanish; this is clearly proven by those things which have been determined by us. By necessity and experience it has been found that we have spoken the truth; therefore, we are not excused from the adoption of glass in the composition of the Aludel. Let there be fashioned, therefore, a round glass bowl, the bottom of which should be of slight curvature, and in the middle of its joints <note>original: "spondilium," referring to the rim or the point where the vessel sections meet</note> let a glass zone <note>a raised ridge or flange</note> be formed surrounding it, and upon that zone let a wall be founded

The round [wall] should be equidistant from the wall of the shell <note>original: "concha"; a basin-shaped vessel</note> to the thickness of the cover of the shell itself, so that in this space the wall of the cover may fall loosely without pressure. Indeed, let the height of this wall be to the measure of the height of the shell's wall, or a little more, or a little less. Let two covers be formed to the measure of this concavity of the two walls, equal to each other, whose length should be equal and of one span <note>a unit of measure, roughly 9 inches</note>, and their shape should be one shape, namely pyramidal, in the heads of which there should be two equal holes. That is, one in one and the other in the other, in both of which a large hen's feather can fit, as will be seen more clearly here a little later.

The intention of the shell-vessel, therefore, is that its cover can be moved according to the will of the craftsman, and that the joint be ingenious, through which the spirits <note>volatile substances or vapors</note> find no exit without any luting <note>original: "lutatione"; sealing with clay or paste</note>. Therefore, he who can be more ingenious in this should not excuse himself from it on account of our tradition. And there is a special intention regarding this: namely, that the interior shell with its joints <note>original: "spondilibus"</note> should enter its cover up to the middle. For since it is the nature of smokes to ascend and not descend, by this we have found through our first invention that the spirits have no exit for consumption <note>loss or escape</note>, and for this reason, it excels other methods which we have sought through our invention. And by experiencing this, they will see <margin>to have estimated</margin> that we have estimated the truth concerning it.

Indeed, there is also one intention: that the head of the aludel <note>a subliming pot, usually pear-shaped</note> be emptied very often, lest by the excessive multiplication of things to be sublimated <note>the process of heating a solid until it becomes vapor and then solidifies again</note> into it, they fall to its bottom, and the multitude of the repetition of the sublimation keep [the craftsman] occupied for a long time. There is similarly another intention: that what ascends as powder toward the proximity of the hole of the aludel's head be always separated apart from that which is found to have ascended as fused and dense in pieces, and at its bottom, transparent and clear, adhering to the joints of the vessel, since this has less...

**N**ow indeed, let us determine the whole intention of the sublimation of quicksilver. It is, therefore, the complete and highest <gloss>1.</gloss> purification of its earthiness, and the <gloss>2.</gloss> removal of its wateriness. For we are excused from the labor of removing its combustibility <note>original: "adustionis," referring to the tendency of a substance to burn or oxidize</note>, since it possesses none. We say, therefore, that the ingenious method of separating <gloss>103. 104.</gloss> its superfluous earth is to mix it with things with which it has no affinity, and to repeat its sublimation from them many times. And of this kind are talc, and the lime of eggshells, and of white marble. Similarly, glass ground very finely, and every kind of prepared salt <gloss>common salt</gloss>. For it is cleansed by these; but by other things having an affinity with it (unless they are <gloss>see two</gloss> bodies of perfection <gloss>gold and silver</gloss>), it is instead corrupted, because all such things have a sulfurous quality, which, ascending with it in the sublimation, corrupts it.

And in this you see the evidence of experience, because if you sublime it from tin or from lead, you will see it stained with blackness after the sublimation. Therefore, its sublimation is better through those things with which it does not agree. It would be better with those things with which it does agree, if they did not have a sulfurous quality. Therefore, sublimation from lime is better than from all other things, since <gloss>with</gloss> that it has little agreement, and it has no sulfurous quality. Now the method <gloss>v. 2. 2.</gloss> of removing its superfluous wateriness is that when it is mixed with the limes from which it ought to be sublimed, it should be ground and mixed with them by soaking <term>imbibition: the process of a solid absorbing a liquid</term>, until nothing of it appears, and afterwards the wateriness of the soaking should be removed over a very slow fire. As that recedes, the wateriness of the quicksilver also recedes with it. Let the fire be so gentle, however, that the whole substance of the quicksilver does not ascend by it. Therefore, from the manifold repetition of soaking with grinding, and

If it possesses a bright and most white color, and is transparent, then you have cleansed it well; if not, you have not. Whence it happens that some form iron, some truly lead, some <term>Venus</term> <note>copper</note> through it, and some tin. This happens because of negligence in purification, sometimes of that substance alone, sometimes in the <term>sulfur</term> mixed with it, or its companion. If therefore you have cleansed it by sublimating directly, and have perfected it, it will be a firm and perfect tincture of whiteness, to which there is no equal.

### On the Sublimation of Marcasite. Chapter XLVI.

**T**he highest intention of the sublimation of quicksilver having been sufficiently handed down, let us now approach the sublimation of <term>Marcasite</term> <note>In medieval alchemy, marcasite often referred to various metallic sulfides, particularly iron pyrites or bismuth.</note> itself. There are, however, two sublimations of it.

<summary>This page details the final purification of a white alchemical substance (likely arsenic) through repeated sublimation and fixation, emphasizing that the quality of the final "tincture" depends entirely on the purity of the preparation. It then transitions into the specific methods for sublimating marcasite.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of mineral sublimation, specifically focusing on the refinement of marcasite and the separation of its components.</meta>

# FOURTH PART OF THE FIRST BOOK

The first process is indeed completed without the use of fire <note>original: "fine ignitione"; referring to a heat level below the point of glowing or combustion</note>, while the other is done with fire. And this is the reason: it possesses a dual substance. One is, namely, pure sulfur in its own nature; the other is truly "mortified" quicksilver <note>original: "argentum uiuum mortificatum"; "mortified" refers to mercury that has been "killed" or fixed into a solid state, likely a sulfide or oxide</note>. The first substance is useful in the same way as sulfur. The second is useful as mortified quicksilver that has been moderately prepared. Let us therefore take up this latter one, because through it we are excused from the need for the aforementioned quicksilver and from the labor of its mortification.

Therefore, the entire method of its sublimation <term>sublimation: the process of heating a solid until it turns into vapor and then solidifies again, used here for purification</term> is as follows: let it be ground, placed in an aludel <note>a pear-shaped chemical vessel used in sublimation</note>, and let its sulfur be sublimated without fire, always removing that which is sublimated from <margin>..</margin> it, very frequently, for the reason stated; and then increasing the strength of the fire until the aludel glows. And the first sublimation of the marcasite <note>a common iron sulfide mineral, often confused with pyrite in historical texts</note> ought to take place in the sublimation vessel until the sulfur has departed, and afterwards, proceed in that vessel successively and in an orderly manner.

<keywords>sublimation, marcasite, sulfur, quicksilver, mortification, aludel, alchemy, mineralogy</keywords>

# THE HEIGHT OF PERFECTION BY GEBER
<page-num>73</page-num>

<note>The text describes the preparation of a "sublimatory," a vessel used to heat a solid substance until it rises as vapor and collects again in solid form at the top.</note>

...very much. And let it be measured from the opening of that vessel, down near to the bottom, to the length of one hand including the fingers. From that point up to its top, let the vessel be glazed <term>vitrified (lined with glass)</term> on the inside <margin>..</margin> with a very thick glazing. And upon its top, let an alembic <note>a distilling cap</note> with a wide "nose" <note>the delivery tube or spout</note> be placed. For in such a vessel, that substance is sublimed. Therefore, let the bottom be joined to its vessel by a clay of firm tenacity <term>lute (a sealant made of clay and binders)</term>, and upon that bottom let Marcasite <note>original: "Marchesita"; likely iron pyrites or a similar metallic mineral used here as a heat-distributing layer</note> be sprinkled. And upon the head <margin>...</margin> of the vessel, place the wide-nosed alembic, and let it be put into a furnace, the property of which must be to provide a strong firing—specifically, the heat required for the melting of silver or of Venus <note>original: "veneris"; the alchemical name for copper</note>, if the craftsman should require it. We shall sufficiently hand down the description of this furnace to you at the conclusion of our work, where we shall describe the varieties of all instruments. 

Surround the top of the furnace with a wheel-shaped cover having a hole in its center, sized to the magnitude of the vessel, through which that vessel can enter. Seal the joints around the vessel and the furnace <margin>.:</margin> with clay, so that the escaping fire does not hinder you in the adhesion of the sublimation. Leave only four small windows in the wheel-cover, which can be closed and opened, through which coals may be put into the furnace. Similarly, in the sides of the furnace, let there be another four intermediate windows below those, through which coals may likewise be sent. Also, include six or eight holes <margin>..</margin> the size of a pinky finger, which are never to be closed, so that through them the furnace can freely rid itself of smoky vapors. Let those holes be at the junction of the furnace and its wheel-cover. 

Indeed, this is a furnace of great firing, the supports <term>spondilia (structural ribs or tiers)</term> of which are at a height of two cubits <note>roughly 3 feet or 90 centimeters</note>. In the middle, let there be a grate <note>original: "rota"; literally 'wheel', here referring to a circular grate</note> perforated with very small and <margin>.:</margin> frequent holes, strongly attached with clay. Let the tops of the holes be narrow above, but wide below, so that ash or coals can fall more freely from them; keep these holes continuously open for the intake of air. For the free and plentiful intake of air through the lower holes is one cause <margin>.</margin> of great heat throughout the furnace. Therefore, practice with it, and [you will find] it...

you will find. Truly, the reason for the length of the vessel is so that a great quantity of it may extend outside the fire and be cooled, so that the vapors of the <term>sublimation</term> <note>The process of heating a solid until it turns into vapor and then collecting it as it cools back into a solid state.</note> may find a place of cooling and adhere there, and not find a way of escape and their own destruction. This, moreover, is known by the man who, when he had sublimated in short <term>aludels</term> <note>Sublimation pots, usually pear-shaped and stacked in a series.</note>, found nothing of the sublimated material because, on account of the shortness of the <term>aludel</term>, the heat was equal throughout the whole of it. Therefore, the material to be sublimated remained always converted into the substance of vapor and did not adhere anywhere, but successively vanished through the pores of the vessel. Therefore, in all things to be sublimated, the vessel should be extended for the greater part of itself into its place of cooling.

Truly, the reason for the <term>vitrification</term> <note>Glazing or coating with glass to make the ceramic non-porous.</note> of the vessel is so that the vapors ascending in the place of their ascent do not find the wall of the <term>aludel</term> to be porous, and, penetrating it, flee away. Therefore, the place of their ascent is vitrified so that the way of escape is blocked for them. The bottom of the <term>aludel</term>, however, is not glazed, because its bottom stands in the fire, which would melt its <term>vitrification</term>; and if that were melted, both its bottom and likewise the material to be sublimated would be melted and become glass. For it is the property of glass to conquer all things and convert them to itself.

Therefore, all these things having been considered along with their causes, let a fire be made under the <term>aludel</term> until you have been made certain by true experience that the whole of it has ascended. Now, the "experience" <note>In this context, a practical test or trial.</note> is the insertion of a well-baked earthen rod, having a small hole in its head reaching to its middle, to the size of a pinky finger, near the very thing from which the <term>sublimation</term> is made. And if anything of the material to be sublimated adheres to the hole, it is not yet totally sublimated; but if not, then it is sublimated. And by this same exercise, you will be able to be made certain in all things to be sublimated. Truly, the description of the <term>aludel</term> vessel for the final <term>sublimation</term> of <term>Marchesite</term> <note>A term often used for iron pyrites or other metallic ores with a bright luster.</note>, along with the furnace and the rod for its testing, is this:

### On the sublimation of Magnesia and Tutty; likewise of the imperfect bodies, and on the addition of the material that elevates them. Chapter 48

<margin>Book 2, Chapter 10</margin>
**T**he method for the <term>sublimation</term><note>Sublimation is the process of heating a solid until it turns into a vapor and then solidifies again on a cooler surface, used here to purify substances.</note> of <term>Magnesia</term><note>In this context, Magnesia likely refers to a variety of minerals, often manganese ore or a form of magnetite, rather than modern magnesium.</note> and <term>Tutty</term><note>Tutty (Latin: *Tutia*) is an impure form of zinc oxide collected from the flues of smelting furnaces.</note> follows the same principle as the final sublimation of <term>Marcasite</term><note>Marcasite (Latin: *Marchesita*) was a general term for various metallic sulfides, often iron pyrites.</note>. For none of these substances can be sublimated without the application of fire <note>original: "ignitione"</note>; therefore, they all share a single purpose, governed by the same causes and experimental trials.

# FOURTH PART OF THE FIRST BOOK

...to the same, and it follows one general order. Because whatever must necessarily be sublimated <note>Sublimation: the process of heating a solid until it turns into vapor and then solidifies again, used here to purify substances.</note> by means of fire ought to be sublimated without dregs. For these substances contain sufficient, or rather superfluous, dregs within themselves; a sign of this is the very difficulty of their sublimation.
<margin>.</margin> Similarly, all "diminished" bodies <note>Refers to base metals that were considered "imperfect" compared to gold or silver.</note> are sublimated according to this same order. No difference occurs except that the fire for sublimation is more intense for metals than it is for Magnesia, Marchesita <note>Iron pyrites or similar metallic ores.</note>, and Tutia <note>Zinc oxide, often found as a crust in furnaces.</note>. Likewise, metals do not differ in their sublimations, except that some require the admixture of a substance to help lift them, while others do not, so that their elevation may be made easier. However, one specific practical experience found to be good in the sublimation of metals is this: that there should not be a large quantity of the body to be sublimated at the bottom of the aludel <note>A pear-shaped laboratory vessel used in sublimation.</note>, because a great mass hinders the process. The bottom of the sublimation aludel should be flat, not concave, so that the body spread thinly and evenly over the bottom can be raised equally and fully in all its parts. The bodies that require the addition of a lifting substance are Venus <note>The alchemical name for Copper.</note> and Mars <note>The alchemical name for Iron</note>, because of the slowness of their melting. Thus, Venus requires Tutia, while Mars requires Arsenic; with these, they are easily elevated, since they agree with them most closely. Therefore, after considering these factors, let the sublimation be performed as with Tutia and similar things, and let their sublimation be arranged in this same order with its causes and practical experiments.

-> Concerning descension, and the method of purifying by means of pellets. Chapter 49. (Book II, Chapter 11). <-

<margin>⁘</margin> **H**aving now related the intentions of sublimation along with all its causes, it remains for us to show the method of Descension <note>Descension: a process where a substance is melted so that it flows downward into a receiver, leaving impurities behind.</note>, likewise with its causes and its determined and complete order.

--- Page 99 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical discourse on alchemical apparatus, specifically focusing on the process of "descension" (downward filtration/melting). It follows the previous discussions on furnace construction and sublimation.</meta>

# THE SUM OF PERFECTION BY GEBER

There were, therefore, three reasons for this invention. The first is so that when some material is enclosed in that vessel called the <term>descensory of Chemistry</term> <note>original: "descensorium Chemiae"; a vessel designed for downward distillation or melting where the liquid flows through a hole in the bottom</note>, it may descend through its opening after it has melted, and by its descent, we may be certain that it has undergone fusion. Another reason
<margin>.</margin> is so that weak bodies <note>metals with low melting points or those easily oxidized, like tin or lead</note> may be preserved from combustion by this method after their reduction from their <term>calxes</term> <note>original: "calcibus"; a calx is the powdery oxide left after a metal is burned or "calcined"</note>. For when we attempt to reduce weak bodies from their calxes, we cannot reduce every part of them at one time. If, therefore, that part which was first reduced into a metallic body were to wait for the reduction of the whole, a greater
<margin>..</margin> quantity of it would vanish through the fire. It was necessary, therefore, to devise a way so that as soon as a part is reduced, it is removed from the fire. This, moreover, is done by the descensory. There is also a third cause for its invention: the purification of bodies from every foreign matter. For the melted body descends clean and leaves every foreign thing behind in the cavity of the vessel. Therefore, since descension was invented for these three
<margin>⸫</margin> necessities, let its method be determined along with its instrument (previously described) and its causes. We say, therefore, that its form shall be such that its bottom is pointed, and its walls should terminate equally and without roughness at the sharpness of the bottom, so that every fusible substance can descend freely without
<margin>...</margin> sticking to the hole of that bottom. Its cover (if necessary) should be in the likeness of a flat <term>platter</term> <note>original: "parapsidis"; a shallow dish or bowl</note> and should fit it well; they should be made of good and firm earth, not easily cracked by the pressure of the fire. Therefore, let the thing intended for descension be placed into it upon round sticks made of earth,
<margin>⸫</margin> so that they may be closer to the upper bottom; let it be covered, the joint secured, and placed in a fire of coals. Let it be blown upon until the whole thing descends into the vessel placed beneath it. However, if the substance is difficult to melt, it can be placed upon a flat tablet, or one of small concavity, from which it can
<margin>.</margin> easily descend with the tilting of the head of the descensory, once it has been melted.

<summary>Geber explains the three purposes of the descensory vessel: confirming fusion, preventing the loss of "weak" metals during reduction, and purifying metals from dross. He provides specific instructions on the vessel's conical shape and the method of heating it.</summary>

<keywords>descensory, fusion, reduction of calx, purification of metals, alchemical apparatus, Geber, metallurgy</keywords>

For through this process, bodies <note>In alchemy, "bodies" usually refers to the seven base metals: gold, silver, copper, iron, tin, lead, and mercury.</note> are purified. However, they are purified better by means of a small cake <note>original: "pastillum"; likely a pellet or pressed mass of material</note>, the method of which is the same as the method of purification by the descensory <note>A "descensory" is a furnace or vessel used for "distillation by descent," where the substance is heated from above so the liquid flows downward into a receiver.</note>. And therefore, by using the cake, we are excused from using the descensory. For it holds back the dross <note>original: "feces"; the impurities or solid residue left after a process</note> of the bodies just like the descensory, and even better; therefore, we shall describe its method. We say, then, that we take the body which is intended to be cleansed, and we reduce it into very small grains, or filings, or into a lime <note>original: "calcem"; a powder produced by heating a substance to high temperatures, known as calcination</note> (which is more perfect). To this, we mix some other lime that is not intended to be melted, and afterward, we melt the mixture. We find that through this, bodies are cleansed by many repetitions—not with the absolute perfection of cleansing that we know exists, but with a useful cleansing, so that once the medicine of perfection <note>A reference to the "Elixir" or "Philosopher's Stone" which transforms base metals into gold.</note> is received, the bodies may be transformed by it more effectively and perfectly. For this is a preparation that precedes it, and we shall sufficiently describe every preparation to you in the following sections. But now, let us set down the description of the Descensory.

it is necessary to arrive at a determined separation of all elements. Indeed, by that method which is done by descent <note>original: "per descensum"; a method where the substance is heated from above so the essence flows downward into a receiver</note>, the oil of any thing can be determinately reached, namely of all vegetables and things similar to them. By that method which is done through a filter, the clarity of any liquid can be achieved. Moreover, all these things are manifest and known even to those with little or no knowledge; but he who is ignorant of these things knows nothing of this mastery <note>The "Magistery" or the Great Work of alchemy</note>. Therefore, let him practice, and he will easily investigate them.

**The arrangement, therefore, of that distillation which is done by ashes**, is that a strong earthen pot be taken and fitted into a furnace, similar to the aforementioned sublimation furnace, with the same distance from the furnace ledges, by the same investigation, and with similar handles. Upon its bottom, sifted ashes should be placed to the thickness of a finger, and upon those ashes the vessel of distillation is placed, and it is covered around its circumference with the same ashes up to near the neck of the alembic <note>The "alembic" is the cap or head of the distilling apparatus</note>; afterward, the substance intended to be distilled in this manner is poured into it.

Finally, let it be covered with the alembic, whose neck should receive the neck of the lower cucurbit <note>The "cucurbit" is the bottom gourd-shaped vessel containing the raw material</note> within itself, up to the curve of the channel of the alembic itself, so that the substance to be distilled finds no way of escape. And afterward, let the alembic be luted <note>sealed with "lute," a specialized alchemical paste or clay</note> to its cucurbit, and their joint made firm, and fire be placed beneath until it distills. The alembic and its cucurbit should be made of glass. Let the fire be increased according to the requirements of the distillation, until it is seen that everything to be distilled has been distilled with a great expression of fire. **The arrangement of the second intention of distillation**, which is done by water <note>A "water bath" or Bain-marie</note>, is similar to this in the vessel and alembic. It differs from it, however, in this: that in this method an iron or bronze pot is taken and fitted to the furnace, as has been said. Afterward, a layer of grass, or wool, or a similar thing is constructed upon the bottom of that pot to the thickness of three fingers, lest the cucurbit be broken.

<note>The author is describing a "Balneum Mariae" or water bath setup.</note> ...and let the gourd <term>cucurbita; the lower bulb of a still</term> be covered all around with the same grasses or similar materials, up to near the neck of the helm <term>alembicus; the cooling cap of the still</term>. Above these bundles, let fine twigs be scattered, and upon the twigs let heavy stones be placed. By their weight, these stones should press down the gourd, the helm, and the bundles themselves, holding them firmly and steadily against the bottom of the pot, lest they float up due to the water. Such lifting would cause the breaking of the vessel and the loss of the substance to be distilled. Afterward, let water be poured over the bundles until the pot is full, and let fire be applied beneath until the whole is distilled.

The arrangement of that distillation which is done by descent <note>original: "per descensum"; a method where heat is applied from above to force oils or essences downward into a receiver</note> is as follows: let a glass descensory be made with its cover, and let both be sealed with clay <note>original: "lutetur"; referring to 'lute,' a paste used to make joints airtight</note>, and let that which one intends to distill be placed inside. Let a fire be built over the head of it, and its distillation will descend.

<margin>1</margin> The arrangement of that distillation which is done by filter <note>original: "per filtrum"; a capillary action method using strips of cloth</note> is as follows: let the liquid to be distilled be placed in a stone basin. Let the wider part of a well-washed and moistened filter be placed into said liquid all the way to the bottom of the basin. Let the narrower part of it hang outside from the mouth of the basin. Under the end of that filter, let a vessel be placed to receive the distillation. When the filter begins to distill, the water with which it was moistened will distill first. When that ceases, the liquid to be distilled follows. If it is not yet clear, let it be returned to the basin as many times as necessary until it distills perfectly clear. Because all these things are easy, they do not require great proof; therefore, we have remained silent regarding their demonstration. The reason for its invention <note>The "invention" refers to the process of calcination, or heating a substance to reduce it to powder.</note> is so that the burning sulfurous quality, which corrupts and defiles, may be destroyed by fire. It is varied, however, <gloss>1.</gloss> according to the diversity of the things to be calcined. Therefore, the bodies are calcined, and <gloss>2.</gloss> the spirits <note>In alchemy, "spirits" usually refer to volatile substances like mercury, sulfur, or arsenic.</note> are calcined, and <gloss>3.</gloss> other things foreign to their nature, though with a different intention. Because there are imperfect bodies of two types: namely the Hard, such as Venus <note>Copper</note> and Mars <note>Iron</note>, and the Soft, such as Jupiter <note>Tin</note> and Saturn <note>Lead</note>, all of which are calcined. It was necessary for them to be calcined with different intentions,

In the general sense, however, they are calcined <note>Calcination: the process of heating a substance to high temperatures in air to cause thermal decomposition or oxidation, often reducing it to a powder.</note> with one intention. # FOURTH PART OF THE FIRST BOOK

<page-num>86</page-num>

The moderation of the fire requires care, and the succession of its increase must be gradual and cautious, until the metal is firmly fixed in its <term>calx</term> <note>A powder produced by heating a metal; the oxide form</note>, lest it easily return to a bodily state. This is done so that a greater fire may be administered to it for the perfection of its calx. Similarly, one must take care not to err regarding <term>Jupiter</term> <note>The alchemical name for Tin</note>, because of the difficulty of its reduction. For if one attempts to reduce its calx, it may happen that it does not return to metal, but instead remains in its prior state or is turned into glass, leading the worker to believe its reduction is impossible. We say, indeed, that if one does not apply a great fire in the reduction of <term>Jupiter</term>, it will not reduce. Yet, if the fire is too great, it does not necessarily follow that it will reduce; rather, it is possible for it to turn into glass <note>Vitrification: the process of turning into a glass-like substance through high heat</note>. This occurs because <term>Jupiter</term> <margin>cc am ta</margin> <note>Marginalia likely referring to specific chemical properties or cross-references</note> has a fugitive substance of <term>quicksilver</term> <note>Mercury</note> enclosed deep within its nature. If this substance remains too long in the fire, it will flee, leaving the body deprived of its moisture, so that it is found to be more fit for turning into glass than for the melting of a metallic body. For everything deprived of its own moisture yields no melting except that which leads to glass. Therefore, it is necessary that one hastens to reduce it quickly with a very swift impulse of fire. Otherwise, it is not reduced. Let the worker practice this, and he shall know.

The <gloss>second</gloss> method of calcination for these two bodies, <gloss>Saturn and Jupiter</gloss> <note>Lead and Tin</note>, which is perfected by the sharpness of salt, is as follows: a quantity of salt should be thrown upon their surface, bit by bit, very often while they are in a state of fusion. They should be mixed with much agitation using an iron rod over the fire, until through the mixing of the salt they are turned into ash. Afterward, their calx is perfected through the same method of completion, with its proper considerations. But there is also a difference in the calcination of these two bodies. For <term>Lead</term> <margin>85, 6</margin> is converted to powder more easily than <term>Jupiter</term> during the first labors of calcination; however, its calx is not perfected more easily than that of <term>Jupiter</term>. The cause of this depends on the fact that <term>Saturn</term> <note>Lead</note> <margin>iron [rod]. boiling. see page 57, 14.</margin> possesses a more fixed moisture than <term>Jupiter</term>.

<header>GEBER’S SUMMARY OF PERFECTION.</header>

# GEBER’S SUMMARY OF PERFECTION.

<margin>Regarding Venus <note>In alchemical terminology, Venus refers to Copper.</note></margin>
Regarding Venus and Mars <note>Mars refers to Iron.</note>, the method is the same, yet different from the first metals mentioned, and this is due to the difficulty of their melting. The method is that they should be placed as thin plates into a strong fire, the intention of which is not to melt them. Because of the great amount of earthiness in them, and the great measure of burning and volatile sulfurousness, they are easily brought into a lime <note>calx: a powder produced by heating a metal or mineral.</note> by this method. And this is because, from the great earthiness mixed into the substance of the quicksilver <note>mercury</note>, the proper continuity of the quicksilver is disturbed. Therefore, porosity is caused within them, through which the passing sulfurousness can fly away; and the fire, reaching it for that reason, can burn it and lift it up. Through this, therefore, it is left behind, the parts become thinner, and are converted into ash through the discontinuity of that thinness. The clear experience of this is seen because when a plate of Venus is exposed to fire, it will give off a sulfurous flame and <margin>cc <note>Likely a shorthand for "calcination" or "concerning."</note></margin> will cause a crushable scale to form on its surface. This happens because it is necessary for the easier combustion of sulfur to occur from the parts nearest the surface. ¶ Now, the design of the furnace for this calcination is the same as the design for the distillation furnace, except that it ought to have only one large hole above its head, from which it may freely release the fumes. And the placement of the materials to be calcined should be in the middle of the furnace, so that they may receive the fire freely all around. Let its vessel be made of earth <note>clay or ceramic</note>, fashioned in the shape of a side-dish.

The method for the calcination of spirits <note>Volatile substances like sulfur, arsenic, or mercury.</note> is that, as they approach fixation <note>The process of making a volatile substance stable in fire.</note>, fire should be administered successively and little by little, increasing it so that they do not flee, until it happens that they can tolerate the greatest fire. And their vessel should be round, closed on all sides, and <margin>cc</margin> the furnace the same as the one last mentioned.

With a similar furnace, and a similar vessel, every thing is likewise calcined. We are, however, spared from greater labor than that which is applied in the prevention of flight <gloss>from fleeing <note>original: "a fuga"</note></gloss>. Since other things do not
