And therefore we prove by this, that all <term>calcined</term> <note>substances reduced to powder by heat</note> materials approach the nature of salts or alums; it is therefore necessary that they accompany one another in their properties. Truly, the method of dissolution is twofold: namely, by warm manure <note>a common alchemical method for providing gentle, steady heat</note>, and by boiling water. Of these, there is one intention and one effect. The method by manure, therefore, is that the calcined material be placed in a glass <term>ampoule</term> <note>a sealed flask</note>, and over it be poured a quantity of distilled vinegar, or something similar, double its weight. Let its head be stopped up well, so that it does not breathe, and let it be left to dissolve under warm manure; and afterwards, let the dissolved material be removed by <term>filtration</term> <note>original: "distillationem filtri," referring to the wicking or straining of liquid</note>.

of that coagulation <note>The process of turning liquid mercury into a solid or "fixed" state</note>. Indeed, some have thought the method of that coagulation would be through its preservation in a tempered heat of fire. When they thought they had coagulated it, they found that after its removal from the fire, it flowed just as before. Because of this, they were led into stupor and wonder, vehemently arguing that this goal could not be reached. Others, however, assuming by necessity from natural principles that any moisture is converted into dryness by the heat of fire, attempted with persistent urgency to continue its preservation in the fire; and through this continuation, they reached a point where some of them converted the stone <note>The "Philosopher's Stone" or the sought-after alchemical result</note> into white, others into red, and others into yellow, yet it had neither fusion nor the power of penetration <note>original: "ingressio" — the ability of the alchemical tincture to permeate and transform a base metal</note>. They could not determine the cause of these variations, and therefore they cast it aside. Others attempted to coagulate it with medicines <note>In alchemy, "medicines" refer to chemical reagents or elixirs used to treat and transform metals</note>, and they did not succeed, but a deception befell them, either because they did not achieve coagulation, or because it was imperceptibly thinned away, or because their coagulation did not take the form of any body. They remained ignorant of the cause of this diversity. Others, composing artificial medicines, coagulated it by means of projection <note>The act of throwing the alchemical powder or elixir onto molten metal to transform it</note>; but their coagulation was not useful, since they converted it into an imperfect body. Likewise, they were unable to see the cause of this. It is therefore expedient for us to relate the causes of these things, so that the craftsman may reach the mastery of that coagulation. As has already been sufficiently related by us, the substance of quicksilver <note>original: "argenti uiui" — mercury</note> is uniform. Therefore, it is not possible in a short space of time to remove its wateriness through its preservation in the fire. Excessive haste, therefore, was the cause of the first error. Since it is of a subtle substance, it retreats from the fire. An excess of fire, therefore, is the cause of the error of those from whom it flees. It mixes easily with sulfur, arsenic, and marcasite <note>A common iron sulfide mineral often mistaken for gold or silver ore</note>, because of their shared

### Concerning Fixation, its causes, and the various ways of fixing bodies and spirits. Chapter 54.

**F**ixation <note>original: "Fixio"; the process of making a volatile substance stable in fire</note> is truly the suitable adaptation of a thing fleeing from fire so that it may endure the same. Now, the reason for the invention of this fixation is so that every dye <note>original: "tinctura"; the transformative essence or color-changing agent</note> and every alteration may be made permanent in the thing altered, and not be changed. Furthermore, this process is diversified according to the multiplicity of things to be fixed, which are, namely, certain bodies <note>original: "corpora"; in alchemy, "bodies" usually refers to the solid metals, while "spirits" refers to volatile substances like mercury or sulfur</note>

<summary>This page introduces the alchemical concept of fixation, defining it as the process of stabilizing volatile substances so they can withstand heat without evaporating. It explains that fixation is necessary to ensure that any chemical or spiritual transformation remains permanent.</summary>
<keywords>fixation, alchemy, tincture, transformation, alchemical vessels, stability, chemical alteration</keywords>

# FOURTH PART OF THE FIRST BOOK

as we have related in our own discourse, namely on <gloss>page 82</gloss> in the chapter on calcination <note>The process of heating a substance to high temperatures to achieve decomposition or change in texture</note>.
Indeed, sulfur and arsenic are fixed <note>term: fixation (the process of making a volatile substance stable in fire so it no longer evaporates)</note> in two ways: namely, by the repetition of sublimation <note>term: sublimation (heating a solid until it turns to vapor and then solidifies again on a cool surface)</note> over them in the aludel vessel <note>term: aludel (a pear-shaped earthenware bottle used in sublimation)</note>, until they remain stable. Therefore, the goal for speeding up their fixation is that you should devise a way to achieve many repeated sublimations in a short time. This is done using two aludels with their double covers in this specific order: you must never cease the act of sublimation until they have become fixed. Therefore, when they have ascended into one aludel, let them be cast into the other, and let it be done thus by alternating turns. And never allow them to sit idle, adhering to the joints of the aludels, but let them be in a continuous elevation by fire until their rising ceases. For the more you can multiply the repetitions of sublimation in a shorter time, the more quickly and effectively the time required for the fixation itself will be shortened.

<summary>This page provides practical instructions for the chemical "fixation" of sulfur and arsenic through continuous, rapid cycles of sublimation using multiple aludel vessels. The author emphasizes that constant heat and movement are essential to preventing the materials from settling before they are fully stabilized.</summary>

Truly, the fixation of marcasite, magnesia, and tutty <note>zinc oxide or a similar mineral crust</note> is such that when, after their first sublimation, we have acquired what we desire from them and cast away their dregs, we should repeat the sublimation upon them. This is done by repeatedly returning that which ascended to the top back to that which remained at the bottom for each of them, until they are fixed. ### On Waxing. Chapter 55.

<margin>Book 2, Chapter 17</margin>
**W**axing <term>Ceration: the process of softening a hard substance until it has a wax-like consistency, making it easier to melt</term> is the softening of a hard, non-fusible thing to the point of liquefaction. From this, it is clear that the reason for this invention was so that a substance—which could not penetrate a body to alter it because it lacked the ability to melt—might be softened so that it flows and gains entry. Therefore, some have thought that waxing ought to be performed with liquid oils and waters. But that is erroneous, and entirely removed from the principles of this natural mastery, and rejected by the manifest works of nature. For we do not see that nature has placed a quickly-ending humidity in metallic bodies for the necessity of their melting and softening, but rather a long-lasting one. For if nature had placed such a temporary humidity in them, it would necessarily follow that by a single firing, the bodies would happen to be totally deprived of humidity. Wherefore it would follow that after one firing, any body could neither be hammered nor melted. For this reason, imitating the works of nature as much as we can, it is necessary for us to follow nature’s method in waxing. Nature herself waxes at the root of the waxing of fusible things with a humidity that survives all other humidities while awaiting the heat of the fire. Therefore, it is also necessary for us to wax with a similar humidity. In no...

<meta>The text continues on the next page with the word "however."</meta>

<summary>This page describes the fixation of minerals like marcasite and tutty through repeated sublimation and introduces 'ceration' (waxing) as a method to soften hard substances using heat-resistant humidity rather than simple oils.</summary>
<keywords>ceration, fixation, sublimation, Geber, marcasite, magnesia, tutty, alchemy, metallurgy, humidity</keywords>

<page-num>100</page-num>
<header>FOURTH PART OF THE FIRST BOOK</header>

### FOURTH PART OF THE FIRST BOOK

However, this <term>waxy humidity</term><note>original: "humiditas ceratiua"; a quality in alchemy that allows a substance to flow like wax when heated without evaporating</note> is found better, more feasibly, and more closely in these things—namely in sulfur and arsenic—but most closely and best of all in <term>quicksilver</term><note>original: "argento uiuo"; mercury</note>. We see that the humidity of these substances does not leave their earthiness because of the strong union they achieved in nature's own work of mixing. In all other things possessing humidity, however, you will find by experiment that the moisture is separated from its earthy substance during dissolution, and after that separation, the substance is deprived of all humidity. But in the aforementioned <term>spirits</term><note>volatile substances like sulfur, arsenic, and mercury that can "fly" or evaporate</note>, this does not happen. Therefore, there is no other means by which we can be excused from using them in the process of <term>ceration</term>. The method of **Ceration** <note>The process of softening a hard substance to a wax-like consistency</note> is performed through them, by repeating their <term>sublimation</term><note>The process of heating a substance until it turns to vapor and then solidifies again</note> over the matter to be softened so many times until, remaining within it with their humidity, they provide a good melting. This, however, will not happen before their perfect cleansing from every corrupting thing. But it seems better to me that their oils should first be fixed by <term>oil of tartar</term><note>A concentrated solution of potassium carbonate, used as a chemical reagent</note>, and with those, every suitable and necessary ceration for this art should be performed.

# THE SUM OF PERFECTION BY GEBER
## *The Second Book concerning Alchemy.* <note>original: "περὶ χυμείας" (peri chymeias) - Greek for "concerning the pouring or chemistry"</note>

### Preface. Dividing this Second book into three parts. Chapter 56.

<margin>chapter 67. page 120.</margin>
<margin>page 150.</margin>
**H**aving therefore delivered the discussion of the principles of this mastery, it is necessary, according to our promise, to pursue the intent of this art completely, in language appropriate to it. And this consists, namely, of the consideration of every thing through which the perfection of this work may be more clearly shown. And the consideration of the necessity of the perfection of the medicine—namely, that it be discussed from what thing that medicine may be better or more closely extracted, for the perfection of every imperfect thing. And likewise the consideration of the technical skills <note>original: "artificiorum"</note> through which we may know whether the perfection is complete. These three things having been delivered, the entire knowledge of perfection will have been handed down, according to the requirements of our art.

### On the nature of Sulfur and Arsenic. Chapter 58.

<margin>Spirits are the principles of bodies.</margin>
<margin>What is arsenic?</margin>
<margin>which</margin>
**T**herefore, asserting first the nature of the spirits, which are the principles of those very bodies—namely Sulfur, and its companion <note>original: "eius compar"; likely referring to Arsenic, often paired with sulfur in alchemical theory</note>, and quicksilver <note>mercury</note>. We say that Sulfur and arsenic are the "fatness of the earth," as has been recounted by us. You may draw out the experience and manifest proof of this through its easy inflammation and its easy liquefaction by heat. For nothing is inflamed unless it is oily in nature, nor does it melt easily by heat unless it possesses that same nature. Sulfur, therefore, and its companion, possess a cause of corruption: an inflammable substance and an earthy dross. Yet they also possess a perfecting cause, which lies midway between this and that. Therefore, the cause of corruption in it is its "earthiness," <term>terreitas</term> <note>the quality of being like earth; solid, dry, and non-metallic residue</note> because it possesses neither the ability to flow nor to penetrate. Similarly, it is an inflammable substance because it neither stands firm nor causes other things to stand, and because it produces blackness in every kind of thing. The cause of perfection in them, then, is their moderate substance; because of its earthiness, it is not disturbed from penetrating, which is perfected through good melting. And because of its subtlety, its impression is not easily removed by flight <note>evaporation or sublimation</note>. However, their moderate substance is not a cause of perfection for bodies or for quicksilver unless it is fixed. For when it is not fixed, although its impression may not be removed easily,

However, some have thought that dividing it is impossible because of its strong mixture. And those people are indeed contradicted by their own manifest works. For they have <term>calcined</term> <note>The process of heating a substance to high temperatures in air to achieve oxidation or reduction to powder.</note> the <term>sulfur</term> itself, though not much, which resulted in no fusion or inflammation. ## On the nature of Marcasite, Magnesia, and Tutty. Chapter 60.

**W**e must necessarily provide a tradition regarding the other spirits, which are namely Marcasite <note>iron pyrites</note>, Magnesia <note>likely manganese ore or a similar dark mineral</note>, and Tutty <note>zinc oxide</note>, which make a great impression upon bodies. And therefore, let us say what they are, along with their proofs, in this chapter. We say, therefore, that Marcasite has a twofold substance in its creation: namely, that of mortified quicksilver approaching fixation, and of burning sulfur. Moreover, we have found by manifest experience that it possesses sulfurousness. For when it is sublimated, a manifest, burning sulfurous substance emanates from it. And even without sublimation, its sulfurousness is similarly perceived.

<summary>This page describes the chemical purification of mercury using vinegar and heat to remove impurities, followed by an introduction to the 'spirits' of marcasite, magnesia, and tutty. Geber argues that alchemical perfection is achieved through 'natural artifice' rather than leaving nature to its own devices.</summary>

sulphurous nature. For if it is placed in a fire to be ignited, it does not undergo ignition until it is inflamed and burns by the inflammation of its sulphur. Furthermore, it is shown to possess the substance of <term>quicksilver</term><note>original: "argenti uiui"; mercury</note> in a way that can be perceived by the senses. For it provides a whiteness to <term>Venus</term><note>The alchemical name for Copper</note> like that of pure silver, just as quicksilver itself does; and we see it provide a heavenly <note>likely sky-blue or cerulean</note> color during its sublimation, and possess a clear metallic luster. These things make the craftsman certain that it contains these substances in its very root. <term>Magnesia</term><note>In this context, likely a manganese ore or a specific pyritic mineral rather than modern magnesium</note>, however, contains a more turbid sulphur and a more earthy and dross-filled quicksilver; its sulphur is likewise more fixed and less flammable. You can clearly prove these things through the same experiments, and see that it approaches more closely to the nature of <term>Mars</term><note>The alchemical name for Iron</note>. <term>Tutia</term><note>Zinc oxide, often collected as "furnace calamine" from the flues of smelting furnaces</note>, truly, is the smoke of those white bodies, and this is weighed by a clear proof. For when the smoke from a mixture of <term>Jupiter</term><note>The alchemical name for Tin</note> and Venus is cast off and adheres to the rafters of the smiths' workshops, it makes the same impression as Tutia. And because metallic smoke does not return <note>to a solid state easily</note>, neither does Tutia itself without the admixture of some body. Therefore, since it is the smoke of white bodies, it does not turn white bodies yellow, but rather red ones. For <term>yellowness</term><note>original: "citrinitas"</note> is nothing other than a determined proportion of red and white. Therefore, because of its subtlety, it penetrates more deeply into the body. And thus it alters more than its own body does, and adheres more firmly during the <term>assay</term><note>original: "examine"; the test of purity</note> with little effort, as has already been told to you. Whatever things are altered, therefore, must necessarily be altered through the power of quicksilver, or of sulphur, or things similar to these, since these alone share a nature with the bodies themselves.

### On the nature of the Sun, or Gold. Chapter LXI.

**N**ow, having expanded our discourse, let us describe the intimate essence of the bodies. # THE SUM OF PERFECTION BY GEBER

<note>Geber is the Latinized name of Jabir ibn Hayyan, the most celebrated alchemist of the Islamic Golden Age. This text, the *Summa Perfectionis*, was the most influential alchemical work in medieval Europe.</note>

When mixed, <term>tin</term> <note>original: *stannum*</note> follows from it. And you shall find the proof of these things through preparation. For if you calcine <note>To "calcine" means to heat a substance to a high temperature in air to reduce it to a powder or "calx."</note> <term>tin</term>, you will perceive from it the stench of <term>sulfur</term> <note>original: *sulphur*</note>: which is a sign of <term>sulfur</term> that is not fixed <note>A "fixed" substance is one that remains stable and does not evaporate or change when exposed to fire.</note>. And because it does not produce a flame, do not think it is fixed. For it is not <margin>.</margin> because of fixation, but because of the abundance of <term>quicksilver</term> <note>original: *argentum vivum*, literally "living silver" or mercury</note> in the mixture—which saves it from burning—that it does not produce a flame. Therefore, a twofold <term>sulfurousness</term> is proven in <term>tin</term>; likewise, a twofold substance of <term>quicksilver</term>. One <term>sulfurousness</term> is indeed that which is less fixed, because <margin>.. <insert>the joy of sulfurousness. The first of these is proven</insert></margin> when it is calcined, it stinks like <term>sulfur</term>. By the first, the experience of the mixture is proven. The other is proven by the continuation of it in its powder <note>original: *calce*</note> over the fire, which it possesses because, being more fixed, it does not stink. Truly, a twofold substance of <term>quicksilver</term> is proven in it, of which one is not fixed and the other is fixed: because before its calcination it "shrieks" <note>This refers to the "cry of tin," a crackling sound made when bending a bar of tin, caused by the internal crystals rubbing against each other.</note>, but after its triple calcination it does not shriek; this is because the substance of its fugitive <term>quicksilver</term>, which causes the shrieking, has flown away. That <margin>:.</margin> the fugitive substance of <term>quicksilver</term> is the cause of the shrieking is proven by the washing of <term>lead</term> <note>original: *plumbum*</note> with <term>quicksilver</term>. For if <term>lead</term> is washed with <term>quicksilver</term>, and after its washing you melt it with fire (not exceeding the fire of its melting), there will remain with it a part of the <term>quicksilver</term> which <margin>...</margin> brings the shriek to the <term>lead</term> and converts it into <term>tin</term>. Conversely, you can observe this through the mutation of <term>tin</term> into <term>lead</term>. For by the multiple repetition of calcination upon it, and by the administration of a fire more suitable for its reduction, it is converted into <term>lead</term>—especially when it is calcined with a great fire through the <margin>:.</margin> removal of its dross <note>original: *scoriae*</note>. And you will be able to be certain of the diversities of these substances through the ingenious methods of their preservation in proper instruments, and by the manner of the fire dividing them: to which we have arrived with persistence, and we have seen with our own certainty that we have judged the truth through it. <margin>.</margin>

## On the nature of Saturn, or Lead. Chapter LXVI.

**I**t remains for us, therefore, to set down a description of Saturn <note>The alchemical name for Lead.</note>. And we say that it does not differ from Jupiter <note>The alchemical name for Tin.</note> after the repetition of calcination and the reduction to its nature, except that it has a more impure substance mixed from two coarser substances: namely, sulfur and quicksilver <note>original: "argento uiuo"</note>. And that the sulfur burning within it is more adhesive to the substance of its quicksilver, and that it has more of the substance of fixed sulfur <margin>combustible p. 118. 4.</margin> in its composition than Jupiter does. And we shall bring forward proofs of these things through manifest experiences. That it is of greater earthy dross <note>original: "feculentiæ"</note> than Jupiter is manifested by sight, and by its washing from quicksilver; in this, that more dross emanates from it through washing than from Jupiter, and that <margin>⸪</margin> it undergoes the first degree of calcination more easily than Jupiter, <margin>much earthiness</margin> which is a sign of much earthiness. For we find bodies of more earthiness are of easier calcination, and those of little earthiness are of more difficult calcination. And the proof of this is the most difficult [calcination] of Gold <note>original: "Solis," literally "of the Sun."</note>.

<summary>Geber explains that Lead (Saturn) is composed of coarser sulfur and quicksilver than Tin (Jupiter), noting that its high "earthiness" makes it easier to calcine. He emphasizes that true alchemical knowledge comes from practical experimentation rather than mere conjecture.</summary>

## On the Impurity of Lead

### Observations on Quantity

The quantity <note>original: "quantitas"</note> of the sulfurous element brought <note>original: "adductum"</note> into its <note>original: "suis"</note> parts is what determines the behavior of this <note>original: "huius"</note> metal. Therefore, because in Jupiter <note>the alchemical name for tin</note> there is a great quantity of quicksilver that is not truly fixed, there remains in it a great speed of liquefaction, and it is not easily removed from it. Indeed, the cause of softness is twofold: namely, quicksilver and combustible sulfur. Therefore, because the burning sulfurousness is removed <margin>namely,</margin> from Jupiter more easily than from Saturn <note>the alchemical name for lead</note>, once one of the causes of softness is removed from it, it must necessarily be hardened when calcined <note>reduced to powder by heat</note>. 

But Saturn, because it has the causes of both types of softness strongly joined <margin>.. in</margin> together, is not easily hardened. There is, however, a difference between softness caused by quicksilver and softness caused by sulfur: for softness from sulfur is yielding <note>compressible or brittle</note>, while softness from quicksilver is extensible <note>malleable or ductile</note>. And this is necessarily proven by what we see: that bodies <margin>.. <unclear>cooked by art</unclear></margin> with much quicksilver have great extension, while bodies with little have little. And therefore Jupiter is extended more easily and finely than <margin>.:</margin> Saturn, Saturn more easily than Venus <note>copper</note>, Venus more than Mars <note>iron</note>, the Moon <note>silver</note> more finely than Jupiter, and the Sun <note>gold</note> more finely than the Moon. 

It is clear, therefore, that the cause of hardening is fixed quicksilver or fixed sulfur. The cause of softness is the opposite. Indeed, the cause <margin>... see chapter 5, verse 25. And you clearly see the experience of this thing by the projection of arsenic upon bodies that are difficult to <margin>see page 118, line 26.</margin> melt: for it makes them easy to melt, and without ignition. Likewise, quicksilver is also a cause of easy melting. But the cause <margin>*</margin> of melting with ignition <note>melting only at high heat</note> is fixed quicksilver. Therefore, the cause of the impediment of any melting is fixed sulfur. 

By this, therefore, you will be able to elicit a very great secret: That since bodies of the greatest quantity of quicksilver are found to be of the greatest perfection, it is necessary that bodies of a larger <margin>... greater</margin> quantity of quicksilver, though diminished from perfection, approach more closely to the perfect state. <margin>.</margin>

<summary>Geber explains the physical properties of metals—malleability and melting points—as being determined by the proportions and "fixity" of mercury and sulfur. He concludes that metals with higher mercury content are closer to the "perfection" of gold and silver.</summary>

# THE FIRST PART OF THE SECOND BOOK

Thus, it happens that many bodies containing a high degree of sulfur are very prone to <gloss>of</gloss> corruption. For this reason, it is clear from what has already been said that Jupiter approaches most closely to perfection, since it participates more in perfection; Saturn, however, participates less; Venus even less; and Mars least of all, according to that upon which perfection depends. On the other hand, the situation is different regarding the medicine that completes, supplies what is lacking, and more perfectly thins the thickness of the bodies to their depths, while cloaking their darkness under the substance of a glittering brightness. For by this standard, Venus is the most capable of being perfected, Mars less so, Jupiter even less, and Saturn least of all.

Therefore, we have exercised ourselves with every kind of ingenuity we could muster, so that through these medicines we might transform each of the imperfect bodies, by a firm change, into a perfect lunar <note>Silver</note> and solar <note>Gold</note> body. For this reason, it happened that different medicines had to be created from the different intentions of the things to be altered. Since, therefore, there are two types of metals undergoing alteration—namely, quicksilver <note>original: "argentum uiuum"</note>, which is coagulable by perfection, and those bodies diminished from perfection—these are again manifold: some indeed are hard and ignoble, while others are soft and not ignoble. The hard and ignoble ones are **Mars** <note>Iron</note> and **Venus** <note>Copper</note>. The soft ones, however, are **Jupiter** <note>Tin</note> and **Saturn** <note>Lead</note>.

After this, a sufficient and suitable narration of all the medicines must be added.

Similarly, by no method of ingenious preparation were we able to brighten the bodies <note>The "bodies" refer to the base metals like lead or copper</note> with the full measure of their clear brilliance, without it happening that they were instead entirely stained and blackened. Because of this, we were likewise struck with amazement and for a long space of time we lay hidden under the shadow of despair. 

<margin>cc</margin> Yet returning to ourselves, and tormenting ourselves with the immense afflictions of deep thought and meditation, we observed that the diminished bodies <note>Metals considered "imperfect" or "diminished" compared to gold</note> exist as foul in the depths of their nature, and nothing bright is found within them, since by nature it is not there. For one does not find in a thing that which is not in it. 

<margin>cc +</margin> Since, therefore, nothing of perfection is found in them, it necessarily follows that nothing superfluous is found in them either—neither in the separation of their diverse substances nor in the depths of their nature. 

<margin>..</margin> And so, through this, we discovered that there was something diminished in them which must necessarily be completed by a material suitable to them, which fulfills what is lacking.

In alchemy, mercury was seen as the principle of metallic luster and fluidity.</note> and the improper thickening of the same. In this context, it refers to the "Philosopher's Stone" or an elixir capable of curing the "sickness" of base metals.</note> created from that very substance. For since this medicine is brought into being from quicksilver, by the benefit of its clarity and splendor, it conceals and covers their darkness by cloaking it; it draws out the splendor and converts it into brilliance. 

<margin>..</margin> For when the quicksilver prepared in the medicine has been cleansed by our **artifice** <note>The alchemical process or "Great Work"</note> and reduced into a most pure and shining substance, once cast upon the things diminished from perfection, it will illuminate them and perfect them by its fixation. 

<margin>...</margin> We shall, however, describe this medicine in its own discourse. It follows, therefore, from the preceding points, that there are necessarily two ways to find perfection: one through a material which separates the foul substance from the mixture, and the other through the medicine which [perfects] it.

### On the preparation of Saturn and Jupiter. Chapter 70.

**S**aturn <note>Lead</note> and Jupiter <note>Tin</note> are therefore prepared by manifold preparations according to the necessity of their approximation to greater perfection, namely by a common and a special mode of preparation. The common mode is through manifold steps of approximation toward perfection. For there is one step of approximation, namely brightness from a clean substance; another indeed is hardness, with the ignition of its fusion; and thirdly, fixation, through the removal of the fugitive substance <note>The "fugitive substance" usually refers to volatile components like mercury or sulfur that escape when heated</note>. They are therefore cleansed and made bright in three ways: either by cleansing agents, or by the method of <term>calcination</term> <note>The process of heating a substance to high temperatures in air to change its physical or chemical state, often reducing it to a powder</note> and reduction, or by solution. They are cleansed by purifying things in two ways: either reduced into lime <note>original: "calcem"; referring to the powdered oxide form</note>, or in the nature of their bodies. When reduced into lime, they are purified in this manner: either by salts, or by alums, or by <term>glass</term> <note>The author uses glass as a flux to help separate impurities</note>. And it is thus: when the body has been calcined, then let the water of alums or salts be poured over their lime, or let <term>glass</term> be mixed with it, and let it be reduced back into a body. Let this be repeated upon these bodies by alternating turns until they show themselves completely clean. For since salts and alums and glass melt with a different fusion than the bodies, they are therefore separated from them, and they carry the earthy substance away with them, leaving only the purity of the bodies. Similarly in the nature of the bodies, and

They are purified by the same method. And it is thus: let these two bodies <note>Likely referring to tin and lead, the "diminished" metals mentioned later</note> be filed very finely; after this, indeed, let them be managed with the same alums, salts, and glass, and afterwards be reduced back into a solid body. <margin>.</margin> And so let it be repeated by alternating turns, until they appear cleaner. And these bodies of both kinds are similarly purified by the repeated turn of calcination <term>calcination: the process of heating a substance to high temperatures in air to change its physical or chemical state</term> and likewise by reduction <term>reduction: the process of restoring a metallic substance from its oxide or calcined state back to its metallic form</term>, with a sufficient amount of its own fire, until they appear cleaner. <margin>..</margin> For by this method these diminished bodies are cleansed from perfection-hindering traits, namely from a double corrupting substance: one being inflammable and fleeting <note>original: "fugitiua," referring to volatile elements like sulfur that evaporate in heat</note>, the other being an earthy dross. And that is because fire raises and consumes every fleeting substance. And likewise, this same fire in the mode of reduction divides every substance of the earth <margin>::</margin> according to its proportion. And this proportion we have recorded in our other volume, which is entitled *On the Investigation of Perfection* <gloss>volume</gloss>: which precedes this book according to the proper order. For in that book, whatever we investigated, we wrote according to the reasoning of our mind; but here, what we have seen and touched <margin>...</margin> completely, we have determined according to the order of science. These are also similarly purified by the solution of their substance, the method of which we have already stated, and likewise by the reduction of that which shall have been dissolved from them. For that result is found to be cleaner and more perfect than by any other kind of preparation, <margin>::</margin> by this same mode of preparation. And no method is compared to this method, except that which is perfected through sublimation <term>sublimation: the process of heating a solid until it turns into a vapor and then solidifies again, used for purification</term>, and therefore it is equivalent to this. There is also a similar preparation for them: the hardening of their soft substance with the ignition of their fusion. And it is thus: that we should contrive to mix with them the substance of fixed quicksilver in their depths, <margin>>></margin> or of fixed sulfur, or of its companion, or with hard and <margin>30</margin> non-fusible things, such as the lime of Marcasite <note>A brittle mineral, iron pyrite</note> and of Tutia <note>Zinc oxide, often found as a crust in furnaces</note>. <margin>viti</margin> For these...

For indeed, they are united and befriended with those substances, and they harden them until they no longer melt before they are heated to redness. And this is indeed perfected by preserving them after the first degree of calcination <note>the process of reducing a metal to a powder through heat</note> in a fire proportional to them. And because a specific order in the methods of preparation is necessarily required, we shall therefore set down the complete order of them. First, therefore, let every fugitive and burning, corrupting substance be cleansed from them. After that, let the earthy superfluity be deleted. Following this, let them be dissolved and reduced, or let them be completely washed by a bath of mercury <note>original: "argenti uiui"</note>. And this order is useful and necessary.

However, the specific preparation of these bodies—first indeed of <gloss>Jupiter</gloss> <note>Tin</note>—is manifold. One way is indeed through calcination: and through this its substance is hardened more, which does not happen to Saturn <note>Lead</note>. Also through alums similarly: for these specifically harden <gloss>Jupiter</gloss> <note>Tin</note>. Another <margin>Preservation in the fire of calcination.</margin> way is through its preservation in the fire of its calcination. For through this it loses its "shriek" <note>The "cry of tin," a crackling sound made when bending the metal</note>, and similarly the brittleness of the bodies: which similarly does not happen to Saturn, because it has no shriek, nor does it break the bodies; and through the reiteration of calcination, it similarly loses its shriek by the sharpness of salt. But the specific preparation of <gloss>Saturn</gloss> <note>Lead</note> is secondary, namely through calcination by the sharpness of salt: for through this it is hardened, and it is specifically whitened by talc, and by Marcasite and Tutia <note>Zinc oxide or crude zinc ore</note>. <margin>All methods of preparation are in the book on the Invention of Perfection.</margin> Indeed, we have determined all the methods of preparation more completely in the book which is entitled *On the Invention of Perfection* <note>A reference to "De inventione perfectionis," often attributed to Geber</note>, since in this work we are abbreviating the summaries of them. ### On the preparation of Mars. Chapter LXXII.

The preparation of Mars <note>Iron</note> is likewise manifold. For some is completed through sublimation, while some is done without sublimation. That which is done through sublimation is with arsenic, the method of which is this: Let us devise a way to unite non-fixed <note>volatile</note> arsenic with it as deeply as we can, so that it may melt with it during fusion.
<margin>...</margin> Afterwards, let it be sublimed in a proper vessel of sublimation. And this preparation is found to be better and more perfect among the others. There is also another preparation of Mars itself through arsenic, sublimed from it many times, until some quantity of the arsenic remains. For if this is reduced, there will emerge a white, fusible, and clean prepared substance.
<margin>⁘</margin> There is likewise a third method of preparing the same by its fusion with lead and tutia.

# SECOND PART OF THE SECOND BOOK

From these things, indeed, flows that which is clean and white. But lest we seem to have spoken insufficiently, since we promised to determine the ingenious softening of hard bodies and the hardening of soft ones through the method of <term>calcination</term> <note>The process of heating a substance to high temperatures in air to change its physical or chemical state, often reducing it to a powder.</note>, let us therefore not omit it: but let us speak of the soft ones first, and the hard ones afterward. And it is this: let precipitated <term>quicksilver</term> <note>original: "argentum vivum"; mercury.</note> be dissolved, and let the calcined body—which is intended to be hardened—also be dissolved. Let both these solutions be mixed, and from them let the calcined body be mixed by turns, by grinding, and <term>imbibing</term> <note>The process of adding liquid to a dry substance until it is saturated.</note>, and calcining, and reducing, until it becomes hard and fusible under fire. This same thing is also completely accomplished with the <term>calx</term> <note>The powdery metallic oxide remaining after calcination.</note> of bodies, and with calcined, dissolved, and imbibed <term>tutty</term> <note>original: "tutia"; zinc oxide, often found as a crust in furnaces.</note> and <term>marcasite</term> <note>original: "marchesita"; iron pyrites or similar metallic sulfides.</note>. And indeed, the cleaner these things are, the more perfectly they transform. Hard bodies are similarly softened with a like ingenuity. And it is this: let them be joined and <term>sublimated</term> <note>The process of heating a substance so it turns to vapor and then solidifies again, used for purification.</note> with arsenic so many times, and after the sublimation of the arsenic, let them be roasted with the proportion of their own fire, the method of which we have narrated in the book of furnaces. And finally, let them be reduced with the expression of their fire, as stated in its own discourse, until they soften in fusion according to the requirement of the body's hardness. And indeed, all these alterations are of the first order, without which the <term>mastery</term> <note>original: "magisterium"; the completion of the alchemical Great Work.</note> is not perfected.

**L**et us therefore first bring forward a universal discourse concerning medicines, with their causes and manifest experiences. <margin>We have found</margin> We suggest, therefore, that unless every superfluity is removed from imperfect bodies—whether by the medicine or by the method of preparation—namely, so that all superfluous sulfurousness <note>original: "sulphureitas"; referring to the combustible, contaminating principle in alchemy</note> and every unclean earthiness is taken from them, they will not be purified. <margin>4</margin> If the medicine is to be made permanent with a firm <gloss>entering</gloss> alteration of the impression, its change is of no value if it does not remain, but rather the <gloss>entering</gloss> impression vanishes. <margin>5</margin> Furthermore, unless it brings the weights to perfection, it does not change according to the firm and true completion of nature, in which there is no deception through the error of belief <note>The author warns against "credulity," or believing a transformation has occurred based on surface appearance alone</note>. For it is drawn from those things which are most closely joined to bodies, and which, as altering agents, adhere to them amicably in their depths. And therefore, when searching among all other things, we have not found in our discovery any other thing more friendly to the natures of bodies than quicksilver <note>original: "argentum vivum"</note>; by bestowing our labor upon it, we find it to be the very thing.

substance, with the ingenuity of its own method, and the manner of its operation through a suitable fire. <meta>This page continues the discussion of alchemical theory, specifically categorizing the different grades of "medicines" or elixirs used for the transmutation of metals.</meta>

<page-num>134</page-num>
<header>SECOND PART OF THE SECOND BOOK</header>

# SECOND PART OF THE SECOND BOOK

Because the greater the weight of the bodies transformed, the greater the perfection discovered through investigation by the art. Therefore, this useful discourse on medicines is completed if we describe the differences between all medicines. We have indeed suggested that it is necessary for there to be a threefold difference in medicines. One is of the first order, another of the second, and another of the third. I call a medicine of the first order any preparation of minerals which, when cast upon bodies diminished from perfection <note>i.e., "base" metals like lead or copper</note>, impresses an alteration: yet this does not bring about a sufficient completion, for it happens that the altered metal changes and is corrupted again, with the impression of that medicine evaporating entirely. Such is every whitening sublimation of <term>Venus</term> <note>Copper</note> or <term>Mars</term> <note>Iron</note> which has not undergone fixation. Of this kind is every addition of the color of <term>Sol</term> <note>Gold</note> and <term>Luna</term> <note>Silver</note>, or those mixed with <term>Venus</term>, placed over a cementing furnace, such as <term>ziniar</term> <note>Verdigris or copper acetate</note> and the like. For this changes the metal with a transformation that does not last, but rather diminishes itself through exhalation.

We call a medicine of the second order every preparation which, when cast upon bodies diminished from perfection, alters them into some degree of completion, while leaving behind some characteristics of corruption entirely; such as the <term>calcination</term> <note>Reduction to powder by heat</note> of bodies, by which every fleeting element is deleted. To this class belongs the medicine that perpetually turns <term>Luna</term> yellow <note>giving silver the appearance of gold</note>, or perpetually whitens <term>Venus</term>, while leaving other corrupt qualities within them.

I call a medicine of the third order every preparation which, when it reaches the bodies, removes all corruption by its casting, and perfects them with every characteristic of completion. This medicine is one and alone. And therefore, by it, we are excused from the labors of inventing the ten medicines of the second order.

<margin>>> </margin>Therefore, the work of the first order is called the "lesser"; that of the second is called the "middle"; and finally, that of the third is called the "greater": and this is a sufficient distinction of all medicines.

### Concerning medicines of the first order for the whitening of Venus. Chapter 77.

**F**ollowing the promise of the established order (since one medicine is for the bodies <note>The "bodies" refer to the solid metals: Gold, Silver, Copper, Iron, Tin, and Lead.</note>, and another for Quicksilver <note>original: "Argenti uiui"; Mercury.</note>; and among the bodies, some medicines are of the first order, others of the second, and others of the third; and similarly for Quicksilver), let us describe the differences of all medicines. First, we shall treat those of the first order, then the second, and finally the third. We shall deliver this account with a complete discourse and in the proper order, dealing first with the bodies and afterwards with the medicines for Quicksilver. We say, therefore, that among the medicines of the first order for the bodies, some are for the hard bodies and others for the soft.
<margin>..</margin>Of the hard bodies, some medicines are for Venus <note>Venus is the alchemical name for Copper.</note>, others for Mars <note>Mars is the alchemical name for Iron.</note>, and others for the Moon <note>Luna or "the Moon" refers to Silver.</note>. For Venus and Mars, the medicine is the pure whitening of their substance; but for the Moon, it is a reddening <note>Turning silver into a gold-like color.</note> accompanied by a yellow brilliance.

# SECOND PART OF THE SECOND BOOK

The intention is for the whitening to be performed, and it is joined with the aforementioned solutions, and then they are coagulated. From this point, they are projected <note>The process of "projection" involves casting a small amount of a potent substance onto a larger mass of base metal to transform it.</note> upon that body, for it is whitened through this process. Alternatively: A quantity of Quicksilver <note>Mercury</note> is sublimated from its body in alternating turns, until a portion of the Quicksilver remains with it through complete ignition <note>Heating until glowing</note>. From this point, it is ground by frequently soaking it in distilled vinegar, so that it may be better mixed into its depths; from here it is roasted, and finally, in a similar manner, the Quicksilver is sublimated from it, and it is soaked and roasted again. And thus the work is repeated upon it so many times, until a great quantity of Quicksilver rests within it through complete ignition. This is indeed a good whitening of the first order. Alternatively, Quicksilver in its own nature should be sublimated over precipitated Quicksilver so many times until it is fixed within it and provides fusion. of its kind to adhere, with a firm entry <note>The "entry" refers to the alchemical medicine penetrating the body of the base metal</note>. We extract it, moreover, either from **Sulfur**, or from **Quicksilver** <note>original: "Argento uiuo"</note>, or from a mixture of both. But through Sulfur it is indeed more diminished, while through Quicksilver it is more perfect. It is extracted likewise through certain mineral things which are not of this genus, such as **Vitriol** <note>Iron or copper sulfates</note> and **copperas**, which is also called the "gum of copper" or the "drippings" of the same. Let us therefore set forth in the following sections all the methods for medicines which arise from Quicksilver. Then, indeed, those from Sulfur, or from a mixture of both. The method for that which is made through Quicksilver is as follows. Let the **precipitate** be taken, mortified <note>rendered chemically inactive or "killed"</note> and fixed through precipitation. Then, let it be placed in a furnace of great heat, according to the method for preserving calxes <note>powders produced by heating metals</note> which we have taught, until it grows red in the likeness of **cinnabar** <note>original: "usifur," a term for vermilion or red mercuric sulfide</note>. If, however, it does not turn red, take a portion of unmortified Quicksilver and repeat its sublimation with Sulfur. Yet let the Sulfur be cleansed of all impurity, and the Quicksilver likewise. And after you have repeated its sublimation twenty times over that precipitate, dissolve it with the sharpness of dissolving waters <note>likely mineral acids like nitric acid</note>, and calcine it again, and dissolve it again, until it is abundantly sufficient.

After this, dissolve a portion of **Luna** <note>term for Silver</note>, and when it has been dissolved, mix the solutions and coagulate them, and project <note>the act of "projection," casting the medicine onto molten metal</note> this upon the fusion of Silver. For it will turn it yellow with a very particular golden hue. If, however, the Quicksilver turned red in the precipitation, the aforementioned administration is sufficient for the projection of its perfection without the mixture of a dyeing agent. 

Indeed, it is turned red through Sulfur, but its reddening is difficult and immensely laborious. **Luna** <note>Silver</note> is likewise turned yellow with a solution of **Mars** <note>term for Iron</note>. However, the necessity of the work leads us to this: that we first calcine and fix it, which is a great deal of labor. From there, let it be administered by this same preparation...

# THE SUM OF PERFECTION BY GEBER. BOOK I. 141

It remains, therefore, to pass on to the medicines of the second order, with the true sufficiency required by this discourse, and with manifest proofs and true experience. Since, therefore, one medicine is for the changing of bodies, and another is for the mercury <note>original: "argenti uiui"</note> that is perfectly coagulable: let us first of all fully describe the medicines for the bodies. Thereafter, we shall proceed to the medicine for that same mercury which can be coagulated into a true <term>Solific</term> <note>Something that transforms a metal into Gold (Sol)</note> and <term>Lunific</term> <note>Something that transforms a metal into Silver (Luna)</note> substance. The medicine of the second order is that which completes an imperfect substance by a single difference of perfection. 

<margin>.</margin>However, since there are many causes of corruption in each of the imperfect bodies—<margin>..</margin>namely, in <term>Saturn</term> <note>The alchemical name for Lead</note> there is a volatile sulfurousness and the flight of mercury, through which corruption necessarily happens, as well as its earthiness. Let a medicine be made which either removes one of these entirely or decorates it by cloaking it, leaving behind only the other causes of imperfection. 

<margin>∴</margin>Because there is something unchangeable in bodies, which is innate in the very root of those bodies and cannot be removed by a medicine of the second order: therefore, every medicine that removes that from the mixture is called a medicine not of the second, but of the third and higher order. 

<margin>..</margin>And because we find that the superfluities of volatile substances are removed by the method of <term>calcination</term> <note>The process of heating a substance to high temperatures in air to change its physical or chemical state</note>, <margin>...</margin>and that non-innate earthiness is removed by the repetition of reduction: it was therefore necessary to find a medicine of the second order which might cloak what is innate, soften what is hard, and harden what is soft—specifically in hard <margin>⸪</margin>and soft bodies—and which might perfectly establish a true <term>Solific</term> or <term>Lunific</term> result, not a sophisticated <note>In this context, "sophistic" means fraudulent or deceptive alchemy</note> one, for the imperfect bodies. 

<margin>..</margin>Since it is evident that in soft bodies alone, the haste of liquefaction <note>The tendency to melt too quickly</note> cannot be removed by the ingenuity of the crafts of this work, nor can the impurity innate in the root of its principles be taken away, it necessarily follows that the medicine...

<summary>Geber defines the "second order" of alchemical medicine, which improves metals by cloaking their flaws or altering specific qualities like hardness, distinguishing it from the more powerful "third order" which changes the metal's root nature.</summary>

-># THE HEIGHT OF PERFECTION BY GEBER<-

<page-num>143</page-num>

In our previous discourses, we described a twofold distinction of the medicine <note>The "medicine" or "elixir" refers to the alchemical agent used to transform base metals into noble ones.</note>: one indeed for the bodies <note>The "bodies" refer to solid metals like lead, tin, or copper.</note>, and the other for mercury <note>original: "argenti uiui" (living silver). Geber views mercury as a unique substance requiring its own specific treatment.</note> that is truly capable of being coagulated. Therefore, let us first deliver a certain account of the medicine for the bodies, and then for mercury.

The material for this medicine is unique for each genus; and it is that which is already sufficiently known. Take that material, then, and if you wish to apply yourself to the Lunar <note>Silver-making</note> work according to the order promised to you, prepare it using the known methods of this mastery <note>term: "magisterium" (mastery/teaching) — the specialized alchemical process or "Great Work".</note>. The intent of these methods is that you separate the pure substance from it, and indeed fix <note>term: "figas" (fixation) — the process of making a volatile substance stable under heat.</note> one part, while leaving another part for waxing <note>term: "cerandum" (ceration) — softening a substance until it has a wax-like consistency.</note>. Thus, follow the entire mastery until you complete its attempted fusion.

If it should suddenly melt among hard substances, it is perfected; but among soft substances, it is the opposite. For this medicine, when projected <note>term: "proiecta" (projection) — the final step of casting the elixir onto a molten base metal.</note> upon any of the imperfect bodies, changes it into a perfect Lunar body <note>Silver</note>, provided that the known preparations precede this medicine. If they do not, it leaves the work diminished; yet it perfects it in the second degree of perfection only as much as depends on the administration of this rank of medicine. However, regarding the third rank, without any preceding administration, it perfects by projection alone.

The Solar <note>Gold-making</note> medicine of this second rank consists of the same material as each of the imperfect bodies, and shares the same regime of administration. In this, however, it has a difference: namely, in a greater refinement of the parts through specific methods of digestion, and in the addition of Sulfur <note>In alchemy, Sulfur represents the principle of combustibility and color.</note>—administered through a subtle regime of preparation—mixed with the material in a known manner. And its regime is, specifically, the fixation of the purest Sulfur and its solution by method. For with this, the medicine is tinged; and with this, when projected upon any of the diminished things <note>Base metals lacking "perfection".</note>, it completes them in Solar fulfillment <note>Gold</note>, as much as depends on the preparation of this second rank of medicine, provided the known and certain administration precedes it.

**A**ccording to the order of the things previously set forth, it remains for us to pass to the narrative of the medicine for coagulating Quicksilver, as part of the completion of the work which we showed we would determine at the outset. We say, therefore, that it is elicited from that same material—namely, from that which is noted in the multiple chapters of this work. And this is because, since Quicksilver is easily "fugitive" <note>meaning it evaporates or escapes when heated</note> without any inflammation, it requires a medicine that suddenly adheres to it in its depths before its flight, joins with it in its smallest parts, thickens it, and preserves it in the fire by its own "fixion" <note>the quality of being non-volatile or resistant to fire</note>. This continues until there comes to it a tolerance for a greater fire, which consumes its humidity, and through this benefit converts it in a moment into true Solar-making or Lunar-making substance <note>original: "Solificum & Lunificum"; meaning it turns the mercury into gold or silver</note>, according to that for which the medicine was prepared. And this indeed is not completed, so as to create the yellow, without the mixture of a thing that tints it, which is of its own nature. Therefore, of whatever kind the medicine may be, the medicine of the precious stone is sought both in the bodies and in the substance of Quicksilver itself.

<margin>Consider these things.</margin>

->*How an entry for the medicines may be achieved through the art.*<-

### Chapter LXXXIII.

**B**ut since it sometimes happens that the medicine mixes well, and at other times it does not, we shall therefore describe the method of mixing—namely, how each substance may acquire a most profound entry into a body, or why a medicine might fail to enter. The method is through the dissolution of that which enters, and through the dissolution of that which does not enter, and by the mixing of both solutions. For any substance of any kind is made to be "penetrating" if it is joined to the other at the level of its smallest parts <note>original: "per minima," a medieval term for atoms or fundamental particles</note>. This is accomplished through solution; and through solution, melting <note>original: "fusio"</note> is achieved in things that do not normally melt; and for this reason, they are more fit to enter and to alter the metal. And this is the reason why we calcine <note>term: calcination—the process of heating a substance to high temperatures to reduce it to a powder</note> certain things that are not of this nature: specifically so that they may be better dissolved, and they are dissolved so that the bodies may better receive an impression from them, and likewise be prepared and cleansed by them. Alternatively, we grant entry to those things which are not permitted to enter because of their own thickness <note>original: "spissitudine," referring to density or lack of volatility</note>, by means of the multiple repetition of sublimation <note>term: sublimation—the process of heating a solid until it turns into vapor and then solidifies again</note> of non-flammable spirits upon them—namely, of unfixed Arsenic and Quicksilver.

# THE THIRD AND FINAL
## PART OF THIS SECOND BOOK
### *concerning the proofs of perfection.*

### *Division of the topics to be discussed. Chapter LXXXVII.*

**H**aving set aside the obvious experiments, which we shall not recount here since they are known and certain to everyone without needing any special ingenuity—namely, those of weight, color, and malleability under the hammer—let us instead attempt the experiments of the craft with caution. We shall see if the projection of this art’s administration brings about a completion consistent with the truth. These tests are: <term>Cupellation</term> <note>original: "Cineritium"; a process of refining gold or silver by melting it with lead in a porous cup made of bone ash</note>, <term>Cementation</term> <note>original: "Cementum"; a process of purifying gold by heating it in contact with mineral salts</note>, <term>Ignition</term> <note>original: "Ignitio"; testing by fire</note>, <term>Fusion</term> <note>original: "Fusio"; melting</note>, exposure to sharp vapors, testing by the mixture of burning sulfur, <term>Quenching</term> <note>original: "Extinctio"</note>, the repetition of <term>Calcination</term> <note>original: "Calcinationis"; reducing a metal to a powder through heat</note> and reduction, and the easy or difficult reception of quicksilver <note>mercury</note>. We must therefore begin with the first according to the order, and then proceed perfectly to the others according to our promise, explaining their known causes.

### *Concerning Cupellation, and why certain bodies endure in it and others do not. Chapter LXXXVIII.*

**L**et us now speak of <term>Cupellation</term>, with all its manifest causes and the method of its preparation. Only the Solar <note>gold</note> and Lunar <note>silver</note> substances endure through the examination of the cupel. However, the secret of these two bodies has been sufficiently described by us in the depths of their substance. And it is, namely, that

The primary root of those <note>referring to the perfect metals, Gold and Silver</note> was a great quantity of Quicksilver <note>original: "Argenti uiui"; the alchemical principle of fluidity and metallic luster</note>, and its purest essence, which was first highly refined and later thickened until it could withstand melting under fire. <margin>..</margin> Therefore, whichever metals are diminished from perfection and possess more earthiness <note>original: "terreitatis"; the dross or non-metallic impurities</note> endure less in this trial. Conversely, those with less earthiness endure longer. This is because the perfect parts adhere more closely due to the subtlety of their particles, which mix and unite themselves most thoroughly. Similarly, those bodies that are of greater thinness, or on the contrary, those of greater density than those which consist in perfection, <margin>..</margin> must necessarily be separated entirely from the mixture because they do not share the same melting point, and thus they are divided. Indeed, those participating in a smaller quantity of Quicksilver are more easily separated from the mixture. It is evident, therefore, that since Saturn <note>the alchemical name for Lead</note> is of much earthiness, small quantity of Quicksilver, and of an easy, thin liquefaction—qualities which are most opposed to the perfection of the trial of the ash-cupel <note>original: "examinis cineritiij"; cupellation, a process where ores are heated in a cupel made of bone ash to separate precious metals</note>—it therefore endures the least among other bodies in the mixture during the craft of the cupel; rather, it is separated and yields most quickly. And so, since it yields more than the other bodies diminished from perfection, it is for this reason more suited to the trial of this mastery <note>original: "magisterij"; the specialized alchemical process</margin>, <margin>...</margin> and this is because it yields faster, and more quickly draws each of the imperfect metals with it out of the mixture. Because of this, a greater quantity of the perfect metal is saved from the strong combustion of the trial-fire, because the perfect metal does not remain for a long space of time in the consumption of the trial; thus, through the trial by Lead <margin>⸫</margin> less of it is burned away, and it is more easily purified. However, because the substance of Jupiter <note>the alchemical name for Tin</note> was capable of more Quicksilver, and participates in a smaller quantity of earthiness, greater purity, and a more subtle substance, it is therefore preserved longer in the mixture than Saturn <note>Lead</note> and Venus <note>Copper</note>, because it adheres more deeply to the mixture. And <margin>.</margin> this is the reason why a great quantity of the perfect metal is destroyed before [it is separated] from it.

# THIRD PART OF THE SECOND BOOK

Among all other bodies, Gold <note>original: "Sol" (the Sun), the alchemical symbol for gold</note> possesses the smallest quantity of Sulfur; therefore, among all mineral bodies, it is the least consumed by the inflammation of fire. Silver <note>original: "Luna" (the Moon), the alchemical symbol for silver</note>, however, ranks after Gold among all remaining bodies as participating in a smaller quantity of Sulfur, yet it contains more than Gold. Therefore, according to this, it can tolerate the ignition of inflammation for a shorter span of time than Gold and other things of a similar combustible nature.

Venus <note>the alchemical symbol for copper</note> possesses less resistance because it contains more Sulfur and a greater degree of earthiness <note>term: "terreitatis" (earthiness), referring to the non-metallic or dross-like impurities in a mineral</note> than Gold and Silver; thus, it tolerates the inflammation of fire less. Jupiter <note>the alchemical symbol for tin</note> participates in more sulfurousness and earthiness than Venus, and certainly more than Gold and Silver; therefore, it is consumed by inflammation more than Venus, and much more than Gold and Silver.

Saturn <note>the alchemical symbol for lead</note>, by its nature in its mixture, has preserved more earthiness and sulfurousness than the bodies just mentioned; therefore, it is ignited more quickly and easily than all said bodies, and is consumed more swiftly by inflammation because it has a very high amount of sulfur joined to it, which is more fixed than in Jupiter.

Mars <note>the alchemical symbol for iron</note>, however, is not consumed by its own nature, but by accident. For when it is mixed with bodies of great humidity, it drinks that moisture up due to its own lack of humidity; therefore, when joined, it is neither ignited nor consumed, provided that the bodies united to it are themselves neither inflammable nor combustible. If, however, combustible bodies are mixed with it, it necessarily follows that Mars is burned and ignited according to the nature of their combustion.

Since, therefore, cement <note>term: "cementum," a potent chemical mixture or paste used in a furnace to purify gold by corroding away base metals</note> is composed of inflammable things, the necessary cause of its invention is clear: it was so that all combustible things might be burned away. Since there is only one single incombustible body <note>Gold</note>, only that body—or one prepared according to its nature—is saved in the cement. Nevertheless, some things endure longer in the cement, and some less. Which endure more and which less is known from the causes already stated. Thus, Silver endures more, but Mars less, and even...

### How the test of the Cement is to be composed and practiced. Chapter 91.

<margin>.</margin>**W**e shall therefore describe the method of the Cement. Since we know it to be most necessary in the examination of perfection, we say that its composition is made with flammable things. And <margin>..</margin>all things of this kind are blackening, fleeing <note>volatile</note>, penetrating, and burning: such as vitriol <note>sulfate minerals</note>, sal ammoniac <note>ammonium chloride</note>, <margin>..</margin>and flower of copper <note>verdigris or copper scale</note>, and crushed old earthenware, and a very small quantity of sulfur—or none at all—and male urine, along with similar sharp and penetrating substances. All these things are therefore moistened into a paste with the male urine and placed upon thin plates of that body <note>metal</note> whose intent is to be examined by the judgment of the trial. Then, indeed, <margin>∴</margin>the plates, enclosed in an earthen vessel, should be spread out upon an iron grate, yet in such a way that one does not touch the other, so that the power of the <margin>⸫</margin>fire may run through to them equally and freely. Thus the vessel should be kept in a strong fire for three days. However, caution must be applied so that the plates are heated to redness, but not melted. After the third day, you will find the plates <margin>...</margin>clean of all impurity if the body existed in perfection. If it did not, they will be altogether corrupted and consumed by calcination <note>the process of reducing a metal to an oxide or powder through heat</note>. Some, however, place the plates in the heat <margin>...</margin>without the composition of the cement, and they are similarly purified if they are bodies of perfection. If they are not, they are entirely consumed. However, they require a longer duration of burning in this latter examination, which is completed by the heat of the fire alone, than those which are examined by the judgment of the <margin>⸫</margin>cement. But since Moon <note>Silver</note> indeed does not differ by much from the nature of Sun <note>Gold</note>, it remains with it in the judgment with only a small amount of management. And neither the separation of bodies...

The difference in the behavior of these bodies in these two types of examination <note>The author refers to "examinis," likely meaning assaying or testing the purity of metals</note> arises only from the diversity of their substance's composition. From this composition results a difference in how they melt, as well as their density and rarity. These qualities are the true causes of separation. It is perhaps because of their specific composition that their substance is not destroyed by the substance of a foreign body, since they do not mix at the smallest level <note>original: "per minima mixtio," a reference to the Aristotelian concept of "minima naturalia," the smallest particles of a substance that still retain its properties</note>. Therefore, it is necessary for those components to be separated from the mixture without the total destruction of their essence. For this reason, the complete processing of imperfect bodies <term>imperfect bodies: base metals like lead, tin, or copper, as opposed to "perfect" gold or silver</term> is recognized when the methods of melting, ignition, and solidification have been discovered through ingenuity.

## Recapitulation of the entire art. Chapter 99.

<margin>The causes [are explained] sufficiently through experience.</margin>
**B**ecause we have thoroughly investigated the known experiences of the sufficiency of the causes of this <term>mastery</term> <note>Latin: *magisterium*; the complete alchemical process or the "Great Work"</note>, according to the requirements of our proposed discourse, it remains for us to reach the completion of the entire divine work in one chapter, and to draw together into a summary the mastery dispersed throughout the abbreviated chapters. We say, therefore, that the sum of the intention of the entire work is nothing other than that the <term>stone</term> <note>Latin: *lapis*; the Alchemical Stone or Philosopher's Stone, the starting material of the work</note>, known from the previous chapters, be taken: then indeed, with the urgency of the work, <margin>Let it be pursued with constant labor. Sublimate it.</margin> let the work of the first degree of sublimation be pursued upon it with constancy. Through this, it is cleansed of corrupting impurity; and the perfection of sublimation is namely this: that the stone be refined by it until it arrives at the ultimate purity of fineness, and finally becomes volatile. From this point, indeed, let it be fixed by the methods of fixation, until it rests within the harshness of the fire. And here stands the goal of the second degree of preparation. But the stone is likewise administered in a third degree, which consists of the final completion of the preparation: namely, that you now make the fixed stone volatile by methods of sublimation, and the volatile fixed, and the fixed dissolved, and the dissolved volatile once again, and the volatile fixed once again, until it flows and alters in a certain <term>Solar-making</term> <note>Latin: *Solifico*; the process of turning base metals into Gold (Sol)</note> and <term>Lunar-making</term> <note>Latin: *Lunifico*; the process of turning base metals into Silver (Luna)</note> completion. Therefore, from the reiteration of this third degree of preparation in the <term>medicine</term> <note>Latin: *medicina*; the refined substance used to transmute metals</note>, there results a multiplication of the goodness of the alteration. From the diversity of the reiteration of the work upon the stone in its degrees, there results a diversity in the multiplication of the goodness of the alteration: so that from the medicines, some might transmute a body a hundred times its own weight, some two hundred times, some three hundred times, some a thousand times, and some indeed into an infinite Solar-making and true Lunar-making perfection. From this point, therefore, and finally, let it be tested whether the mastery consists in perfection.
<margin>Let it be investigated by the craft of testing, concerning the goodness of the silver and gold.</margin>

x ij <meta>catchword: Whom</meta>

<summary>This page provides a concise summary of the alchemical process, outlining three degrees of preparation: purification through sublimation, fixation against fire, and the repeated cycles of dissolution and stabilization to increase the potency of the "medicine." It emphasizes that the strength of the final transmutation (turning base metals to gold or silver) depends on how many times these cycles are repeated.</summary>

# THIRD PART OF THE SECOND BOOK

## On the method the Author has observed in handing down the art. Chapter 100.

<margin>
<unclear>...</unclear>
<unclear>...</unclear>
<unclear>...</unclear>
honor, veneration
</margin>
<margin>skillful reflections</margin>

-> **B**ut so that we ourselves are not bitten by the envious, we declare that we have not handed down our science in a continuous discourse, but have scattered it throughout various chapters. And this was done because, had it been handed down continuously, the wicked as well as the upright would have seized upon it unworthily. Likewise, we have hidden it even where we have spoken most openly; yet not through riddles, but rather we have addressed the artisan through a plain series of speech. We have recorded it in such a manner of discourse that it can only be gathered together by the mind of the most high God—blessed, sublime, and glorious—and by our own mind, we who wrote it; or perhaps by one filled with the grace of divine goodness, which grants to whom it wills and withholds from others. Therefore, let the son of learning <note>original: "doctrinae filius" — a common alchemical term for a devoted student or initiate</note> not despair, for if he seeks it, he shall find it: not by following the teaching alone, but by the investigation of his own natural inclination. For he who seeks through the excellence of his own industry shall find the science. But he who seeks only by following books shall arrive at this most precious art <note>term: Art (ars) — here referring to Alchemy, the "Great Work" of transforming matter</note> very slowly. For we have published this most true and entirely certain art, investigated by ourselves, for ourselves alone and not for others. We have therefore enticed only the prudent to the art, and through the techniques handed down by us, we have set forth the way of investigation for them. We have not written of it as something found except for ourselves alone, but we have written both the method of discovery and the techniques of those methods. Therefore, let the artisan of good mind exercise himself through those things we have handed down, and he will rejoice to have found for himself the gift of the most high God. Let these words, then, suffice for the pursuit of this exalted art.<-

## The End of the Second Book of the Height of Perfection by Geber.

<summary>In this concluding chapter, the author explains that he has intentionally scattered alchemical secrets throughout the text to prevent the "wicked" from abusing the knowledge, urging students to rely on their own industry and divine grace rather than rote reading.</summary>

->Concerning the six properties of things from which the medicine is drawn. Chapter 1.<-

<margin>.</margin>We have considered in our volumes—based on the secrets of the principles of natural properties, and also through our own experience and the entirely certain investigation of our discovery—that those things from which our medicine <note>In alchemy, the "medicine" or "elixir" refers to the substance capable of transmuting base metals into gold or healing the "sickness" of imperfect metals.</note> is drawn for the purpose of transmuting bodies, possess within themselves these properties of qualities. <margin>..</margin>First, that they contain within themselves a most subtle and incombustible earth, suited for fixing <note><term>Fixing</term>: the process of making a volatile substance stable under heat so that it does not evaporate.</note>, and entirely fixed with its own proper radical moisture. <margin>...</margin>Second, that they possess an airy and fiery humidity uniformly joined to that earth in such a way: that if one part be volatile, the other is as well. And that this humidity itself, above all other humidities, withstands the heat of the fire until the complete limit of its sufficient thickening is reached, fulfilling what is required for its completion while remaining inseparably attached to its earth without evaporating. <margin>⁘</margin>Third, that the natural disposition of the humidity be such that, by the benefit of its oiliness <note>original: "oleaginitas"</note> in all the differences of its properties, it may temper the earth joined to it through a greasy and total union—both being converted into one another homogeneously—and by a bond of inseparable conjunction, so that after the final stage of preparation, it may provide a good fusion <margin>...</margin> <note>The text cuts off here, likely continuing the description of the "medicine's" flow or melting point on the next page.</note>
