Sixth, that the whole compound, waxed <note>Latin: "inceratum," referring to the process of bringing a substance to a soft, wax-like consistency</note> with its final completion, be of such subtlety and thinness of matter that, after the final limit of its decoction <note>The process of heating or "cooking" the substance</note>, it remains in a state of very thin fusion when projected <note>The act of casting the medicine onto base metal to transform it</note>, like water, and of deep penetration even to the furthest part of the changeable thing, of whatever fixation it may be in its completion; and that by its closeness or affinity it adheres naturally to its like, with inseparable consolidation against the impression of fire in that very hour, reducing the bodies to the desired state by its spirituality.

## On the seven properties of the medicine: Chapter II.

**H**aving considered these things, we have found through our investigation seven properties of things necessary and opportune in our Stone, and they are these: oiliness <gloss>1</gloss>, thinness of matter <gloss>2</gloss>, affinity <gloss>3</gloss>, radical humidity <gloss>4</gloss> <note>The essential, life-giving moisture inherent in matter</note>, clarity of purity <gloss>5</gloss>, fixing earth <gloss>6</gloss>, and tincture <gloss>7</gloss>. Indeed, the first property of these differences is oiliness itself, giving universal fusion and the opening of the matter during projection. For the first thing that is necessary after the projection of the medicine is the sudden and suitable fusion of the medicine itself, which is perfected and incorporated with the mineral oiliness. The second is the thinness of the matter, or its spiritual subtlety, flowing very thinly, penetrating in fusion like water into the depths of the alterable thing, because secondly...

Because seventhly, after fixation, a splendid tincture is necessary, and furthermore a staining color, or a tincture coloring the convertible matter into true silver or gold, with all its certain and known characteristics.

## SECOND PART
### On the Purifications of Spirits. Chapter V.

<margin>arsenic</margin>
**W**e have treated in the first part those things which seemed necessary to us for our certain discovery, regarding the sufficient preparation of spirits and imperfect bodies. We intend here now to expand our discourse on the sublimations or preparations of the spirits of each species, insofar as is required for the completion of our mastery. Do not depart from this doctrine of our discovery, which we have seen to be most necessary and entirely certain through profound meditation and practical works. Here indeed we investigate the weights and the special method in their preparations and sublimations with entirely certain experience; first concerning **Sulfur** and its companion <note>likely referring to arsenic</note>, and so on for the others in order.

### On the Preparation of Sulfur. Chapter VI.

**G**rind "living" <note>native or raw</note> sulfur—clear and gummy—very finely, and cook it in a lye made of potash <note>original: "cineribus clauellatis"; literally ashes from charred wood</note> and quicklime <note>original: "calce uiua"; calcium oxide</note>. Collect its oily combustibility from the top, extracting it until it appears clear. Once this is done, remove it and stir with a rod; carefully draw out that which has come forth with the lye, leaving the coarser parts at the bottom. Let that extract cool slightly, and add to it a fourth part of its...

with good vinegar, and behold, the whole will congeal like milk. Draw off the clear lye <note>original: "Lixiuium"; a solution of alkaline salts</note> as much as you are able; dry the residue over a slow fire and save it. Regarding the administration of the dregs, observe this method:
<margin>»</margin> For one pound of this prepared Sulfur, take one pound of iron scales <note>original: "squamis ferri"; the flaky oxide that falls from iron when forged</note> well-calcined until red, one pound of well-calcined rock alum <note>original: "Aluminis rochę"; a naturally occurring sulfate mineral used as a mordant and astringent</note>, and half a pound of prepared common salt. Grind it thoroughly and place it in an aludel <note>term: aludel (a pear-shaped subliming pot used in alchemy to collect vapors)</note> with <margin>..</margin> a wide cover. Let the cover of the alembic <note>term: alembic (the upper part of a still used for distilling or subliming)</note> have a large and wide rim to preserve the rising of the spirits. Let the aludel be one and a half feet in height, so that the heat of the fire does not reach the rim of the alembic, and thus sublime it, as we have taught in our *Sum of Perfection* <note>original: "summa perfectionis nostrę"; a reference to the most famous Latin alchemical work attributed to Geber</note>. Collect that which has gathered in the rim <margin>..</margin> as a dense substance; however, that which adheres to the upper sides of the vessel in the alembic <margin>∴</margin> being light and powdery, throw away, because it is combustible, foul, and defiling. Put the collected substance by itself into a vial and cook it over ashes for so long until the combustible moisture is gradually destroyed, <margin>»</margin> and keep it cleanly, for it has been perfectly purified.

### On the Preparation of Arsenic. Chapter VII.

**I**ts companion, Arsenic, after being crushed, must be boiled in vinegar, and therein all the combustible fatness <note>original: "pinguedo"; a term used by alchemists to describe the oily, flammable component of a mineral</note> is to be extracted and dried. Then take one pound of calcined copper, and a half-pound of calcined alum, and as much prepared common salt as there is alum. Grind with vinegar so that it is liquid, and cook as with the <margin>»</margin> sulfur, and sublime it in an aludel without an alembic, and let it be one foot in length. Collect the white, dense, clear, and shining material <margin>..</margin> and save it, for it is sufficiently fit for use.

### On the preparation of Quicksilver. Chapter VIII.

<margin>The Height of Perfection, chapter 45, page 70.</margin>
Sublimate <note>Sublimation is the process of heating a substance until it becomes a vapor and then collects again as a solid on a cooler surface, used here for purification.</note> **Q**uicksilver <note>original: "Argentum uiuum"</note> in this manner. Take one pound of it, two pounds of reddened vitriol <note>Iron or copper sulfate heated until it turns red</note>, one pound of calcined rock alum, half a pound of common salt, and a fourth part of saltpeter. Once these are incorporated, sublimate them. Collect the white, dense, clear, and heavy material found around the rim of the vessel, and keep it as I have written to you regarding other matters. But if in the first sublimation it is found to be cloudy or unclean—which might happen to you through your own negligence—know that you must sublimate it again with the same dregs <gloss>sediment</gloss> and preserve it.

### On the preparation of Marcasite. Chapter IX.

<margin>..</margin>
**L**et crushed marcasite <note>A term used historically for various metallic sulfides, often iron pyrites or bismuth</note> be placed in an aludel <note>A pear-shaped pot used in sublimation</note> with a wide base to the thickness of a finger. First, collect the sulfur with a slow fire; when that ceases, strengthen the fire and replace the lid. That which takes the place of Quicksilver will then ascend, as I have fully written to you in our completed Summary of Perfection.

### On the preparation of Tutty. Chapter X.

<margin>∴</margin>
**T**utty <note>Zinc oxide, often found as a crust in the flues of furnaces</note>, when pulverized and placed in the aludel under the administration of great heat, is sublimated and becomes good. Sal ammoniac <note>Ammonium chloride</note> is sublimated from common salt, as I wrote to you in the practical manual on salts. Quicksilver is also sublimated to a deep red in this way: Take one pound of Mercury and two pounds of reddened vitriol <margin>...</margin>, and a pound of saltpeter; kill them <note>To "kill" or "mortify" mercury means to change its liquid state into a powder or paste so it can be worked</note> together, and sublimate also from calcined rock alum and saltpeter with the same weights. This is a great secret of our invention, nor should it be overlooked; indeed, the consideration of the dregs must be noted in the sublimation of Sulfur and its companion <note>Likely referring to Mercury, the "companion" of Sulfur in alchemical theory</note>, for Sulfur sublimated from <margin>∴</margin> lime of copper <note>Copper oxide</note> is whitened more than from lime of iron. And thus it is to be investigated.

# THE THIRD PART
->of this book, concerning how the bodies are to be prepared. Chapter XI.<-

**W**e set forth our third part as the complete preparation of the imperfect bodies <note>the "base" metals</note>, and we investigate the method by which they ought to be prepared so they may be perfected toward the completion of the first or second order by themselves, without medicine. Secondly, we address their preparation for receiving the white or red medicine <note>the elixirs for silver or gold</note>.

## On the preparation of Saturn. Chapter XII.
<note>Saturn is the alchemical name for Lead</note>

**C**alcine <note>reduce to powder through heat</note> Saturn by melting it with prepared common salt, stirring it with an iron spatula until it is turned into ash. Cook it 
<margin>»</margin> for a natural day <note>24 hours</note>, and let it be somewhat ignited, but not 
<margin>»</margin> much. Wash it thoroughly with fresh water, then calcine it for three days 
<margin>salt of tartar »</margin> until it is turned red throughout. If you wish to turn it white, 
<margin>»</margin> soak it with white alum water and reduce it with oil of tartar <note>potassium carbonate solution</note> or its salt. If, however, you seek the red, soak it with the water of saffron of iron <note>iron oxide</note> and the aforementioned verdigris <note>copper acetate</note>, and reduce it with salt of tartar as before; repeat this until it is sufficient.

## On the preparation of Jupiter. Chapter XIII.
<note>Jupiter is the alchemical name for Tin</note>

**C**alcine Jupiter just as you did Saturn. Whiten its lime <note>the powdered oxide</note> for three days as with Saturn. You shall not err in their reduction <note>returning the powder to a metallic state</note>, though it is difficult unless performed in a furnace of the kind used for cupellation or cementing—

"Know, you investigator of this work, that we have fully recorded the preparations of those things that can be reduced within this book <gloss>right here</gloss>, since this entire book is practical, compiled with <margin>The Summa (referring to the books he called the *Summa Perfectionis* or 'The Height of Perfection')</margin> certain investigation. After you have reduced these two leads <margin>of Saturn and Jupiter <note>Alchemical names for Lead and Tin</note></margin>, and you have found the brightness and color and all other things to your liking, they will perhaps lack "ignition" <note>the ability to withstand the heat of the furnace without vanishing</note>; and so that you may achieve your intent, begin thus. Dissolve calcined tutty <note>zinc oxide</note> and calcined tin, and mix these waters; with this water, soak the lime of tin, time after time. Thus, when you have introduced an eighth part of the tutty into the whole lime, reduce it into a body, and see if it has ignition; if it does, well indeed; if not, repeat until it has ignition. And I will place all the dissolving waters of spirits and bodies at the end of this book, each for its own kind. Do not wonder that we have dispersed the specifics of this practice in different volumes, since we endeavor to keep this art away from the wicked. With talc or precipitated Mercury <note>Quicksilver</note>, or more usefully with pure Moon <note>Silver</note> brought to this purpose by calcining and dissolving, we arrive at the ignition and complete hardness of these two bodies, with an unspeakable brightness. Know, however, that speculation alone, which is worth much in our *Summa*, profits little in <margin>a practical way of life <note>original Greek: "πρακτικος βιου"</note></margin> this invention of ours. Instead, one must grind, cook, roast, bury <note>referring to "inhumation," placing a vessel in warm earth or dung for gentle heat</note>, calcine, melt, destroy, build up, and cleanse the bodies.

### On the preparation of Mars <note>Iron</note>. Chapter 14.

**M**ars is to be prepared thus: Grind one pound of its filings with one and a half pounds of **sublimated Arsenic**. Moisten it with the water of **Saltpeter** <note>Potassium nitrate</note> and **alkali**, repeating this three times; melt it with great heat, and you will have **white iron**. Repeat this until it melts sufficiently, with a particular whitening.

### On the preparation of Venus <note>Copper</note>. Chapter 15.

The cleansing of **V**enus is twofold: one indeed for the white work, and the other for the red. For the white, it is cleansed thus: Calcine <note>reduce to powder by heat</note> it by fire alone, just as we have handed down in our **Summa of Perfection**. Take this calcined material, grind one pound with four ounces of **sublimated Arsenic**, and moisten it with the water of **litharge** <note>Lead oxide</note> three or four times, and reduce it <note>return it to a metallic state</note> with **Saltpeter** and **oil of tartar**. And you will find its body white and splendid, ready to receive the medicine. There is also another preparation of it for the red. Grind one pound of its filings with four ounces of **Sulphur**, or by cementing its plates with **Sulphur**, and thus calcine it and wash it with **Salt water** and **alum water**, and with reducing agents return it to a clean body, fit for the red tincture. There is also a third preparation of it. Calcine it by fire alone, and then dissolve a part of it, and dissolve an equal amount of calcined **Tutty** <note>Zinc oxide</note>, and with the solution moisten the remainder of the lime <note>the powdered metal</note> four times or more. Or do this with the dissolved **Tutty** alone, so that the **Tutty** enters beyond the middle of the lime, and thus reduce it with reducing agents; you will have a clean and splendid body, and with a little assistance it is led to a higher state, if you are a true seeker of the truth. The first Solar medicine <note>Solar medicine refers to a preparation intended to produce gold or create a gold-like elixir.</note> is therefore made thus: Calcine <term>the Sun</term> <note>original: "Solem"; alchemical code for gold.</note> by first creating an <term>amalgam</term> <note>A mixture of a metal with mercury.</note> with <term>Mercury</term> <note>original: "Mercurio"; quicksilver.</note>, just as you did with the moon <note>Silver.</note>. Extract the Mercury through a cloth, grind the residue with twice its weight of prepared common salt, and place it over a slow fire so that the Mercury may depart. Extract the salt with fresh water, dry it, and <term>sublime</term> <note>To heat a substance until it becomes vapor and then solidifies again, used for purification.</note> from it as much <term>sal ammoniac</term> <note>original: "salis ammoniaci"; ammonium chloride, used as a flux.</note> as required by adding it back four times. Join these waters in equal parts, extract the water by distillation, and return it to the residue four times. Finally, dry it and soak it with rectified <term>oil of tartar</term> <note>A thick, alkaline solution made from potassium carbonate (tartar).</note>, as described at the end of this book, until it flows like wax. This tints four parts of <term>pure gold</term> <note>original: "obrisæ"; gold of the highest purity.</note>.

<margin>solar medicine (chapter 7, book...)</margin> The second medicine is made from dissolved gold (as in the first recipe) and "green" made from copper, calcined and dissolved, in equal parts. These are mixed and brought to a waxy consistency <note>original: "inceratis"; the process of ceration, making a substance soft and pliable.</note> by distilling and repeating the process. At the end, soften it with prepared sulfur until it melts like wax; it beautifully tints eight parts of <term>Saturn</term> <note>original: "Saturni"; alchemical code for lead.</note>.

The third is made from dissolved gold, dissolved sulfur, and dissolved green <note>likely verdigris or a green copper salt.</note>, mixed and prepared as in the second recipe. In the end, it is softened with prepared <term>oil of hair</term> <note>original: "oleo capillorum"; a distilled oil believed by alchemists to contain a "vital spirit."</note> or oil of eggs—since the method is the same—and one part falls upon ten parts of lead. These medicines can also be projected upon <term>Jupiter</term> <note>original: "Iouem"; alchemical code for tin.</note> that has been prepared into a red state, and it will become a most brilliant and enduring material, a most fine work according to its degree. For these medicines transform in the "second order," as we have reported.

### On the Solar medicine of the third order. Chapter XXII.

<margin>...</margin> Indeed, the **Solar medicine** <note>The elixir for creating gold.</note> is made from dissolved **Gold** <note>Original: "Sole" (the Sun).</note>, prepared in the same manner as the **Silver** <note>Original: "Lunae" (the Moon).</note>. ## THE MOST SKILLFUL PHILOSOPHER AND MOST EXPERT IN NATURAL THINGS
### The Book of Furnaces pertaining to the practice of <term>chemistry</term> <note>original: "χημείαν" (chemeian); the Greek term for the art of transformation.</note>. Translated by Rodoger of Seville.

**C**onsidered we have, with a consideration not rooted in fantasy, that we have handed down the whole art in our volumes. But lest we be bitten by <term>envy</term> <note>In alchemical literature, "envy" (invidia) refers to the professional jealousy of other masters or the accusation that a writer is being too secretive or deliberately misleading.</note>, we have set forth this *Book of Furnaces*, in which we shall treat of manual practice, as much in the preparations of <term>spirits</term> <note>Volatile substances that can be vaporized, such as mercury, sulfur, or arsenic.</note> as of <term>bodies</term> <note>Solid metals.</note>, so that artisans may more easily attain the completion of the work. Since, therefore, the final consideration consists in the knowledge of things more closely related to the work, and in the method of operating, and since things can be extracted from other things by the <term>regimen of fire</term> <note>The precise control and graduation of heat levels.</note>. And since we cannot reach this end unless by separating the superfluous from the desired content—namely, the combustibilities and earthiness of sulfur that defile any body—hence it is that first we shall treat individual modes of operating, such as what kind of furnace with its instruments is suited for preparing any thing, up to the completion of the work with the <term>governance of fire</term> appropriate to it, and what vessels pertain to the purpose, so that the artisan may be able to perfect his operation. Secondly, which things are to be prepared, so that from simple or mixed substances one may be able to generate true <term>Sun</term> <note>original: "Solem"; alchemical gold.</note> or <term>Moon</term> <note>original: "Lunam"; alchemical silver.</note> with splendor. Thirdly, we shall relate those things which can be perfected with alteratives, and those which are naturally altered with total completion, and the method of mixing with due proportion, and

# THE FIRST PART OF THIS
*book concerning the methods of operating.*

## Concerning the calcining furnace. Chapter II.

Let a square <term>calcining furnace</term> <note>A furnace designed for "calcination," the process of heating a substance to high temperatures in the presence of air to oxidize it or reduce it to a powder.</note> be made, four feet in length and three feet in width, and let the thickness of the walls be half a foot in this manner. Therefore, let <term>Silver</term> <note>original: "Luna"</note>, <term>Copper</term> <note>original: "Venus"</note>, <term>Iron</term> <note>original: "Mars"</note>, or other things to be calcined, be placed in earthenware pans made of the strongest clay—the same kind from which crucibles are made—so that they can withstand the harshness of the fire until the total combustion of the calcination. Calcination is the treasure of the matter; do not let the work of calcination weary you. Moreover, study what we have said in our volumes. Through calcination, the imperfect bodies are purified, and through the reduction of the calcined material into a solid mass. And then let our medicine be projected upon ∴ <note>This symbol often indicates the point of "projection," where the transformative elixir is added to the base metal.</note> them, and rejoice.

## Concerning the sublimating furnace. Chapter III.

Let a <term>sublimating furnace</term> <note>A furnace used for "sublimation," the process where a solid substance is heated until it turns into a gas and then cools back into a solid, used here to purify volatile substances like sulfur or mercury.</note> be made, specifically in the manner previously and completely handed down in our *Summary* concerning the sublimation of spirits, according to this form.

...of the <term>cucurbit</term> <note>original: "bici," likely a truncated form of "cucurbiti." A cucurbit is the lower, gourd-shaped vessel of a distilling apparatus that holds the substance to be heated.</note> without a spout. For otherwise, all of the <term>sublimated material</term> <note>The pure solid substance that has transitioned from a vapor back into a solid on the cooler parts of the apparatus.</note> would descend to the bottom of the vessel because of the excessive heat. For at the conclusion of the sublimation, the <term>sulfur</term> <note>In this context, sulfur refers to the chemical element or the alchemical "soul" of the metal being refined.</note> does not rise except with strong pressure, even to the point where the <term>aludels</term> <note>original: "aludelis." These are a series of pear-shaped pots or condensers used to collect the products of sublimation.</note> reach a glowing heat. And unless the sublimated material were held in the upper channel when it is lightly melted, it would descend again through the sides of the vessel to the bottom, and thus nothing would be found to have been sublimated, as is well known to those experienced in the art.

### On the Distillation Furnace. Chapter 4.

**T**he <term>distillation furnace</term> <note>original: "Furnus distillatōrius"</note> is the same as the sublimation furnace, but the distillation vessel <note>original: "uas distillatorium." In alchemy, the vessel usually consisted of a "cucurbit" (the base) and an "alembic" (the cap) to capture and condense steam.</note> is made according to the shape of this figure. The fire, however, must be managed according to the requirements of the substance being distilled. We have sufficiently described the method of distillation for both minerals and <term>vegetables</term> <note>original: "uegetabilum." This refers to organic, plant-based matter as opposed to metallic or mineral substances.</note> in our *Summa Perfectionis* <note>original: "Summa nostra perfectionis." This refers to "The Height of Perfection," the most influential alchemical manual of the Middle Ages, attributed to Geber (the Latinized name of Jabir ibn Hayyan).</note>.

# SECOND PART CONCERNING
## THINGS TO BE PREPARED.

### On the preparations of spirits, middle minerals, and alums. Chapter IX.

<margin>iron scales or dross</margin>
**I**n this chapter I will describe the preparations of the **spirits**, <note>In alchemy, "spirits" refers to volatile substances that can be vaporized by heat, such as mercury, sulfur, and arsenic.</note> and first that of **Mercury**. If you desire to sublimate <term>sublimate</term> <note>The process of heating a substance until it turns into vapor and then collecting the purified solid that reforms upon cooling.</note> it perfectly, add two and a half pounds of common salt and half a pound of saltpeter <note>original: "salis petræ." Potassium nitrate.</note> to one pound of mercury. Mortify <note>original: "mortifica." To "mortify" a metal meant to destroy its outward metallic form, usually by grinding it with other substances until it became a dull powder.</note> the whole mixture by grinding it together with vinegar until no part of the "live" (liquid) mercury is visible, and then sublimate it as you know how, for it is a useful process. **Red Mercury** is likewise sublimated—specifically, one pound of it—with one pound of saltpeter and one pound of vitriol; <term>vitriol</term> <note>A sulfate mineral, likely iron or copper sulfate.</note> when these are ground together very well, the mercury is sublimated into a red and shining form. Furthermore, **Arsenic**—specifically one pound—is sublimated using one pound of filings of **Venus** <note>original: "limaturæ Veneris." Venus is the alchemical name for copper.</note>, half a pound of common salt, and a quarter-pound of calcined alum. These should be mortified with vinegar while stirring over a fire until the entire mixture turns black; then soak it again and dry it while stirring, repeating this three times. Sublimate the "pure" <note>original: "azymum." Literally "unleavened," here meaning purified or free from dross.</note> substance, for it is useful. **Sulphur**, having been boiled in lye and dried, is sublimated with the same dregs as arsenic, except that in place of the copper filings, you must use filings of **Mars** <note>original: "limatura Martis." Mars is the alchemical name for iron.</note> or its scales (the dross from the forge) in the aforementioned vessel. **Sal ammoniac** <note>original: "Sal ammoniacum." Ammonium chloride, prized for its volatility.</note> is sublimated with common salt using equal weights. **Tutia** <term>Tutia</term> <note>Zinc oxide, often collected from the flues of smelting furnaces.</note> and **Marcasite** <term>Marcasite</term> <note>A metallic mineral, typically iron sulfide.</note>, as well as the "imperfect bodies" <note>Metals other than gold or silver.</note>, are sublimated just as we have narrated in the *Summa of our Perfection*. <note>original: "Summa nostræ perfectionis." One of the most influential alchemical texts of the Middle Ages, traditionally attributed to Geber.</note> The various salts, alums, boraxes, <note>original: "bauratia."</note> and vitriols are prepared as we have sufficiently written in our *Book of Investigation*.

### On the Calcination of Jupiter. Chapter X.

**Jupiter** <note>In alchemy, Jupiter is the symbol and name for the metal Tin.</note> is specially calcined <term>calcined</term> <note>The process of heating a metal or mineral in the presence of air to reduce it to a powder (oxide), known as a "calx."</note> in this way: Let a large clay pot be placed upon the furnace, and when the **tin** has been put inside, add an equal amount of prepared common salt and calcined roach alum. <note>original: "aluminis roche." A high-quality potassium alum, often found in rocky form.</note> Stir it constantly with a perforated iron spatula until the whole substance has been turned to ash. Sift it and place it back in the fire, and let it remain glowing hot until it is perfectly whitened; then save it.

### On the calcination of Saturn. Chapter XI.

**Saturn** <note>The alchemical name for Lead.</note> is calcined in the same manner as Jupiter, but its calx <note>The powdery residue left after burning.</note> is made red like minium, <term>minium</term> <note>Red lead (lead tetroxide), used historically as a pigment and in chemical experiments.</note> which you should then save.

### On the calcination of Venus. Chapter XII.

**Venus** <note>The alchemical name for Copper.</note> is calcined thus: It is placed in the aforementioned calcining furnace, either in the form of filings, or by itself, or with powdered arsenic, or with sulfur, while being smeared with common oil. In this way it is calcined for three or four days with a very strong fire. Strike the calcined material so that it falls from the plates; <note>The metal was often placed on ceramic or metal plates within the furnace.</note> calcine the residue again. Once it has been calcined and crushed, calcine it yet again until it is perfectly reddened, and save it.

### On the calcination of Mars. Chapter XIII.

**Mars** <note>The alchemical name for Iron.</note> is indeed calcined through its filings in the aforementioned furnace until it has been perfectly reddened into a powder so fine it cannot be felt by touch. And this is called **Crocus of Mars**. <term>Crocus of Mars</term> <note>A red iron oxide (rust) used in medicine and as a polishing agent; the term "crocus" was used for any fine, saffron-colored powder.</note>

### On the calcination of the middle minerals. Chapter XIV.

All vitriols, <note>original: "atramenta." Literally "black things," referring to the sulfate minerals like green or blue vitriol used for making inks and acids.</note> salts, alums, and types of Tutty <term>tutty</term> <note>original: "Thutiæ." Zinc oxide that collected as a crust in the flues of furnaces where zinc-bearing ores were smelted.</note> are calcined with tartar <note>original: "tartaro." Potassium bitartrate, a byproduct of winemaking used as a flux or reagent.</note> and various other substances in the furnace called the Calcinatory, using a moderate or strong fire according to the requirements of the substance...

### *On the washing of the ashes of burned metals.*
### Chapter XV.

<margin>.</margin> **The** washing <term>washing</term> <note>Known as "ablution" (ablutio), this involves rinsing the calcined metal (the ash or "calx") to remove soluble impurities and salts used during the burning process.</note> of all burned and calcined bodies is performed in this way: First, have a large, glazed earthenware vessel filled with warm fresh water. With this, wash any ash of a calcined body, rubbing it frequently so that all the salt and alum is dissolved. Then, when it has settled at the bottom, carefully pour off the water so that none of the metal escapes the washing. <margin>..</margin> Place the calcined material back into warm water again and repeat as before, until it has been well and perfectly washed, and then save it.

### *On the waxing of the washed ashes.*
### Chapter XVI.

**The** waxing <term>waxing</term> <note>Known as "inceration" (inceratio), this process involves adding liquid to the dry ash to give it a soft, wax-like consistency, preparing it for further chemical changes.</note> of the washed ashes is done thus: Dry the washed ash, then dissolve two ounces each <note>original: "ana," a medical term meaning "of each."</note> of common salt, rock alum <note>original: "aluminis glaciei," literally "icy alum."</note>, and rock salt <note>original: "salis gemmæ."</note> in two pounds of distilled vinegar. Take four pounds of the aforementioned washed ash and soak it <margin>∴</margin> with this liquid until it has absorbed all the aforementioned water; dry it and save it.

### *On the reduction of ashes into a solid mass.*
### Chapter XVII.

**The** reduction <term>reduction</term> <note>The process of returning a calcined powder back into a solid, or "fixing" it, often aiming to restore metallic properties or prepare it for transformation.</note> of that washed and waxed ash is done as follows: Wash the waxed ash with distilled urine until you have extracted the salts and alums along with the filth of the calcined body. There should be four pounds of the ash, and let such soaking be performed

### On the treatment of Venus and Saturn. Chapter XXI.

**L**et there be three pounds of the paste of **Venus** <term>Venus</term> <note>The alchemical name for **Copper**.</note>, two pounds of **Saturn** <term>Saturn</term> <note>The alchemical name for **Lead**.</note>, and one pound of ferment. Extract it, returning to it a third part of its reserved water, and cook it as described in the preceding chapter. Do this three times. When it has "drunk" all its water, place it in the *orobus* <note>original: "orobo." Likely referring to a specific alchemical vessel or a method of slow heating using vetch-seeds or a similar medium to regulate temperature.</note> for fixation. Once it is fixed, restore it with reducing agents; the body is then ready for augmenting and tingeing <note>In alchemy, "tingeing" or "dyeing" refers to the process of a small amount of the Philosopher's Stone transforming a larger mass of base metal into gold or silver.</note>.

### On the treatment of Mars. Chapter XXII.

**S**ince the dissolution of **Mars** <term>Mars</term> <note>The alchemical name for **Iron**.</note> is found to be exceedingly difficult—for which reason we will discuss several methods and various other experiments we have performed at the end of this book—let there be two pounds of the paste of Mars, four pounds of Venus, and four pounds of Saturn. Let them be mixed without ferment and cook for seven days, and you will find the whole to be dry. Fix it, and place it with half its weight of crushed and mixed **litharge** <term>litharge</term> <note>original: "Litargirij." Lead oxide, a byproduct of silver refining often used as a flux or reagent.</note> in the reduction vessel. You will find a mineral body that is very useful, if you understand the things we have frequently discussed.

## *On the treatment of Silver. Chapter XXIII.*

<margin>.</margin> **T**he treatment of **Silver** <note>original: "Lunae"</note> is such that it may be brought from its mineral state to a more noble one. To do this, you must dissolve it; take three pounds of it, four pounds of dissolved **Copper** <note>original: "Veneris"</note>, and one pound of dissolved ferment. Join these liquids and cook them for seven days with a slow fire in a closed vessel <note>original: "Orobo clauso." This refers to a luted vessel or a specific type of pot used in the furnace.</note>, just as was done with **Iron** <note>original: "Marte"</note>, with all its liquid. Then, gradually strengthen the fire for another seven days, so that it is like the heat used for <term>sublimation</term> <note>A process where a substance is heated into vapor and then collected as it cools back into a solid.</note>. But for yet another seven days, give it a slightly stronger fire so that all the liquid is fixed <note>original: "figatur." Referring to <term>fixation</term>, the process of making a volatile substance stable under heat.</note> with it. Reduce this powder in a small quantity; if it retains a portion of **Mercury** <note>original: "Mercurij"</note> (which you can easily determine if you know how to perform <term>calcination</term> <note>Reducing a metal to a powder through intense heat.</note>), then it is done well. <margin>..</margin> If it does not, put it back to be fixed again until it is sufficient. Then reduce this with red reducing agents. You will find the **Silver** colored, transmuted, and fixed; hold it in high regard. For if you study our volumes well, you will find through our considerations <margin>∴</margin> the foundations upon which a true seeker must base their actions.

...to dissolve for seven days, extract the water with a cloth, and make the residue volatile <note>original: "uolatile." Therefore, guard this book even from your own son, because this work explains everything that we have written in our various other books.

### *On the ferment of Silver for the unleavened. Chapter XXV.*

**F**erment of **Silver** <note>original: "Lunae"</note> for the unleavened <note>original: "azymum." Borrowing from liturgical language, alchemists used "unleavened" to describe a base material that had not yet been "raised" or transformed by a ferment.</note> is made when Silver has been dissolved in its corrosive water <note>Likely a mineral acid like nitric acid, often called 'aqua fortis'.</note>. And if you boil this water down to a third of its volume, and place it in the open air by itself in a <term>bath</term> <note>A "balneum," usually a water or sand bath for gentle heating.</note>, or in dung <note>Warm, decomposing horse dung was the standard method for maintaining a steady, low heat for long periods.</note> for several days, it will become the oil of Silver, and the ferment itself; keep this for the white work <note>original: "ad albũ." The <term>albedo</term>, or the second main stage of the alchemical process, which results in the purification of the matter.</note>.

In alchemy, "tinging" is not just dyeing the surface, but a fundamental transformation of the metal's essence.</note> infinite parts of Mercury into the highest Gold, more noble than any natural gold.

**A good whitening.** <note>original: "Bona dealbatio." ## Mercurial Games. Chapter XXIX.

**N**ow, however, I will begin the Mercurial Games <note>The word "games" (ludi) in alchemy often refers to specific experimental demonstrations or minor operations of the craft.</note>. Take a <term>cement</term> <note>A powder used to surround and treat metals at high heat.</note> made of <term>silver litharge</term> <note>Lead oxide, often a byproduct of silver refining.</note> and <term>alkali salt</term> of <term>zoza</term> <note>original: "sale alkali de zoza." Likely a reference to soda or a specific plant-based salt used as a flux.</note>; place this in a <term>crucible</term> to the thickness of a finger. In the same place, put a ball of an <term>amalgam</term> <note>A blend of mercury with another metal.</note> of <term>Mercury</term> and <term>Silver</term> <note>original: "Lunae." The Moon represents silver.</note>, and place the rest of the cement on top, so that the ball is in the middle of the cement. Dry it, seal it with clay, and place it in a slow fire for half a day, gradually increasing the strength of the fire; let it begin to glow from evening until twilight. With a moderate fire, extract it and refine it in a <term>cupel</term> <note>original: "cineritio." A shallow vessel made of bone ash used to separate precious metals from base metals.</note>, and the Silver will be much improved in weight, resonance, and <term>fixation</term> <note>The ability of a substance to withstand heat without evaporating.</note>.

Also: Amalgamate Silver with Mercury, to which you add as much <term>Lead</term> <note>original: "Saturni." Saturn represents lead.</note> as there is Silver. Place this in another crucible, such that three-quarters of it remain empty. Pour <term>oil of sulfur</term> <note>Sulfuric acid or a sulfur-based solution.</note> over it and cook until the oil is consumed. Afterward, keep it in a moderate fire for two hours, and there will be generated a black stone with a little redness. Pass this stone through a cupel, and you will find the Silver increased in weight, resonance, and fixation.

Also, another thing to be considered: Grind Silver amalgamated with Mercury with double its weight of <term>metallic arsenic</term>. To this, add a tenfold proportion of amalgamated Copper (that is, of the Silver and arsenic combined). Grind the whole, fix it, and <term>reduce</term> <note>To return a chemical compound or "calx" back into its metallic form.</note> it into a metal body, and it will be well for you.

To this, add a third part of dissolved gold, wax as before, and congeal.

# THE LITTLE BOOK 
# ON ALCHEMY BY THE MOST 
# LEARNED MAN ROGER BACON,
->to which he gave the title, *The Mirror of Alchemy.* <gloss>12 pages</gloss><-

### PREFACE.

**I**n many ways and in various fashions the philosophers of old spoke through their writings, when, as if in a riddle and with a sort of clouded voice, they left behind for us a certain science more noble than the rest, completely obscured and altogether denied under a veil of despair—and this not without cause. Wherefore I instruct you that, above all the writings of others, you firmly ground your mind upon these seven chapters containing within themselves the transformation of metals, and that you revolve their beginning, middle, and end frequently in your heart; and you shall find in them such subtlety that your soul will be fulfilled.

### On the definitions of Alchemy. &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; Chapter I.

<margin>Where this definition of Hermes may be found.</margin>
**I**n many volumes of the ancients, many definitions of this art are found, the meanings of which we ought to consider in this chapter. For <term>Hermes</term> <note>original: "Hermes." Referring to Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary father of alchemy and the supposed author of the Emerald Tablet.</note> says of this science: Alchemy is a bodily science, simply composed of one and through one, joining together more precious things to one another through knowledge and effect, and by that same natural mixing, converting them into a better kind. Another says: Alchemy is the science teaching how to transform every kind of metal into another. And this is done through a proper <term>medicine</term> <note>original: "medicinam." In alchemical literature, the 'medicine' or 'elixir' refers to the catalytic substance—often the Philosopher's Stone—that triggers the transmutation of base metals into gold.</note>, as is evident in many books of the philosophers. Wherefore Alchemy is a science teaching how to make and generate a certain

<header>ROGER BACON.</header>

...a certain medicine, which is called the elixir, which, when it is cast upon metals or imperfect bodies, perfects them <margin>»</margin> completely in the moment of projection <note>The "moment of projection" refers to the final step of the alchemical Great Work, where the Philosopher's Stone or Elixir is added to base metal to instantly transform it into gold or silver.</note>.

### On the natural principles and the procreations of minerals. Chapter II.

**S**econdly, I will perfectly declare the natural principles and the procreations of minerals. Wherefore it must first be noted that the mineral principles in the mines are <term>Quicksilver</term> <note>original: "Argentum uiuum." Literally "living silver," known today as mercury.</note> and sulphur. From these are procreated all metals and all minerals, of which there are many and diverse species. But I say that nature always proposed and strove toward the perfection of gold. But various accidental <margin>..</margin> circumstances occurring transform the metals, as is found clearly enough in many books of the philosophers. For according to the purity and impurity of the two aforesaid things—namely, quicksilver and sulphur—pure and impure metals are generated, namely: Gold, silver, tin, lead, copper, and iron. Regarding their nature—namely their purity and impurity, or their <margin>∴</margin> unclean superfluity and defect—receive these truths. On the nature of gold: Gold is indeed a perfect body, generated from quicksilver that is pure, fixed, clear, and red, and from sulphur that is clean, fixed, red, and non-burning; and it has no defect. On the nature <margin>...</margin> of Silver: Silver is a clean, pure body, almost perfect, procreated from quicksilver that is pure, almost fixed, clear, and white, and from such a sulphur; it lacks only a little fixation <note>In alchemy, "fixation" is the process by which a volatile substance is made stable and resistant to heat.</note>, and color together with weight. On the nature of Tin: Tin is a clean but imperfect body, procreated from quicksilver that is pure, both fixed and not fixed, clear, and white in its outward appearance, and red in its inward secret, and <margin>⁑</margin> from such a sulphur; it lacks only <term>decoction</term> <note>original: "decoctio." A process of "cooking" or maturing within the earth's heat.</note> or digestion. On the nature of lead: Lead is an unclean and

<sig>D</sig>
<meta>catchword: imperfect-</meta>

<summary>This page outlines the "Sulphur-Mercury theory," explaining that all metals are composed of these two principles and that differences in their purity and "cooking" time in the earth result in different metals. Bacon defines gold as the only perfect metal, while silver and tin are described as nearly perfect but lacking specific qualities like "fixation" or "digestion."</summary>
<keywords>Roger Bacon, alchemy, elixir, projection, sulphur-mercury theory, quicksilver, gold, silver, tin, lead, maturation of metals</keywords>

...imperfect, from impure <term>quicksilver</term> <note>original: "Argento uiuo." Traditionally viewed as the "mother" of metals, providing their fluidity and shine.</note>, not fixed, earthy, dreggy, somewhat white on the outside and red within, and procreated in part from such a combustible sulphur. And it lacks purity, stability, color, and the ability to endure fire. **On the nature of copper.** Copper is an unclean and imperfect body, generated from impure, unstable, earthy, burning red (not clear) quicksilver, and from such sulphur; it lacks stability and purity of weight, and it possesses too much impure color and non-burning earthiness. **On the nature of iron.** Iron is an unclean and imperfect body, generated from impure, too-stable, earthy, burning quicksilver (both white and red, though not clear), and from such sulphur; it lacks the ability to melt easily, as well as purity and weight, and it has too much fixed, unclean sulphur and burning earthiness. Therefore, every Alchemist should take note of these things already mentioned.

->From which materials the matter of the elixir is most closely to be drawn.<-

### Chapter III. <gloss>This chapter is noteworthy.</gloss>

**I**n the things already said, the procreation of both perfect and imperfect metals has been sufficiently described. Now let us return to choosing and perfecting the imperfect matter. Since it is well known from the preceding chapters that all metals are procreated from quicksilver and sulphur, and seeing how their impurity and uncleanness corrupts them; and since no substance ought to be applied to metals which is not composed of or originated from them, it is left clearly enough to us that no foreign thing <margin>such as minerals.</margin> <note>The marginal note "ut mineralia" emphasizes that the Alchemist must look specifically to mineral sources rather than organic ones.</note>, which did not take its origin from these two, is powerful and sufficient to perfect them or to bring about a new transmutation. Therefore, it is a wonder that any prudent person would base their intention upon animals or vegetables, which are very remote, when minerals are found that are close enough in nature.

<summary>This page concludes the descriptions of imperfect metals (copper and iron) and begins Chapter III, which argues that the "elixir" must be derived from mineral sources—specifically mercury and sulfur—rather than animal or plant matter.</summary>
<keywords>copper, iron, quicksilver, sulphur, mineral principles, transmutation, elixir, Roger Bacon, impurity of metals</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Chapter III of Roger Bacon's "Mirror of Alchemy." The previous page concluded by stating that all metals are formed from quicksilver and sulfur, and that the Alchemist must choose the correct matter to create the "elixir" (the Philosopher's Stone). This page argues that while many substances (plants, animals, other minerals) contain the essence of quicksilver and sulfur, it is most efficient to work directly with the mineral forms provided by nature.</meta>

<page-num>211</page-num>
<header>ROGER BACON.</header>

<margin>page 20, line 6.</margin>
<margin>from quicksilver and sulfur, chapters 2, 3, 3.</margin>
One should not believe at all that any of the philosophers placed their art in those aforementioned remote things, except metaphorically. But from the two aforementioned things, all metals are made, and nothing adheres to them, nor is joined to them, nor transforms them, except what is of them. And thus, by right, we must take <term>Quicksilver</term> <note>original: "Argentum uiuum." Mercury, considered the feminine, passive principle of metals.</note> and <term>sulfur</term> <note>original: "sulphur." Considered the masculine, active principle of metals.</note> as the material for our stone. Neither Quicksilver by itself alone, nor sulfur by itself alone, generates any metal; rather, from the diverse mixture of both, different metals are procreated in various ways, along with many minerals. Therefore, our matter must be chosen from a mixture of both. But our final secret is most excellent and deeply hidden: from which mineral substance it [the Stone] ought to be made most closely and nearly, and we are bound to choose this very thing with great care. Suppose then, that our matter were chosen first from **vegetables**, such as herbs, trees, or everything that grows from the earth; in that case, Quicksilver and Sulfur would first have to be made from them through long boiling, a labor from which we are excused, since nature proposes Quicksilver and Sulfur to us directly. And if we were to choose from **animals**, such as human blood, hair, urine, dung, chicken eggs, and everything that comes from animals, it would also be necessary to make Quicksilver and Sulfur from them by boiling—from which we are excused, as before. If we were to choose from intermediate **minerals**, such as all types of <term>Magnesias</term>, <term>Marcasites</term> <note>Iron pyrites or similar glittering minerals.</note>, <term>Tutty</term> <note>Zinc oxide collected from furnace flues.</note>, <term>Black-pigments</term> <note>original: "Atramentorum." Mineral salts used for making ink or dyes, often vitriols.</note>, or <term>vitriols</term>, <term>alums</term>, <term>Borax</term> <note>original: "Baurach."</note>, <term>salts</term>, and many others, it would similarly be necessary, as with the others, to make Quicksilver and Sulfur from them by boiling—from which we are excused, just as from the others before. And if we were to choose any of the **seven spirits** by itself, such as Quicksilver alone, or Sulfur only, or Quicksilver and one of the two sulfurs, or native sulfur, or <term>orpiment</term> <note>original: "auripigmentum." A yellow arsenic sulfide mineral.</note>, or <term>yellow arsenic</term>, or red [arsenic] alone or in combination, by no means—

<summary>Bacon argues that the "First Matter" of alchemy should be sought directly in mineral quicksilver and sulfur, rather than laboriously extracting these principles from plants, animals, or complex mineral salts. This reflects the alchemical theory that while all things contain the same basic elements, "like produces like" most efficiently in the mineral kingdom.</summary>

<margin>Some things seem to be missing, nor do we know the proportion of the mixture.</margin>
...we would perfect [them], because since nature never perfects anything without an equal mixture of both, neither can we—from which we are then excused, as from the aforementioned <term>Quicksilver</term> <note>original: "Argento uiuo."</note> and <term>Sulfur</term> <note>original: "Sulphure."</note> in their own nature. Finally, if we were to choose them, each as it is, it would be necessary for us to mix them according to the due proportion, which human ingenuity does not know, and afterwards to cook them until they coagulate into a solid mass. And therefore we are excused from taking both in their own proper nature—namely <gloss>etc.</gloss> of Quicksilver and Sulfur—since we are ignorant of the said proportion, and instead we find bodies in which we find the aforementioned [elements] proportioned, coagulated, and joined together in the proper way.

<margin>The matter of the stone</margin>
"**Keep this secret most secretly.**" Gold is a perfect and masculine body, without any excess or deficiency. And if it could perfect imperfect things by mere melting when mixed with them, it would be the <term>red elixir</term> <note>The "Red Elixir" refers to the final stage of the Philosopher's Stone, capable of transmuting metals into gold.</note>. Silver is also a nearly perfect and feminine body; if it could nearly perfect imperfect things by common melting, it would be the <term>white elixir</term> <note>The "White Elixir" transforms metals into silver.</note>, which it is not, nor can be, because they are only perfect in themselves.

<margin>And if</margin>
For if that perfection were mixable with imperfect things, the imperfect would not be perfected by the perfect; rather, the perfection of the perfect would be diminished and rendered imperfect by the imperfect things.

<margin>NB</margin>
But if they were **more than perfect**, whether by double, or quadruple, or a hundredfold, or beyond, then in the meantime they would perfect the imperfect.

<margin>NB</margin>
<margin>Nothing seems to be missing. The words "nor by art for the work" depend on what precedes. But if they were [returned] to their original state in quicksilver, from which gold and silver have their origin—since the highly volatile nature of quicksilver overcomes the highly fixed nature of gold or silver, because quicksilver dissolves and melts gold and silver mixed with it.</margin>
And because nature always operates simply, the perfection in them <gloss>gold and silver</gloss> is simple, inseparable, and unmixable <gloss>to the white and red</gloss>; nor by art, to shorten the work, should they be placed in the stone as a <term>ferment</term> <note>A substance added to the alchemical mix to "leaven" or trigger the transformation, much like yeast in bread.</note> and then returned to their original state, since <gloss>liquid mercury</gloss> being highly volatile overcomes the high stability of the fixed body. And because gold is a perfect body, made from red, clear quicksilver and from such sulfur, therefore <gloss>for us</gloss> we do not choose it as the matter of the stone for the Red Elixir, because it is so simply perfect without clever purification—and <gloss>but not</gloss> so strongly digested that quicksilver dissolves and melts the gold and silver mixed with it.

<summary>Bacon explains that while gold and silver are perfect, they cannot act as the Philosopher's Stone in their metallic state because their perfection is "simple" and fixed; they must be reduced to their primary components to achieve a "more than perfect" state for transmutation.</summary>

## On the method of operating, and on moderating and continuing the fire. Chapter 4.

<margin>c</margin>
<margin>c</margin>
<margin>c</margin>
**I** believe you have found—unless you are very stiff-necked and completely covered by the veil of ignorance or foolishness—through the words already spoken, the true matter of the philosophers known to those skilled in the <term>blessed stone</term> <note>The Philosopher's Stone, the goal of the Great Work.</note>. This is the matter upon which the operation of Alchemy must be applied when we attempt to perfect the imperfect, and to do this with the "more-than-perfect" <note>original: "plusquàm perfectis." This refers to substances like gold that have been processed further into an elixir to have the power to transform other metals.</note>. And since nature has handed down to us only the imperfect along with the perfect, it behooves us to "more-than-perfect" the matter noted in these chapters through our work and artificial labor. If we are ignorant of the method of acting, what is the reason except that we do not observe how nature—which long ago perfected the metals—frequently operates? Do we not see that in the mines, through the continuous heat found in the mineral-bearing mountains, the thickness of water is cooked down and thickened to such an extent that over time it becomes <term>quicksilver</term> <note>original: "argentum uiuum." Mercury, considered by alchemists to be the "mother" of all metals.</note>? And from the fatness of the earth, through that same cooking and heat, is <term>sulfur</term> <note>original: "sulphur." Considered the "father" of metals; the combination of sulfur and mercury was thought to create all metallic ores.</note> not generated? And do we not see that through that heat, continued persistently upon them, all metals are generated from these aforementioned substances according to their purity or impurity? Nature perfects all things, whether perfect or imperfect—or rather, it makes the metals—solely by <term>decoction</term> <note>original: "decoctione." A process of steady heating or "cooking" to extract or refine substances.</note>. O, what great madness! What, I ask, compels you to wish to perfect these things through strange, melancholic, and fantastical regimes?

<margin>M</margin>
Just as a certain man says: Woe to you who wish to surpass nature and "more-than-perfect" the metals by some new regime or work arising from your senseless headstrongness. The God of nature gave a linear path, namely continuous cooking, yet you fools despise imitating it, or you are simply ignorant of it. Likewise: Fire and <term>azoth</term> <note>original: "azot." A term for the essential transformative agent or universal solvent, often associated with mercury.</note> are sufficient for you. Elsewhere it is said: Heat perfects all things. And elsewhere: Cook, cook, cook, and do not let it weary you. And elsewhere: Let your fire be gentle.

<margin><image-desc>A calligraphic mark or monogram at the bottom left, consisting of a stylized 'N' or 'M' shape with a vertical line and cross-stroke, possibly a personal mark or a reference symbol.</image-desc></margin>

<meta>catchword: gentle</meta>

<summary>Roger Bacon argues that the secret to alchemy is the imitation of nature's own process of slow, persistent heating (decoction) within the earth. He scolds alchemists who use overly complex or "fantastical" methods, insisting that a gentle, continuous fire is the primary tool for perfecting metals.</summary>

## On the quality of the vessel and furnace: Chapter 5.

<margin>∴</margin> **W**e have already determined the limit and the method of acting; now 
<margin>cc</margin> it is permitted to hear about the vessel and the furnace—how and from 
<margin>cc</margin> what they ought to be made. Since nature cooks down metals in the mines with a natural fire, she denies that decoction without a vessel suitable for it. And if we propose to follow nature in cooking, why should her vessel be rejected? Let us see first, therefore, what kind of place is the site for the generation 
<margin>∴∴</margin> of metals. It is clearly perceived in mineral locations that at the bottom of a mountain there is a heat persisting equally, whose nature is always to ascend. In ascending, it always dries everywhere and coagulates the thicker or coarser water hidden in the belly or veins of the earth or mountain into <term>quicksilver</term> <note>original: "argentum uiuum." Mercury, which alchemists believed was the base "water" of all metals.</note>. And if the mineral 
<margin>∵</margin> <term>fatness</term> <note>original: "pinguedo." An oily or fatty substance believed to be the basis of sulfur.</note> of that place was gathered from the earth heated in this way into the veins of the earth, it runs through the mountain, and it is sulfur. And as 
<margin>‥</margin> may be seen in the aforementioned veins of that place, that sulfur, generated from the fatness of the earth as mentioned before, also meets with the quicksilver...

<summary>Roger Bacon emphasizes the necessity of a gentle, steady fire that gradually increases in intensity, comparing the process to the growth of a human infant. He also begins a discussion on laboratory equipment, arguing that the alchemist's vessel must mimic the natural environment of mountains where mercury and sulfur are formed.</summary>
<keywords>slow fire, decoction, mastery, quicksilver, sulfur, alchemical vessel, furnace, mineral generation</keywords>

--- Page 238 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the furnace and vessel, establishing that the alchemist must mimic the "closed" conditions of a mountain to produce metals. It concludes Chapter 5 and introduces Chapter 6, which deals with the color changes observed during the process.</meta>

<page-num>216</page-num>
<header>THE MIRROR OF ALCHEMY.</header>

(as it is also written) in the veins of the earth, and procreates the thickness of the mineral water. There, by a heat that persists equally within the mountain, different metals are generated over a long period of time, according to the diversity of the location. In the places where minerals are found, a heat is discovered that is always lasting. For these reasons, it is rightly established for us to note that the external mineral mountain is everywhere closed in on itself and made of stone; for if the heat were able to escape, the metals would by no means be procreated. If, therefore, we intend to imitate nature, it is necessary for us to provide a furnace in such a manner—like mountains, not in size, but in continuous heat—so that the fire placed within, when it rises, finds no exit, and the heat reflects back upon the vessel containing the matter of the Stone within it, which is firmly closed. This vessel ought to be round with a small neck, made of glass, or of some earth representing the nature or composition of glass, whose mouth ought to be marked or sealed with such a cover and <term>bitumen</term> <note>original: "bitumine." A mineral pitch or tar used here as a sealant.</note>. And just as in mines the heat does not immediately touch the matter of <term>Sulphur</term> <note>original: "Sulphuris." In alchemy, Sulphur represents the active, masculine, and combustible principle.</note> and <term>Quicksilver</term> <note>original: "Argenti uiui." Literally "living silver," the Latin name for mercury, representing the fluid, metallic principle.</note>, because the earth of the mountain is everywhere in between; so also the fire ought not to touch the vessel containing the matter of the aforesaid substances directly, but it must be placed in another vessel, similarly closed, so that the tempered heat may reach the matter from above and below, and wherever it may be, in a better and more suitable way. Wherefore <term>Aristotle</term> <note>Alchemical texts often cited Aristotle, though many such works, like the "Light of Lights" mentioned here, were later attributed to "Pseudo-Aristotle."</note> says in the *Light of Lights* <note>original: "lumine luminum."</note> that Mercury is to be cooked in a triple vessel, and that the vessel should be made of the hardest glass, or, what is better, of earth possessing the nature of glass.

<margin>It is certain that the <term>decoction</term> <note>original: "decoctio." The process of heating or "cooking" a substance to refine it.</note> of the matter of the Stone can be done in an earthen vessel. If we wish to look inside, the vessel will cool; we shall remove the lid and close it again.</margin>

### *Concerning the method of projecting the medicine upon any of the imperfect [metals]. Chapter VII.*

<margin><unclear>margin</unclear></margin> **I** have completed the end of the promised matter, namely the Great Mastery, for making the most excellent red and white elixir. Finally, we must treat the manner of <term>projection</term> <note>The final stage of the alchemical process where a small amount of the finished "Stone" or Elixir is cast upon molten base metal to transform it.</note>, which is the completion of the work, and the joy desired and expected. And indeed the red elixir yellows <note>original: "citrinat." Refers to *citrinitas*, the yellowing stage that signals the transition to gold.</note> infinitely, and transmutes all metals into the purest gold. The white elixir, however, whitens infinitely, and brings any metal to perfect whiteness <note>Turning base metals into silver.</note>. But it must be known that one metal is further removed from perfection than another, and another is closer and nearer than another. And although any metal may be reduced to perfection by the elixir, nevertheless those that are near are reduced more easily, quickly, better, and more perfectly than those that are very remote. And since we find a metal near and close to perfection, we are excused by it from many remote ones <note>The author suggests using metals like lead or mercury, which were thought to be "closer" to gold in their nature, to make the transformation easier.</note>. Which metals are remote and which are near, and which is closer and nearest to perfection, you will find clearly enough and truly determined in my chapters, if you are wise and clever. And without doubt, he who has been so clever in this *Mirror* of mine that he knows how to find the true matter by his own industry, well understands upon which body the medicine should be projected for perfection. For the precursors of this art, who found it through their philosophy, point out the straight and naked path quite clearly with their fingers when they say: <term>Nature contains nature</term> <note>original: "Natura naturam continet." A foundational alchemical axiom suggesting that only substances of a similar nature can work together.</note>: <term>Nature overcomes nature</term> <note>original: "Natura naturam superat."</note>: And nature, meeting its own nature, rejoices, and is transmuted into other natures. And elsewhere: Every like applauds its like, because similarity is said to be the cause of friendship, about which many
->philosophers<-

<summary>This page describes the final stage of the Great Work, called "projection," where the completed red or white elixir is used to transform base metals into gold or silver. The author emphasizes that while the elixir works on all metals, it is most effective on those already "near" to perfection in their natural properties.</summary>

<meta>This page concludes Roger Bacon's "The Mirror of Alchemy." Following the previous page’s discussion on the nature of the red and white elixirs and their affinity for certain "near" metals, Bacon here explains the final secret of successful projection: the necessity of "fixing" the elixir through a "near" body before it can successfully transmute larger quantities of base metal.</meta>

# ROGER BACON. #

<margin>Philosophers</margin> have left behind a notable secret. Know that the <term>soul</term> <note>In alchemy, the "soul" (anima) refers to the volatile, spiritual essence or "tincture" of the Stone that gives it the power to change other metals.</note> quickly enters its own body, but it is in no way joined with a foreign body. And elsewhere: For the soul quickly enters its own <margin>♂</margin> body; if you attempt to join it with a foreign body, you will labor in vain. <note>original: "Anima enim cito corpus ingreditur suum"</note> For the very proximity [to its own body] is more luminous. For since corporeal things, through the <term>regimen</term> <note>The specific sequence of heating and chemical stages in the alchemical process.</note>, become incorporeal, and conversely, incorporeal things become corporeal; at the completion, the whole body becomes a **fixed spiritual entity**. And because that spiritual elixir, whether white or red, has been so highly prepared and drawn out beyond its nature, it is no wonder that it is <term>unmixable</term> <note>The elixir is so concentrated and "spiritual" that it will not naturally blend with crude, molten metal without a bridging step.</note> with the body upon which it is cast while molten. It is also <margin>.. thence compounded</margin> difficult to project it upon a thousand thousand parts and more, and to penetrate and transmute them instantly. Therefore, I shall now hand down to you one great and hidden secret. <margin>B.</margin> One part [of the elixir] is to be mixed with a thousand parts of a <margin>⁜</margin> more closely related body <note>Likely referring to purified mercury or lead, which were considered "nearer" to gold and silver in nature.</note>, and all this should be firmly enclosed in a suitable vessel; place it in the **furnace of fixation**, first with a slow fire, and always increasing the fire for three days, until they are inseparably joined. And this is the **work of three days**. Then again and finally, any one part of this [new mixture] is to be projected upon another thousand parts of any closely related body. And this <margin>...</margin> is the **work of one day**, or one hour, or a moment. For which our wonderful God is to be praised forever.

# RICHARD THE EN=

### ful booklet concerning alchemy <note>original: "περὶ χυμείας" (peri chymeias)</note>, to which he gave the title, The Corrector. <insert>in 3 pages</insert>

->Preface. How art imitates nature. Chapter I.<-

---

<margin>It is found in books published on this art, that</margin>
<image-desc>A decorative woodcut initial 'C' depicting a landscape with a tree in the foreground and a building in the distance.</image-desc>
**S**ince every improvement of a thing increases the nature of that very thing to which it belongs, it follows that in many of the teachings of the <term>philosophers</term> <note>The "philosophers" usually refers to ancient authorities like Aristotle or Hermes Trismegistus.</note>, nature is improved through art beyond the natural progression it possessed in its original form. And yet, no thing can perform its work except through the mediation of nature, since nature itself works hiddenly and intrinsically within the art through the management of the craftsman. From this it follows that the improvement of a nature is the increasing of its power, and the labor of art is the refinement of that thing. For nature completes its own degree—that which it is naturally able to achieve—and it cannot go beyond that limit unless nature itself is assisted <note>The original "impedita" (hindered) here refers to the art "interfering" with nature's raw state to force a refinement.</note> through the intervention of art. For although art does not transcend nature by creating a *new* nature through simple labor, art does transcend nature in regards to that specific nature which it is able to properly <term>refine</term> <note>original: "subtiliare" – to make "subtle" or spiritually pure.</note>. And therefore it is said: Art imitates nature—not that it builds a new one, but that it refines the power of that nature. To this end, art begins to profit where nature fails: to uncover the subtle nature enclosed within a thing and to make it manifest. When nature generates metals, it is unable to generate <term>tinctures</term> <note>A "tincture" is the concentrated alchemical substance capable of "staining" or transforming base metals into gold or silver.</note>, even though it hiddenly contains the full tincture within itself. Whence the philosopher says: Nature contains within itself those things it needs, and it is not perfected unless it is moved by art and operation. Therefore, in our work, art is nothing other than a helper to nature. This is evident in many of the works of common craftsmen: where nature first produces wood; second, the burning of fire produces ash from the wood; third, art produces glass from the ash.

<vocab>Richard the Englishman, The Corrector, alchemy, nature, art, metals, tincture, wood, ash, glass</vocab>

<summary>This page introduces Richard the Englishman’s "Correctorium," arguing that alchemy is not a violation of nature but a refinement of it. Using the example of glassmaking, the author explains that art "assists" nature to achieve results (like tinctures) that nature cannot produce on its own.</summary>

And this should be understood in this way: If the <term>primary matter</term> <note>original: "prima materia"; the fundamental substance or essence required to form a thing.</note> of glass had not been hidden in those ashes, art could never have produced or perfected glass from them without the pre-existing nature. Thus you will realize that nothing can be drawn out of things that does not already exist within them. 
<margin>.</margin> Therefore, every species within its own species, every genus within its genus, and every nature within its own nature naturally seeks the increase of its own power. It brings forth fruit according to its own nature, and not in another nature contrary to it, since everything sown corresponds to its own seed. We say this regarding <term>generation</term> <note>The natural process of reproduction or creation.</note>.

For if an alien or "worse" nature is introduced, art no longer simply imitates nature; instead, those inferior foreign natures infect the original nature, and immediately the expected result does not come from it. 
<margin>⁜</margin> This is because everything "worse" working upon a thing strives to destroy what is "better," while everything "better" working upon a thing strives to perfect what is "worse." Therefore, it is necessary for every craftsman to imitate nature and to know the nature of the thing his art intends to copy. 
<margin>...</margin> Otherwise, he is seen to be foolishly trying to "correct" nature through art without understanding it.

### The study of philosophy is necessary for this art. Chapter II.

**S**tudy, according to the <term>doctors</term> <note>original: "doctores"; the learned authorities and teachers of the Middle Ages.</note>, removes ignorance and leads the human intellect back to true science and the knowledge of any thing. Therefore, it is first necessary to acquire knowledge of this "sweet work" through study, and to sharpen one's wit through the sayings of the philosophers, since the known way of truth is found within them. 
<margin>⁜</margin> If, therefore, the laborers do not despise the labor, they will sweetly taste the resulting fruit. But those who shrink from studying...

<margin>»</margin>through the faithful instruction of a learned person when it is taught. For he who is lazy in reading books will not be able to be quick in preparing things. And further: He whose mind refuses to toil in study cannot easily become accustomed to practice.

## How many parts of minerals there are. Chapter IV.

**M**ineral bodies are specifically distinguished into two parts: namely, into the metallic part—that is, into the metals which derive their origin from Mercury, such as Gold, Silver, Copper, Tin, Lead, and Iron—and these are called the "greater minerals." And into the mineral part which does not derive its origin from Mercury, such as salts, <term>blackening agents</term> <note>original: "atramenta"; usually referring to vitriols or substances used to make ink and dyes.</note>, alums, vitriol, arsenic, <term>orpiment</term> <note>original: "auripigmentum"; a yellow arsenic sulfide mineral used as a pigment.</note>, sulfur, and the like; these are called "lesser minerals," for they are non-metallic bodies. Since it is manifest that the greater minerals—that is, the metallic bodies—have their origin from sulfur and <term>Quicksilver</term> <note>original: "Argento uiuo"; another name for mercury, literally "living silver."</note>, according to whether they are more or less purified. And now I shall set down the difference of their generation, and I shall say from whence they have their origin, which nevertheless is sufficiently clear in many of the books of the philosophers.
<margin>..</margin>
<margin>∴</margin>

Wherefore the watery substance is congealed by great cold after the action of heat, and therefore they are malleable and forgeable. ### How metals are made from Mercury, specifically. Chapter VI.

**N**ature has naturally crafted all kinds of liquefiable things from Mercury, or <term>quicksilver</term> <note>original: "Argento uiuo"; literally "living silver," the common name for the liquid metal mercury.</note>, and the substance of its sulfur. This is because it is the property of quicksilver that it is <term>coagulated</term> <note>original: "coagulatur"; the process of thickening or becoming solid, like curds from milk.</note> from vapor, or from the heat of white or red non-burning sulfur. Hence Aristotle says in the fourth book of <term>Meteorology</term> <note>original: "Metheoron"; Aristotle's treatise on natural philosophy, which was the standard textbook for understanding the physical world in the Middle Ages.</note>:
<margin>»</margin> If there is white, non-burning sulfur, it freezes Mercury into good Silver.
<margin>»</margin> If there is pure sulfur, clear with redness, and in it the power of <term>fiery-ness</term> <note>original: "igneitatis"; the essential quality of fire.</note>
<margin>»</margin> is simply non-burning, it freezes Mercury into the purest Gold,
<margin>»</margin> because everything dry naturally drinks up its
<margin>»</margin> moisture so that it is consumed within its parts. Therefore, the vapor of sulfur,
which coagulates the quicksilver out of its own substance, is a subtle earthy thing, an airy thing <term>decocted</term> <note>original: "decoctus"; a term from alchemy and cooking meaning "boiled down" or concentrated.</note> and <term>digested</term> <note>original: "digesta"; in alchemy, "digestion" refers to the slow application of heat to mature a substance, mimicking the way the stomach processes food.</note> from the first mixture united to itself by the cooking of heat. Afterward, it is elevated, decocted, and digested until it has a sulfurous power of coagulating Mercury into metallic bodies, according to whether the sulfur was simple or burning—which causes perfection or imperfection in metals,
<margin>notable</margin>
<margin>»</margin> as will appear later. An example of these things—that their
<margin>»</margin> primary matter is quicksilver—is that when they are liquefied
<margin>»</margin> by heat, they are turned back into it. It is indeed certain that they
<margin>»</margin> were previously quicksilver, because every thing consists of that into
<margin>»</margin> which it is <term>resolved</term> <note>original: "resoluitur"; the process of a compound breaking down into its original components.</note>. For ice is converted into water by the mediation
<margin>»</margin> of heat; it is therefore necessary that the ice was previously water.
