**H**ere it must be noted that Sulfur originates from the "fatness of the earth," <note>original: "pinguedine terræ"; medieval naturalists believed the earth contained an oily, combustible substance that formed the basis of minerals.</note> thickened within the mine through a temperate <term>decoction</term> <note>original: "decoctionem"; a process of natural "cooking" or heating within the earth's crust.</note> until it hardens and becomes dry. When it has hardened, it is called Sulfur. Quicksilver <note>original: "Argentum uiuum"; literally "living silver," the metal we know as mercury.</note>, however, in its primary root, is composed of a white, subtle, and highly sulfurous earth, mixed strongly with clear water. These are united so intimately—at the smallest scale—that the moist is tempered by the dry and the dry by the moist in equal measure until they become a single substance. This substance does not rest on a flat surface, nor does it stick to what touches it, because the dryness has altered the "wateriness" within it.

<margin>••</margin>
Furthermore, it is <term>homogeneous</term> <note>original: "homogenium"; meaning the substance is uniform throughout its nature.</note> by nature, because it either remains entirely in the fire and stays <term>fixed</term>, <note>original: "fixum"; in alchemy, a substance that does not evaporate or change when exposed to high heat.</note> or it entirely flies away in smoke. Since it is incombustible and "airy" in nature, this is a sign of its perfection; therefore, when it later flows down from the sulfurous earth and is heated, it ascends to the top. Thus, it is in its very nature to be <term>sublimated</term> <note>original: "sublimetur"; the process where a solid or liquid turns into vapor and collects at the top of a vessel.</note> by heat.

<margin>∴</margin>
Nevertheless, through continuous and excessive sublimation, it is purified, "cooked," and thickened. It is gradually <term>congealed</term> <note>original: "congelatur"; turned from a liquid into a solid state.</note> through the action of white and red sulfur. This sulfur is dissolved many times and afterwards congeals the quicksilver. By the action of heat during that sublimation, it is "waxed" <note>original: "inceratur"; a term for reaching a state where a substance is soft and fusible like wax, a key stage in the alchemical Great Work.</note> until—

<margin>•••</margin>
<margin>although</margin>
scarcely over the course of thousands of successive years and the operation of nature—it is congealed into a perfect metal. And this is indeed how nature itself works upon metals in the "mineral vessels" <note>original: "uasis mineralibus"; the cavities or pockets in the earth where minerals form, viewed by alchemists as natural versions of their laboratory flasks.</note> by means of heat. Therefore, whoever wishes to perfect a <term>medicine</term> <note>original: "medicinã"; here referring to the Elixir or Philosopher's Stone, which was thought to "heal" or perfect base metals into gold.</note> for the perfection of imperfect things must imitate nature in these works, even though these metallic bodies

<margin>⁘</margin>
differ in their composition from the quicksilver from which they are generated. They are formed in the same way that the quicksilver itself was pure or impure, and whether the sulfur was clean or unclean (and foreign to it), as has been said.

<margin>••</margin>
For if quicksilver is congealed by white, non-burning sulfur, it will become Silver. If it is congealed by pure sulfur in which there is

<margin>•</margin>
the power of "fiery-ness"—similarly non-burning—it will be Gold. If, however, the sulfur was...

<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 225 regarding the Mercury-Sulfur theory. <page-num>226</page-num>
<header>THE CORRECTOR</header>

if [the sulfur] is poor and weak, and the Mercury is of good substance, it turns it into copper <note>original: "æs"; used here for copper or bronze, which was seen as a "diseased" or imperfect form of gold.</note>. But if the quicksilver is porous, earthy, and impure, and the sulfur is also impure, foul-smelling, and earthy, and of a fixed substance, iron is made from it, which later cannot be melted <note>Iron's high melting point was a major distinction in early metallurgy; it was often worked as a "bloom" rather than cast like lead or copper.</note>. Tin, however, appears to have good quicksilver, but poor sulfur that is not well mixed, and as if it were not properly congealed <note>original: "congelatum"; refers to the process of a liquid substance turning into a solid mineral.</note>. Lead, on the other hand, has bad and coarse quicksilver, of foul flavor and impurity, and of weak virtue. For this reason, it is continually corrupted by the violence of fire. Thus do metallic bodies differ from quicksilver, according to whether there is foreign sulfur within them, or burning sulfur, or simple sulfur. And thus it is considered what power <note>original: "uirtus"; the inherent quality or "strength" of a material.</note> is in them. For since a great quantity of sulfur is an infection <note>original: "infectio"; here meaning a corruption or stain that degrades the metal's purity.</note>, a large quantity of quicksilver is recognized as perfection in them, since it is incombustible and "airy," whereas sulfur burns and <margin>>></margin> is burned, and always hinders perfection. These are the words, <margin>>></margin> word for word, of the philosophers regarding Aristotle in the fourth <margin>>></margin> book of the *Meteorologica*. It must be believed, however, that the <margin>>></margin> truth of those philosophers was never found in any lie. In doing so, art follows the way of nature, from which truth can be drawn; nor should belief be given to fictional fables, nor to lying works <margin>to liars</margin> which are entirely foreign to the work of nature, as it is said: "He who believes in a lie, and not in the secrets of the philosophers, loses his time and his labors with the work."

### *Concerning the formation of Minerals which do not derive their origin from Mercury. Chapter VIII.*

**T**here are, however, "middle minerals" <note>original: "media mineralia"; substances that sit between common stones and true metals in the alchemical hierarchy.</note> according to what has been said, which do not take their origin from Mercury—that is, from proximate Mercury <note>The author distinguishes between substances made directly from mercury and those made from more distant, "weaker" mineral roots.</note>—and are of weak substance. Some of these are salts, which <margin>>></margin> liquefy easily in moisture, such as alum and vitriol <note>original: "chalcantum"; blue or green vitriol (sulfates of copper or iron).</note>, simple salt, <margin>>></margin> and sal ammoniac, saltpeter <note>original: "sal petræ lapidis"; literally "salt of the stone," used in making gunpowder and acids.</note>, and all types of salts. And...

<summary>This page describes how different combinations of Mercury and Sulfur create specific metals (copper, iron, tin, lead) and introduces "middle minerals" like salts and vitriol.</summary>
<keywords>Mercury-Sulfur theory, metallurgy, Aristotle, Meteorologica, copper, iron, tin, lead, salts, vitriol, alchemy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical classification of mineral substances. Following the discussion of the primary metallic "roots" (Mercury and Sulfur), Richard now addresses secondary minerals—orpiment, arsenic, and vitriols—explaining their relationship to true metals and their role in alchemical deception.</meta>

<page-num>227</page-num>
<header>OF RICHARD THE ENGLISHMAN.</header>
<sig>F ij</sig>

Certain substances are oily and do not easily melt with moisture alone, such as orpiment <note>original: "auripigmentum"; a golden-yellow arsenic sulfide used as a pigment and in alchemy.</note>, arsenic, sulfur, and other sulfurous things. For the watery nature of sulfurs is mixed with viscous earth by a strong combination <margin>.</margin> through the boiling of heat, until they become oily, and afterward they are congealed by cold. Vitreous minerals <note>original: "Atramenta"; literally "inks," but in medieval science, this refers to vitriols or metallic sulfates used for making ink and in chemical processes.</note>, however, are composed of salt, sulfur, and stones. It is believed that within them lies the mineral power of certain liquefiable substances that are made from them, such as blue vitriol <note>original: "chalcanthum"; copper sulfate.</note> and yellow vitriol <note>original: "calcatar"; iron sulfate.</note>, which are generated from larger grains of vitreous mineral. These <margin>..</margin> do not dissolve unless the saltiness within the sulfur itself is dissolved, and afterward it is frozen in the cold. This substance then takes on a mineral power from certain bodies naturally in the earth; that which takes on an iron-like power will be red or earthy, and is yellow vitriol. That which <margin>⁑</margin> takes on a copper-like power will be "green of brass" <note>original: "uiride æris"; often refers to verdigris or copper acetate.</note> or blue vitriol. Hence it is possible for these two—namely yellow and blue vitriol—to be generated from it. 

Although all the aforementioned substances participate in the mineral power along with metals, nevertheless metallic bodies cannot be made from them artificially, since they are of a different nature and did not draw their origin from the same "proximate matter" <note>In Aristotelian physics, "proximate matter" is the immediate physical substance a thing is made of, whereas "remote matter" is the primal elements.</note> as metals. <margin>notabile</margin> Nevertheless, I do not deny that metals can be purified or dissolved by them, <margin>...</margin> or that a sophisticated form <note>A "sophistic" form refers to a deceptive appearance that makes a base metal look like gold or silver without changing its internal essence.</note> can be introduced through them, so that men are led into error. The blackness or impurity of lead can also be wiped away; yet lead always remains lead, even though it may look like silver and they introduce other new qualities into it so that it appears to be silver. Thus, craftsmen can also create "congelations" <note>The process of solidifying a liquid, specifically turning liquid quicksilver into a solid.</note> <margin>⁑</margin> through them—that is, to extract the moisture from Mercury using dry things so that the Mercury appears coagulated—but that kind of coagulation is the worst. The coagulation of the Philosophers, however, does not dry out the moisture, but <margin>⁑</margin> coagulates the Mercury through the consumption of its radical humidity <note>In alchemy, "radical humidity" is the essential, internal moisture that gives a metal its flexibility and life; consuming it properly "fixes" the metal.</note>. 

Hear what Aristotle says: "Let the practitioners <margin>.</margin> of Alchemy know that the species of things cannot be transmuted, but [only] things similar to them [can be]... <note>This is a famous quote from the "Sciant Artifices," a 13th-century translation of Avicenna that was often attributed to Aristotle. It argues that alchemists can only change the "accidents" (color, weight) of a metal, not its "essence."</note>

<meta>catchword: phuris</meta>

...they can do, and they can dye red with lemon-yellow, and dye white with a red color, until it becomes very much like gold or silver—just like those who combine tin, copper, and Mercury and make **sophistic gold** <note>original: "aurum sophisticum"; imitation or fake gold that appears real to the eye but lacks the density and permanence of true gold.</note> from them. Thus, refining by means of lesser minerals is not impossible, but the vapor of heat, weight, and even the loss of mass do not withstand testing in these [fakes]; whereas they do in true gold and silver, which are in no way permitted by nature or art to be created except by the **reduction of bodies into the primary matter** <note>original: "primam materiam"; the formless, fundamental substance that alchemists believed must be reached before a base metal could be "reborn" as gold.</note>. In this way, the species of things can be transmuted, as **Aristotle** <note>The author likely refers to the "Meteorologica" or the "De mineralibus" (attributed to Aristotle), which provided the scientific framework for medieval alchemy.</note> adds later. This is not achieved through simple melting alone, but through the **resolution** <note>The process of dissolving or breaking down a solid into its liquid or "mercurial" essence.</note> of congealed Mercury; when mixed with its own spirit, the body is transformed into Mercury.

### Chapter IX: On the generation of common and simple Sulfur, and of Mercury.

<margin>Take note.</margin>
Since it has already been said that sulfur is an impediment in metals, I will now set forth the difference between the generation of Mercury and of sulfur. Although both contribute to the cause of a metal—namely, Mercury as the essential part and sulfur as the accidental part—nevertheless, that sulfur is itself twofold: **living** and **burning**. **Living sulfur** <note>original: "vivum"; "Sulfur vive" or "virgin sulfur," considered a pure, active principle of nature rather than the common yellow mineral.</note> causes metals, although one metal differs from another according to how much it is tainted by the viscosity of the earth. Yet simple living Sulfur, which causes Gold and Silver, is nothing but a hot and dry vapor generated from the purest terrestrial dryness, in which fire predominates in every way; this is called an **element** alongside the Mercury of metals. But the generation of common sulfur differs from the generation of Mercury, as has been said: the wateriness of common sulfur is mixed with a highly viscous earthiness by means of boiling heat, until they become oily. Thus, the generation of Mercury differs in its second part from the generation of sulfur...

<summary>This page distinguishes between "sophistic" gold (imitation) and true transmutation, which requires reducing metals to their "primary matter." It also introduces the dual nature of sulfur, distinguishing the "living sulfur" that forms gold and silver from the "common sulfur" found in the earth.</summary>

...of this sulfur, since it is generated from the most subtle, whitest, and sulfurous earth, combined with the clearest water that the earth produces through a very strong mixing—so much so that one cannot be separated from the other. This continues until it rests upon a flat surface; nor does it cling to what <margin>.</margin> touches it, because of the dryness of the earth, which alters the wateriness by means of that strong mixing. Therefore, it is an element which, along with simple sulfur, is the source of all **malleable substances** <note>original: "ductibilium"; referring to metals that can be hammered or shaped.</note>. Or more properly, it is similar to certain malleable metals; and because of a certain harmony in their generation, sulfur is mixed with Mercury, and one <margin>..</margin> alters the other in its nature. For this reason, every metallic body is known to clearly contain within itself sulfur and Mercury. When it melts by means of heat, the substance of Mercury and sul- <margin>..</margin> fur appears, especially in the color and the red "skin" floating on top. But <margin>.</margin> the adherence of one to the other is a property of their nature, since one <margin>..</margin> <margin>∴</margin> cannot generate a metal without the other. And insofar as the sulfur <margin>..</margin> is more "simple" <note>In alchemy, "simple" refers to a state of high purity and lack of internal conflict between elements.</note>, it rejoices and coheres all the more to the simple and pure Mercury, so that one is joined more strongly with the other; <margin>.. cc</margin> and thus, from these, the more perfect metals are generated.

### Chapter X. That it is impossible for intermediate minerals to be artificially made into metals.

<margin>...</margin> **B**ut because it was determined in the preceding chapter that minor minerals cannot be artificially turned into metals, it remains to prove this more strongly for the sake of a greater truth. First, it is thus: Because **lesser minerals** <note>original: "minora mineralia"; these are non-metallic substances like salts, alums, and vitriols that were often used in chemical experiments but were considered fundamentally different from metals.</note> are not generated from the **primary matter** of metals, which is Mercury. Since their generation differs from the generation of Mercury from the very start—in form, in <margin>∴</margin> matter, and in composition—they therefore cannot become metals. This is because for any one species, there is but one primary matter and "seed" from which it is generated. But the first part of this argument is clear, because lesser minerals do not [come from Mercury]...

--- Page 252 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument from Chapter X regarding why "lesser minerals" (such as salts, vitriols, and alums) cannot be used to create metals. The author builds on the previous page's discussion of the Sulfur-Mercury theory by contrasting the true "first matter" of metals with these external substances.</meta>

<page-num>230</page-num>
<header>THE CORRECTOR</header>

[All metals] are generated from **Mercury**, as is clear from the writings of **Aristotle** and **Avicenna** <note>Aristotle and the Persian polymath Avicenna (Ibn Sina) were the primary authorities for the medieval "Sulfur-Mercury theory," which claimed all metals were made of these two principles in varying degrees of purity.</note>. Therefore, if these lesser minerals were to become metals, it would be necessary for them to first be transformed into the **first matter** <term>materia prima; the formless, foundational substance from which all specific things are created</term> of metals. But because this cannot be achieved through artificial means, they will never become metals. Thus, the second part of the previous argument is sufficiently clear, as was explained in the preceding chapter.

Secondly, regarding the same point: because these lesser minerals cannot be artificially transformed into the **principle of the art**—which is Mercury—they likewise cannot reach the "middle" or the "end" of the process, which are the **metal** and the **tincture** <note>original: "tinctura"; in alchemy, the "tincture" is the transformative agent or Elixir that perfects base metals into gold.</note>. This logic holds firm. For just as food in a human being cannot become a human through the process of generation unless it is first converted into **seed** <note>original: "sperma"</note>, and thus by adding its like a new human is generated; so too the lesser minerals are of a nature foreign to metals. Although they may share in some general mineral power, they are nevertheless of a weaker virtue and are **combustible** <note>original: "adustibilia"; in alchemy, being "combustible" or "burnable" was a sign of impurity, as true gold resists the fire.</note>. Therefore, the metallic nature does not "rejoice" in them, but rather rejects them. It preserves only those things which are of its own nature.

For example: if water and earth were mixed, they would eventually separate from each other, because earth seeks the bottom—being heavy and dry—while water seeks the surface. And by no means can they be so joined by artifice that these two natures remain united as a single nature, even though water is able to wash and cleanse the earth. But that the dryness of earth should be artificially changed into the moisture of water is believed to be impossible, even if the earth is dampened by the water. 

In the same way, lesser minerals can be joined with metals to purge them and, in some manner, introduce a new outward form; but nature does not permit them to remain permanently with the metals, nor to ripen that which is immature. Therefore, those people are fools who bring forward so many and such diverse operations and **deceptions** <note>original: "sophisticationes"; refers to "sophistry" or fraudulent alchemical practices intended to mimic gold without actually creating it.</note> to trick people. They use disproportionate things that neither provide a nature nor receive one—namely: **placentas** <note>original: "fecundinas"</note>, eggshells, hair, the blood of a red-haired man, **basilisks** <note>The basilisk was a mythical serpent; in alchemical "slang," it sometimes referred to a highly toxic or potent mineral crust.</note>, urine, herbs, human dung, and so on through infinite kinds of filth. They hope to achieve the best results using the worst materials, and to fulfill the deficiencies of nature with these things. But because in these natural things, there is never any true [matter]...

<meta>catchword: matter (materiã)</meta>

<summary>This page argues that lesser minerals and organic waste cannot be transformed into metals because they lack the "first matter" (Mercury) essential to the metallic nature. The author dismisses the use of bizarre ingredients like blood, hair, or dung as fraudulent "sophistry" that fails to respect the laws of natural generation.</summary>

# RICHARD THE ENGLISHMAN.

...investigated the [first] matter by using their imagination. They wanted to sow dung and reap wheat, which is quite impossible, as it is said: "Whatsoever a man soweth, that shall he also reap." <note>A reference to Galatians 6:7, a common biblical citation used by alchemists to argue that gold can only be produced from a "seed" of gold.</note> Therefore, if he sows dung, he will find dung. It is no wonder that no one reaches perfection in such filth, for they and all who follow them are deluded. You must, therefore, sow **Gold** and **Silver** so that they may bring forth fruit through your labor and the mediation of nature. For nature possesses that which you seek, and no other thing in the world does. All other things are foul-smelling, and they perish under the persistence and testing of the fire.

There are also some Alchemists working with the lesser minerals <margin>the four spirits</margin>, specifically with the **four spirits**: common sulfur, arsenic, orpiment <note>original: *auripigmentum*; a yellow arsenic sulfide mineral used as a pigment and in alchemy</note>, and sal ammoniac. They wish to create **tinctures** <term>tinctura; a transformative substance that changes the color and nature of a base metal into gold or silver</term> from these, but they are entirely unable to do so, as is clear from the definition of nature. For <margin>on tinting</margin> to "tint" is nothing other than for the tinting agent to transform the thing being tinted into its own nature, and for them to remain together without any separation. It teaches nature to struggle against the pursuing fire, so that the nature of the tinter and the tinted are in harmony. For example: if you tint lead or tin (or something similar) with Gold or Silver, they agree in their natures because both sides draw their origin from **Mercury**. When the mature is joined with the immature, it will be brought to perfection through this path. 

But since these four spirits are of a different nature than the metals—as was sufficiently stated before—if they "tint," then I ask: should they convert the metal, or be converted by it? If they are converted, then it is not a "tincture," as was previously shown by its definition. If, however, they convert the metal, then they will convert the thing being tinted into their own nature. This nature is **earthy**, which is contrary to the metallic nature; therefore, it cannot create metal through tinting. That the tinter converts the thing being tinted into its own nature is proven by the fact that everything that generates naturally produces something like itself. But because the generating nature of the four spirits is earthy, it will therefore generate something like itself, which will also be earthy matter just like the spirit itself. Thus it is with every other foolish tincture that is not found...

<summary>This page argues that true alchemy requires "sowing" gold and silver rather than lesser minerals, because the "four spirits" are too earthy and volatile to permanently transform metals. The author defines "tinting" as a process where a substance converts another into its own nature, reinforcing the idea that only metallic seeds can produce metals.</summary>

### Chapter XI. On the difference between common sulfur and the simple, non-burning sulfur of the philosophers.

<margin>that is, gold.</margin>
**N**ow we must inquire into the difference between the simple, non-burning sulfur of the philosophers and common sulfur. Since the philosopher <note>The "Philosopher" usually refers to Aristotle, but in this context, it likely refers to Geber (Jabir ibn Hayyan), the foundational authority on the Sulfur-Mercury theory of metals.</note> generally states: "Sulfur coagulates Mercury."

<margin>Note.</margin>
Does every kind of sulfur coagulate Mercury? It must be said: No. Because all common sulfur, according to the philosopher, is contrary to metals. Likewise, Avicenna <note>Ibn Sina, the Persian physician whose work *De Congelatione* was a staple of Latin alchemy.</note> says: It does not enter into our mastery <term>magisterium; the "mastery" or Great Work of creating the Philosopher's Stone</term>, because it did not originate from it, since it always infects, blackens, and corrupts, in whatever way it may be prepared by art. For it is itself an "infected fire." Therefore, if it is fixed <note>made resistant to heat</note>, it hinders melting, nor is it possible for it to be joined to a body. An example of this is seen in iron, which is known to have fixed, thick, and unclean sulfur within itself. If, however, it is calcined <term>calcinatio; the process of heating a substance to high temperatures to reduce it to a powder</term>, it returns to an earthy substance, like a dead powder; and how could it inspire life into other metals? For it has a double superfluity: a flammable substance and earthy dregs. Therefore, by this, consider the sulfur of the common people and not that of the philosophers, since the latter is a simple, living fire, giving life to other dead bodies and maturing them. It supplies what nature lacks, since it is of itself possessed of an abundant maturity, being perfect in its own nature and increasingly purified through art. Whence

<margin>since this sulfur of the philosophers is the true improvement of fire</margin>

<margin>•</margin>
Again, Avicenna <note>original: "iterum Auicenna"</note> says: Such sulfur is not found upon the earth, except for that which exists within those two bodies, the Sun and the Moon <note>Sol and Luna; the alchemical names for Gold and Silver</note>. In the Sun, however, it is more perfect, because it is more highly "digested" and "boiled down" <note>Alchemy often used cooking metaphors—digestion and decoction—to describe the natural heat that matures metals underground over centuries.</note>. The philosophers, however, have subtly imagined how those sulfurs could be drawn out from these more perfect bodies, and how their qualities could be better purified by art, so that through this art—acting as a mediator for nature—something might be achieved that did not previously appear in them, even though they possessed it fully in a hidden state.

<margin>to make it visible</margin>
And they by no means allow this to happen without the "dissolution" of the body and its reduction into its "first matter," which is Quicksilver <term>Argentum vivum; literally "living silver," or Mercury</term>, from which they were made from the beginning. This must be done without any admixture of outside things, since foreign natures do not improve our Stone. For nothing suits a thing except that which is most closely related to it, since it is a medicine of a simple and mineral nature, produced from Mercurial water, in which gold and silver were previously dissolved.

<margin>NB <note>Latin "Nota Bene," meaning "Note well"</note></margin>
<margin>••</margin>
For example: If the power of nature created ice from snow <margin>⁕</margin> in simple frozen water, that ice is dissolved by heat and returns into the primary substance of water. Thus the water is "tainted" <note>In alchemy, "tincture" or "tainting" refers to the way a substance shares its color or properties with another.</note> by the power that was hidden in the ice. If, however, the ice is not resolved into water by heat, it does not join with the water in which it lies, nor does it "taint" that water with its power, which was previously solidified within it by the nature of its species.

<margin>•••</margin>
In the same way, if you do not resolve the body into Mercury by means of Mercury, you cannot have the hidden power from it—that is, the "digested" and "boiled down" sulfur created by the work of nature in the mine. Thus the Stone is one, a single medicine, which according to the philosophers is called **Rebis** <margin>☞</margin>, that is, from a "double thing" <note>original: "ex bina re"</note>, namely from a body and a spirit, either white or red. In this matter, many fools have erred, explaining it in various ways:

->**The Rebis is the most correct rule in the aforementioned figures.**<-

<margin>•</margin>
That is to say, two things—and these two things are one thing—which is to say, water joined to the body, by which the body is dissolved into "spirit," which is to say, into Mineral water.

...from which it was made from the beginning: and so from body and spirit, one Mineral water is made, which is called the elixir, that is, the ferment <note>original: "fermentum"</note>, because then the water and spirit are one thing, from which the tincture <note>original: "tinctura," a substance capable of changing the color and nature of metals</note> and the medicine for purging <note>the process of "healing" or purifying base metals into gold</note> all bodies is made—a thing which seems impossible to many fools. And thus I have explained to you what the *Rebis* <term>literally "the double thing"—a symbol for the union of opposites, like spirit and body or male and female</term> is, and the elixir, and the medicine, and from where their names come, and what the water of the philosophers is, from...

...by which the living elements are made afterwards, and conversely they become one. Verses:
> Four are made from one, and four become one.
> These are the secrets; thus you shall rejoice without end <note>original: "sine meta," meaning without limit or boundary</note>.

<margin>Note well.</margin>
And so, according to the philosophers <note>in this context, "philosophers" refers to the ancient and medieval masters of alchemy</note>, we have the nature of sulfur and Mercury <term>Sulfur and Mercury: the two fundamental principles of medieval alchemy; sulfur represents the active, "fiery" soul, while mercury represents the passive, "metallic" spirit</term> upon the earth, from which gold and silver are made under the earth. From these sayings it is seen how art imitates nature, and by no other way.

### How White and Red Sulfur Exist in the Moon and the Sun. Cap. XII.

<margin>cc</margin>
**S**ince it has been said that the red sulfur of the philosophers exists in the Sun <note>the alchemical symbol for Gold</note> through greater digestion; and white sulfur in the Moon <note>the alchemical symbol for Silver</note> through lesser digestion. * Whence the philosopher says: Citrination <term>citrinatio: the "yellowing" phase of the alchemical work, indicating the transition from the white stage to the final red stage</term> is nothing other than complete digestion. For heat acting upon moisture first generates blackness <note>the stage known as "nigredo"</note>, and acting upon dryness generates whiteness <note>the stage known as "albedo"</note>; the fire then transcends this whiteness, and by acting upon it, causes the purest yellowing. And all these things can be observed in the calcination <note>the process of heating a substance to high temperatures to reduce it to a powder or "calx"</note> of lead. 

The philosopher also says that each of the perfect bodies now actually contains its own good sulfur with Mercury—namely, golden gold and silvery silver. Therefore, white sulfur becomes golden through citrination, because red sulfur—that is, the substance of fire—is present there, which has digested this white substance more thoroughly; and thus both white and red sulfur exist in both parts in the Sun. For this reason, fire is its highest perfection, as it is with things generated in fire. <margin>of its</margin> And therefore nature friendly rejoices in its own fiery nature. Whence it is asked: Can any foreign substances cause this within the bodies of metals? <margin>B.</margin> Since art is nothing other than the cooking and digestion of that nature, with nature acting as the mediator through simple labor. For example: In the morning when I have risen and I see my urine is white, I judge that I have slept too little; I therefore return to sleep, and after taking sleep, the urine becomes yellow, and

<sig>G ij</sig>
<meta>catchword: this (Latin: hoc)</meta>

<summary>This page explains the alchemical theory that gold and silver are formed by different degrees of 'digestion' of sulfur and mercury. It describes the color transitions from black to white to yellow, comparing the alchemical process to the natural heat and digestion found in the human body.</summary>

<page-num>236</page-num>
<header>THE CORRECTORIUM</header>
<meta>Handwritten number 138 appears next to the printed page number 236. Catchword 'quibus' (to which) at bottom right.</meta>

<margin>Manifestly clear. <note>original Greek: "Ευδηλα"</note></margin>
This happens in no other way except through the digestion <term>digestio: an alchemical process where substance is matured through gentle, continuous heat, mimicking the stomach or the earth</term> of heat, which exists naturally within me. In this way, follow nature through art, similarly in regards to cooking, digesting, ripening, and refining, since nature already contains within itself the actual natural fire by which it is ripened; other things do not have this fire, and they cannot provide it. In the Moon <note>the alchemical name for Silver</note>, however, there is nothing but simple white sulfur, which is not as fully digested as the red sulfur <note>found in Gold</note>, since it has not been stripped of its blackness through the action of the heat it naturally contains. Rather, it is a species of fire that is veiled and hidden, acting both in art and in nature, and vice versa. And therefore it is not impossible that art—with nature acting as a mediator—should digest and perfect this further, since nature naturally desires to reach perfection; but on its own, nature cannot succeed unless it is aided by art and operation. Nevertheless, I believe these labors do not reach the "stiff-necked" man <note>original: "homini durae cervicis," a biblical idiom for those who are stubborn or refuse to learn</note>. This is because good workers are rarely found. And for this reason, true Silver and Gold are not made unless they are so digested and cooked, or prepared like a medicine <note>referring to the Elixir or Philosopher's Stone</note>, with which the "worse" <note>base metal</note> is tinged and improved into something better. For the intention of all philosophers is to perfect the worse with the better. Fools understand this in the opposite way, because they strive to perfect the better with the worse; they seek a thing in a substance where it never existed—namely, they look for Gold and Silver in dung and in combustible things. They do this through sophistry <note>deceptive or false methods</note> so that they might appear truthful to men, as was said before.

<margin>These things are also useful. <note>original Greek: "ωφελιμα και ταυτα"</note></margin>
### That it is not useful to seek this sulfur in other "sick" bodies, since it is not in them. Chapter XIV.

<margin>namely in gold and silver <note>original: "videl. in auro et in argento"</note></margin>
**I**t may indeed be asked, and not without reason, whether this sulfur—namely the white and the red—can be drawn out from other "sick" <note>the alchemists referred to base metals like lead or tin as "sick" because they were "imperfect" versions of gold</note> bodies to tinge Mercury. I say that it cannot. This is because it was said previously that in those base metals, there is no matter of greater temperance or less dross <note>impurities</note> than there is in these two bodies <note>Gold and Silver</note>.

<summary>Richard explains that the "natural fire" required for the maturation of metals is only found within Gold and Silver. He warns that trying to extract the alchemical sulfur from base metals or "dung" is a fool's errand, as art must work with nature's best materials to achieve perfection.</summary>
<keywords>digestion, natural heat, sulfur, Silver, Gold, base metals, alchemy vs. nature, sophistry, Chapter XIV</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Richardus Anglicus’s argument that base minerals and "lesser" minerals cannot achieve transmutation. <page-num>237</page-num>
<header>RICHARD THE ENGLISHMAN</header>

<margin>Stinking and combustible sulfur. <note>original: "Sulphur foetidum et adustibile"</note></margin>
...to which belong dyeing rays, which you might use for dyeing <term>dyeing: or 'tincture' (Latin: tingendum); the alchemical process of imbuing a base metal with the color and properties of a noble metal</term>. But you will not dye with other things, since it was said before that they contain in themselves a stinking and combustible sulfur, and not one of a virtual nature <note>i.e., lacking the inherent "power" or "virtue" required for alchemy</note>, as exists in those [gold and silver]. Since all art is powerless unless it has nature beforehand to follow: You could, however, purge imperfect metals with lesser minerals, and once they were purged, they would not have a golden or silver nature in themselves. This is because the golden decoction, or digestion <term>digestion: a process of refining a substance through gentle, steady heat to reach a state of maturity</term>, was not in them as it was in those two bodies [gold and silver], nor was their sulfur as mature; therefore, these immature metals must be assisted with the mature, so that they may be matured. Thus, they do not dye, but are themselves dyed, because the tincture of gold or silver has a proportional nature in them, for they drew their origin from Mercury along with them. From these things it is clearly evident that lesser minerals cannot dye. For if imperfect metallic bodies, which agree with Gold and Silver on the part of Mercury, cannot dye, nor infuse a golden or silver nature, how then could those things do so which do not agree in any nature with them? And therefore one must not dye except with those things in which the power of dyeing exists. Dye, therefore, with Gold and Silver, because gold gives a golden, and silver a silver, color and nature. For this reason you should despise all other things, since there is no fruit in them, but only the loss of resources, as well as of time and labor.

<margin>This chapter is also clear. <note>original Greek: "σαφές και τούτο το κεφάλ[αιον]"</note></margin>

**...[perfec]tion, it strengthens every infirmity of the body, expels sickness, wards off poison from the heart, moistens the arteries, dissolves dark impurities from the lungs, heals wounds, purifies the blood, breaks stones <note>Likely referring to kidney or bladder stones, a common ailment of the era</note>, purges the contents of the vital organs <note>original: "spiritualibus"; in medieval medicine, the 'spiritual' organs were those responsible for the breath and vital spirits, namely the heart and lungs</note>; if the head is congested with rheum, it purges the flow, and strengthens the stomach in its natural heat.** And if the sickness has lasted one month, it heals it in one day, or even one hour. If, however, the sickness has lasted one year, it heals it in eight days. If the disease is ancient and has persisted for a long time, incurable by other medicines, it heals it in half a month. O happy science, and happy the one who knows it! Wherefore, not without merit, this medicine is to be sought above all other medicines of the physicians; for whoever possesses it holds an incomparable treasure, and was born under a healthy constellation. Such a person is rich in this world, with riches more infinite than the kings and princes of this age. Who would not love such things, which preserve the user in health and long life, beyond all the medicines of the physicians? For it enriches the possessor while impoverishing no one else.

<margin>
See now, O follower of Galen. <note>original: "Vide jam Galenice." This is a challenge to traditional physicians who followed the ancient Greek doctor Galen, suggesting alchemy surpasses traditional herbal medicine.</note>

**These goods are just before God and men, not acquired through usury, or through the fraud and deception of lies, nor through commerce designed to deceive the many,** as is the case in the acquisition of all other goods. For this art is a special gift of God, as it is said: **"Because you shall eat the labors of your hands, blessed are you, and it shall be well with you."** <note>A quote from Psalm 128:2, emphasizing that the alchemist's wealth comes from their own physical and intellectual labor.</note> But on the contrary, the blessed Augustine says to all those other Alchemists who delude men with sophisticated tricks: **"You are deceivers, and weighed down by the sentence of excommunication, since your works and your elements are false and evil."** This excludes or saves those craftsmen who imitate nature, although few are able to know the truth of this art to the highest degree, as was said above regarding the purpose and virtue of this medicine. **Thus the tricksters <note>original: "truffatores"</note>, seeking gold and silver in dung and filth in their own books,** which they themselves have composed,

<summary>Richardus describes the miraculous healing speed and power of the Alchemical Elixir, claiming it surpasses traditional Galenic medicine. ### That only two particular methods are true in this art, the first of which is in Mercury. Chapter XV.

<margin>A clear explanation of the contents of this chapter.</margin> <note>original Greek: "σαφῆ τῶν τὰ τούτου τοῦ κεφαλαίου"</note>
**F**irst, I declare universally to all observers to whom these present writings shall come, that in the entire progression of this art, there are but two "particulars" <term>particularia: specific, limited alchemical operations or transmutations, as opposed to the universal "Great Work" that produces the Elixir</term>, which are completed in a particular manner according to the philosophers and nature. Although deceivers perform infinite sophistications, whitenings <term>dealbationes: the alchemical process of turning the matter white (albedo)</term>, and reddenings <term>rubificationes: the process of turning the matter red (rubedo)</term>, by which the faithful are deluded. The first particular method, as much in the white stage,

<summary>Richard the Englishman begins a new chapter defining the only two legitimate "particular" alchemical methods, warning that all other complicated procedures are merely the tricks of "deceivers." He identifies the first true method as being rooted in Mercury.</summary>

<page-num>242</page-num>
<header>THE CORRECTORIUM</header>

<margin>Obscure words concerning a clear matter.</margin>
<margin>Through the mixing of the Sun and Moon.</margin>
as in the red, is mature because it is matured through art. For this reason, other metals thus coagulated and stained by sulfur <term>sulphur: in this context, the principle of impurity and combustion that makes base metals "imperfect"</term> cannot be made into the Sun <note>original: "Sol" - Gold</note> and Moon <note>original: "Luna" - Silver</note> in a "particular" <term>particulariter: referring to the limited alchemical method rather than the universal elixir</term> way. The first reason is this: if imperfect metallic bodies were transformed and mixed with the Sun and Moon, their Mercury would still possess that previously mentioned bad sulfur <gloss>not</gloss>, and even if they were purged, they could not be purged to such an extent <gloss>so that</gloss> they could be reduced into Mercury as they were before such superfluity, nor could a perfect body be dissolved in it. And 
<margin>These words "nor even could a perfect body," etc., clearly reveal the author’s mind.</margin>
since they could not be dissolved in it, with the natures of both sides being locked away <note>original: "seratis" - literally "bolted" or "locked," meaning the inner essence of the metal is inaccessible</note>, each would be separated from the other during the testing <note>examinatione: the process of assaying or testing the purity of metal in a furnace</note>. From this, it would not possess a perfect nature hidden within itself, through which their proper solutions could be perfected by means of the art, so that other perfect bodies might assist with their own nature, which is naturally perfect.

The second reason is that if they were joined to perfect bodies without being dissolved, they could not become Gold and Silver, since naturally, through coagulation on both sides, their natures are locked. Since there is no medium <gloss>Mercury</gloss> opening those natures, and since no one power can be joined to another by a natural conjunction, nor be sent into it so that they return into the Mercury from which they both drew their origin: therefore, they are separated from one another by the harshness of the fire, namely through the burning of the imperfect nature and the resistance of the perfect nature, as is clearly seen.

<margin>Note the natures (of the metals).</margin>
But when you wish to join them <gloss>then</gloss>, you must create a medium through Mercury, which dissolves and opens the natures, so that one may simply pass into the other and send a perfect power into the imperfect one, so that it may perfect it along with itself. And these are the labors of the "particular" path by which Gold and Silver can be made, but not universally.

<margin>The Mercury of bodies, which is in individual bodies.</margin>
<margin>* Crude Mercury, that is, crude quicksilver.</margin>
**Note.** Crude Mercury dissolves bodies and reduces them into the primary matter, but the Mercury of bodies cannot do this. For it is because of the crudity of its sulfur, which the white earth possessed in the beginning, from which it was made with clear water from the start, for that crude state always...
